Refraction in the Surveys

How 21 triangulation surveys (1739 to 1908) and the geodesy manuals of the period dealt with atmospheric refraction, from the printed records. 387 passages, each with its page.
Horizontal angles. None of the 21 surveys records a refraction correction to its horizontal angles: 18 state that none was applied or apply none in their reductions, and 3 do not say. They controlled it by when and how they observed: steady-air hours, night lights and heliotropes, high stations, many repetitions on different days, and rejection of observations taken in unsteady air. The spherical excess comes from these horizontal angles.
Vertical angles. The surveys that print a height method correct their zenith distances for refraction, in most cases with a coefficient measured from reciprocal observations on the same lines. Single printed values run from -0.0035 to 0.4304 of the arc (median 0.078, 78 values); the largest come from short, low lines. Heights enter the triangles only through the reduction of the lengths to sea level.

1. Two kinds of refraction

Air density falls with height, so a sight line bends slightly. Almost all of that bending lies in the vertical plane. It lifts the apparent elevation of a target and changes a measured zenith distance. The surveys measured it and corrected for it in their heights. This is how they measured it on each line:

Without refraction: zA + zB = 180° + C
With refraction, each target looks higher by r: zA + zB = 180° + C − 2r
So the refraction on that line: r = (180° + C − zA − zB) ÷ 2, and per arc = r ÷ C

Worked out here from the figure. Example: Maclear's mean per arc, 0.0712, on a line with C = 10′ (600″) gives r = 0.0712 × 600″ ≈ 42.7″ at each end. The two zenith distances need not be read at the same moment; Clarke (1858) and Schott (1900) note that theirs were not.

A sideways bend (lateral refraction) needs a sideways change in air density. That happens near heated ground, slopes, water and cliffs along the line. It changes a horizontal angle, and no general rule predicts it, so the surveys handled it by method (see Working Around Refraction).

The size they report:

  • Maclear at the Cape: probable error of a measured angle 0.05″ with first-rate definition, 0.3″ with bad definition and lateral refraction.
  • Gill in South Africa (reported by Furber): combining morning and afternoon observations cut the probable error of an angle from 0.49″ to 0.33″.
  • Clarke (1880): refraction affects horizontal angles "to a very small extent"; where the local ground causes it, it is small "but persistent". It shows only in the triangle closures.

For scale (worked out here): 1″ of spherical excess is about 197 km² of triangle area. A height error from a wrong refraction coefficient changes a side only through the sea-level reduction, by L·Δh/R. A 3 m height error on a 30 km side changes it by 30,000 × 3 / 6,371,000 ≈ 0.014 m.

no-0.0035 to 0.17One series of ten for each zenith distance, unless something made the observation doubtful. In fine weather Delambre preferred to repeat the angles of position; in bad weather, he says, the variations of terrestrial refraction would have made the zenith distances more uncertain. Distances above 91 or below 89 degrees should be observed at the same time as the angle, which was hardly possible, so they were taken just before or after, or at several hours of the day.

3. The vertical coefficient, survey by survey

Every single value printed in the sources, put on one scale: the refraction at one station divided by the arc at the earth's centre between the two stations (the "fraction of the contained arc" of Roy and Lambton, Delambre's n, Schott's and Maclear's m, Clarke's k). Lines show printed ranges. Hover a point for its line and page. The shaded band is the average range Clarke (1880) gives. The modern coefficient k (earth radius ÷ ray radius) is twice this value.

4. What the manuals recommended

Clarke (1880) states that refraction acts almost wholly in the vertical plane; lateral refraction affects horizontal angles only to a very small extent, but can be persistent where the local ground shape causes it, and he lists it with centring and signal phase as constant errors that show up in triangle closures. For heights he defines the coefficient k as the ratio of the refraction to the arc v at the centre of the earth, gives a mean of 0.0771 from 144 Ordnance Survey values (0.0750 over land, 0.0809 over sea), Massachusetts values 0.0784 and 0.0697, and Indian plains values from -0.09 to +1.21. He gives the daily cycle (greatest at daybreak, near constant from 10 A.M. to 4 P.M.) and recommends simultaneous reciprocal zenith distances, which eliminate k. Airy (1848) rejects a refraction correction to the geodetic lines as a refinement, notes that azimuths from low celestial bodies are little affected by refraction, and records Everest's preference for night signals because of the night refraction. Bessel (1841, AN 438) does not treat refraction.

Clarke 1880: observe reciprocal zenith distances simultaneously so the refraction can be taken equal at both ends.
“For assuming the refraction to be the same at both stations (and for this end the observations should be simultaneous)”
Clarke (1880), pp. 282-283
Clarke, A. R. (1880). Geodesy. Oxford: Clarendon Press.
Clarke 1880: with mutual observations, either eliminate k with formula (3), or compute two results from a mean k and weight them.
“When the observations at A and B are mutual, we may either eliminate the coefficient of refraction by using the formula (3), or we may get two different results from a mean assumed value of k, and then combine these results by assigning them weights deduced from the consistency of the observations from which they are separately derived”
Clarke (1880), p. 284
Clarke, A. R. (1880). Geodesy. Oxford: Clarendon Press.
Clarke 1880: use separate k for rays over sea (0.0809) and over land (0.0750).
“But it is necessary to discriminate between rays which cross the sea and those which do not.”
Clarke (1880), p. 283
Clarke, A. R. (1880). Geodesy. Oxford: Clarendon Press.
Clarke 1880: height differences from zenith distances that do not close round a triangle are adjusted by least squares with weights.
“When at each of three stations we have the observed zenith distances of the other two, the deduced differences of height will exhibit a discrepancy.”
Clarke (1880), p. 284
Clarke, A. R. (1880). Geodesy. Oxford: Clarendon Press.
Clarke 1880: for the theory of terrestrial refraction he refers to Bauernfeind's papers in the Astronomische Nachrichten for 1866.
“For the theory of this refraction we may refer to some able papers by Dr. Bauernfeind in the Astronomische Nachrichten for 1866.”
Clarke (1880), p. 280
Clarke, A. R. (1880). Geodesy. Oxford: Clarendon Press.
Airy 1848: for azimuth, a celestial body near the horizon is used because the angle is little affected by refraction; the Polaris method with a repeating circle needs precise knowledge of latitude and refraction.
“The former method requires extremely good adjustment of the theodolite; the latter, a very precise knowledge of the latitude and the refraction.”
Airy (1848), p. 199 · text layer
Airy, G. B. (1848). Figure of the Earth. In Encyclopaedia of astronomy (Encyclopaedia Metropolitana, vol. 5). London: J. J. Griffin.

5. Survey by survey

Under each quote is the printed passage, cropped from the page scan; click it for full size. "Text layer" marks a quote checked against the scan's text layer only.

Cassini de Thury and Lacaille, verification of the Paris meridian and the arc of the parallel near 43.5 N

France · 1739-1744

Horizontal angles were reduced to the horizon from zenith distances that needed only 3 or 4 minutes of accuracy; no lateral refraction correction is stated. Where a zenith distance was not observed, it was computed from the observed reciprocal one, and the authors say refraction makes that computation inexact; their rule is to take 2 or 3 minutes off the itinerary arc when it exceeds 20 minutes. A footnote gives four reciprocal pairs, observed with an instrument checked by reversal, that show 43 seconds to 4 minutes 57 seconds of total refraction on arcs of 9 to 35.5 minutes. The authors warn that the printed zenith distances were not all observed precisely enough to give heights of mountains above the sea. Delambre (1810) later states that the 1744 heights were computed without terrestrial refraction and so came out too large.

Horizontal angles corrected for refraction: not stated

Angles were reduced to the horizon with zenith distances good to 3 or 4 minutes; the reduction was applied only from Bourges to the southern end. No correction for lateral refraction and no rejection of angles for unsteady images is stated in the pages read.

Zenith distances for the reduction to the horizon needed only 3 or 4 minutes of accuracy.
“il n'étoit pas néceffaire de connoitre les diftances des objets au zenith avec une extrême précifion : il fuffifoit de les avoir à 3 ou 4 minutes près.”
In English: “it was not necessary to know the distances of the objects from the zenith with extreme precision: it was enough to have them to within 3 or 4 minutes.”
Cassini de Thury (1744), x (Troisième partie)
Cassini de Thury, C.-F. (1744). La méridienne de l'Observatoire Royal de Paris vérifiée dans toute l'étendue du Royaume par de nouvelles observations. Paris: H.-L. Guérin & J. Guérin.
The reduction to the horizon was used only from Bourges southward, because north of Bourges no reduction reached 2 seconds.
“ayant trouvé qu'aucune des réductions à l'Horizon, calculées depuis Paris jufqu'à la bafe mefurée près de Bourges ne montoit à 2", nous n'avons pas cru qu'il fût néceffaire de calculer nos Triangles d'après ces réductions , non plus que depuis Paris jufqu'à Dunkerke : nous n'y avons eu égard que depuis Bourges jufqu'à l'extrémité méridionale de la Méridienne.”
In English: “having found that none of the reductions to the horizon computed from Paris to the base measured near Bourges reached 2", we did not think it necessary to compute our triangles with these reductions, nor from Paris to Dunkirk: we took them into account only from Bourges to the southern end of the meridian.”
Cassini de Thury (1744), xj (Troisième partie)
Cassini de Thury, C.-F. (1744). La méridienne de l'Observatoire Royal de Paris vérifiée dans toute l'étendue du Royaume par de nouvelles observations. Paris: H.-L. Guérin & J. Guérin.

Vertical angles and heights

Zenith distances were observed at one or both stations. Unobserved reciprocal zenith distances were computed from the observed one with the itinerary arc (1 minute for 951 toises), less 2 or 3 minutes for refraction when the arc exceeded 20 minutes. Four reciprocal pairs measured the total refraction. The authors state the zenith distances were not all precise enough for mountain heights. A sea-horizon dip series was observed at Sainte-Victoire; no refraction treatment is stated with it.

Refraction makes the computed reciprocal zenith distance inexact; the correction is to take 2 or 3 minutes from the itinerary arc if it exceeds 20 minutes, else neglect it.
“Il eft vrai que la réfraction empêche que ce calcul ne foit exact ; mais on peut y avoir égard , en quelque façon (*), en ôtant deux ou trois minutes de l'arc de la diftance itinéraire , s'il excéde 20';car lorfqu'il eft au-deffous , on peut négliger cette correction.”
In English: “It is true that refraction prevents this calculation from being exact; but one can allow for it, in some fashion (*), by taking two or three minutes from the arc of the itinerary distance, if it exceeds 20'; for when it is below, this correction can be neglected.”
Cassini de Thury (1744), x-xj (Troisième partie)
Cassini de Thury, C.-F. (1744). La méridienne de l'Observatoire Royal de Paris vérifiée dans toute l'étendue du Royaume par de nouvelles observations. Paris: H.-L. Guérin & J. Guérin.
Footnote: the observations were made with an instrument checked by reversal, to find the amount of horizontal refraction, which makes long levellings uncertain.
“Pour faire voir fur quoi eft fondée la correction que nous difons ici qu'on peut faire , nous allons rapporter quelques obfervations qui ont été faites avec un Inftrument vérifié exprès par le renverfement,pour connoitre la quantité de la réfraction horifontale, qui rend incertains les grands nivellemens.”
In English: “To show on what the correction we say can be made here is founded, we shall report some observations made with an instrument checked expressly by reversal, to learn the amount of horizontal refraction, which makes long levellings uncertain.”
Cassini de Thury (1744), x (Troisième partie), footnote
Cassini de Thury, C.-F. (1744). La méridienne de l'Observatoire Royal de Paris vérifiée dans toute l'étendue du Royaume par de nouvelles observations. Paris: H.-L. Guérin & J. Guérin.
The zenith distances printed at the end of each station are not all precise enough to give mountain heights above the sea.
“Nous devons avertir auffi que les diftances au zenith , qui font marquées à la fin de chaque ftation , n'ont pas été toutes obfervées avec une précifion fuffifante , pour en conclure la hauteur des Montagnes au-deffus du niveau de la Mer, parce que l'incommodité des lieux n'a fouvent pas permis d'y prendre les précautions néceffaires”
In English: “We must also warn that the zenith distances given at the end of each station were not all observed with sufficient precision to conclude from them the height of the mountains above sea level, because the inconvenience of the places often did not allow the necessary precautions”
Cassini de Thury (1744), xj (Troisième partie)
Cassini de Thury, C.-F. (1744). La méridienne de l'Observatoire Royal de Paris vérifiée dans toute l'étendue du Royaume par de nouvelles observations. Paris: H.-L. Guérin & J. Guérin.
Sea-horizon dip observed from the terrace of the hermitage of Sainte-Victoire, December 1739 to January 1740, with the quadrant checked by reversal.
“Obfervations de l'abbaiffement apparent de l'horizon de la Mer.”
In English: “Observations of the apparent dip of the sea horizon.”
Cassini de Thury (1744), 107 (Première partie) · text layer
Cassini de Thury, C.-F. (1744). La méridienne de l'Observatoire Royal de Paris vérifiée dans toute l'étendue du Royaume par de nouvelles observations. Paris: H.-L. Guérin & J. Guérin.
Delambre (1810), reviewing the 1744 work: terrestrial refraction was neglected in the calculation, so all heights came out too large; recomputed with mean refraction 0.08 they came much closer.
“Mais , dans le calcul , on négligeoit la réfraction terrestre : ainsi l'on trouvoit toutes les hauteurs trop grandes . C'est ce qu'on peut aussi remarquer dans la méridienne de 1718 . En refaisant tous les calculs avec la réfraction moyenne 0.08 , j'ai trouvé des nombres beaucoup plus rapprochés”
In English: “But in the calculation terrestrial refraction was neglected: so all the heights were found too large. This can also be seen in the meridian of 1718. Redoing all the calculations with the mean refraction 0.08, I found numbers much closer”
Méchain & Delambre (1810), T3 p. 145
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1810). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 3). Paris: Baudouin.

fixed allowance of 2′ to 3′ for both lines of sight together on arcs above 20′, not a fraction of the arc; at the 20′ threshold it is 120″ to 180″/1200″/2 = 0.05 to 0.075 per station, less on longer arcs
Cassini de Thury (1744), x-xj (Troisième partie)

Observing rules

Zenith distances were often omitted in steeples (timber, no openings, makeshift scaffolds) or in high wind, and were replaced by the equivalent calculation.
“Cela eft fur-tout très-difficile à exécuter dans les clochers , qui font ordinairement trop embarraffés de charpente , fans ouvertures , & dans lefquels on eft obligé de s'échaffauder, comme on peut, avec des planches , telles qu'elles fe trouvent fur les lieux. Quelquefois aufti le grand vent ou quelque autre inconvénient nous ont obligé de les omettre dans les endroits libres : mais nous y avons fuppléé par un calcul équivalent.”
In English: “This is above all very difficult in steeples, which are usually too cluttered with timber, without openings, and in which one must build a scaffold as one can with planks found on the spot. Sometimes also strong wind or some other problem made us omit them in open places: but we made up for it by an equivalent calculation.”
Cassini de Thury (1744), x (Troisième partie)
Cassini de Thury, C.-F. (1744). La méridienne de l'Observatoire Royal de Paris vérifiée dans toute l'étendue du Royaume par de nouvelles observations. Paris: H.-L. Guérin & J. Guérin.
Each object's zenith distance was observed from at least one of the two stations when it could not be observed reciprocally.
“Nous avons donc eu grand foin d'obferver la diftance de chaque Objet au zenith d'une des deux ftations , lorfqu'elle n'a pu l'être réciproquement.”
In English: “We therefore took great care to observe the zenith distance of each object from one of the two stations, when it could not be observed reciprocally.”
Cassini de Thury (1744), xj (Troisième partie)
Cassini de Thury, C.-F. (1744). La méridienne de l'Observatoire Royal de Paris vérifiée dans toute l'étendue du Royaume par de nouvelles observations. Paris: H.-L. Guérin & J. Guérin.

Recommended practice

Explicit rule: subtract 2 or 3 minutes from the itinerary arc for refraction when it exceeds 20 minutes; neglect the correction below that.
“en ôtant deux ou trois minutes de l'arc de la diftance itinéraire , s'il excéde 20';car lorfqu'il eft au-deffous , on peut négliger cette correction.”
In English: “by taking two or three minutes from the arc of the itinerary distance, if it exceeds 20'; for when it is below, this correction can be neglected.”
Cassini de Thury (1744), x-xj (Troisième partie)
Cassini de Thury, C.-F. (1744). La méridienne de l'Observatoire Royal de Paris vérifiée dans toute l'étendue du Royaume par de nouvelles observations. Paris: H.-L. Guérin & J. Guérin.
Lacaille's note in the 1740 registers, as extracted by Delambre (1810): terrestrial refraction is not so irregular that it cannot be allowed for, and it is too large to be neglected in computing heights; he proposes finding refraction from observation and building a table.
“1° que cette réfraction n'est pas si irrégulière qu'on ne puisse trouver le moyen d'y avoir égard d'une manière satisfaisante ; 2° que la réfraction terrestre est trop grande pour être négligée dans le calcul des hauteurs des objets au-dessus de l'horizon”
In English: “1st that this refraction is not so irregular that one cannot find a way to allow for it satisfactorily; 2nd that terrestrial refraction is too large to be neglected in computing the heights of objects above the horizon”
Méchain & Delambre (1810), T3 p. 534
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1810). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 3). Paris: Baudouin.
Lacaille excepts the dip of the sea horizon, which he calls the most subject to irregular variations.
“Quand je dis que la réfraction horizontale des objets terrestres n'est pas si irrégulière qu'on pense, j'en excepte celle de l'abaissement de l'horizon de la mer.”
In English: “When I say that the horizontal refraction of terrestrial objects is not as irregular as people think, I except that of the dip of the sea horizon.”
Méchain & Delambre (1810), T3 p. 534
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1810). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 3). Paris: Baudouin.

Not found in the pages read

  • No correction of horizontal angles for lateral refraction in the pages read (Troisième partie §IV, pdf 314-317).
  • No adopted single refraction coefficient for terrestrial lines; only the four footnote pairs and the 2 to 3 minute rule.
  • No statement that refraction enters the triangle closures or the spherical excess.
  • No night observation of terrestrial angles with lamps; the 'feux' (pdf 112, 119) are longitude fire signals.
  • No stated hours of observation aimed at refraction for terrestrial angles.
  • Refraction references at pdf 36, 121 and 426 (section 'De la Réfraction', Bouguer's hypothesis) concern astronomical refraction for the zenith sector and solar heights, not terrestrial lines.
Pages read: Cassini de Thury (1744): pdf pp. 36, 105, 112, 119-122, 314-317, 422, 426 (text layer; page images 316-317); Méchain & Delambre (1810): pdf pp. 170, 558-563 (later review of the 1739-1740 registers and of Lacaille's note on horizontal refraction)

Delambre and Méchain, meridian arc Dunkirk to Barcelona (Base du système métrique)

France · 1792-1798

Horizontal angles were not corrected for refraction. Delambre says refraction raises the signals unequally, so the three angles of a triangle lie in different planes, and the reduction to the horizon handles this; he was at times inclined to believe in a lateral refraction but gives only two remedies, not infallible: waiting for favourable moments and repeating at other hours and conditions. Heights came from reciprocal zenith distances with the refraction taken as a fixed fraction n of the angle C between the verticals; a table of 189 reciprocal pairs gives n, with a mean of 0.08388 for all and 0.07876 for 159 pairs after rejecting fog and decidedly rainy weather, and 0.0783 from 17 sea-horizon observations. The height tables use n = 0.08, said to vary from 0.07 to 0.09 and sometimes 0.10. Fog gave n of 0.14 to 0.17, sight lines grazing heath gave excessive undulations, and evening observations gave stronger refraction.

Horizontal angles corrected for refraction: no

No lateral refraction correction is applied. Unequal vertical refraction is handled by the reduction to the horizon. Undulations are noted series by series, some series are rejected, and Delambre advises waiting for favourable moments and repeating at different hours.

Possible lateral refraction: reverberatory lamps would be equally subject to it; the two remedies are to wait for favourable moments and to repeat observations at different hours and circumstances.
“S'il existe une réfraction latérale , comme j'ai été par fois tenté de le croire , les réverbères y seroient également sujets , et l'on n'a pour se garantir de cette illusion que deux moyens qui ne sont pas infaillibles , l'un est d'attendre les instans favorables ; l'autre , de répéter les observations à des heures et dans des circonstances différentes.”
In English: “If a lateral refraction exists, as I have at times been tempted to believe, reverberatory lamps would be equally subject to it, and there are only two means, not infallible, to guard against this illusion: one is to wait for favourable moments; the other, to repeat the observations at different hours and in different circumstances.”
Méchain & Delambre (1806), T1 Discours préliminaire p. 106
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1806). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 1). Paris: Baudouin.
The lamp lit at Montmartre in August 1792 showed very noticeable undulations that made the apparent place oscillate around the true one; Delambre calls this inherent in signals of every kind and says he often saw it with day signals.
“le réverbère allumé à Montmartre le 11 et le 15 août 1792 étoit sujet à des ondulations très-sensibles , qui faisoient osciller le lieu apparent autour du véritable . C'est un inconvénient au reste qui paroît inhérent aux signaux de toute espèce , je l'ai éprouvé bien souvent sur les signaux de jour”
In English: “the lamp lit at Montmartre on 11 and 15 August 1792 was subject to very noticeable undulations, which made the apparent place oscillate around the true one. This drawback seems inherent in signals of every kind; I experienced it very often with day signals”
Méchain & Delambre (1806), T1 Discours préliminaire p. 106
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1806). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 1). Paris: Baudouin.
Refraction raises terrestrial objects unequally, so the three angles of each triangle were observed in different planes and must be brought to one surface (reduction to the horizon).
“La réfraction élève inégalement les objets terrestres ; il y a toujours une différence sensible entre la hauteur du centre du cercle et le point extérieur qui a servi de mire dans les stations circonvoisines ; il en résulte que les trois angles qui doivent former chacun des triangles, ont toujours été observés dans des plans différens”
In English: “Refraction raises terrestrial objects unequally; there is always a noticeable difference between the height of the centre of the circle and the outer point used as target from the surrounding stations; hence the three angles that form each triangle were always observed in different planes”
Méchain & Delambre (1806), T1 Discours préliminaire p. 137
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1806). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 1). Paris: Baudouin.
Station Méri (3-5 vendémiaire): excessive undulations moved the spire tip to one side or the other and held it there for nearly a minute.
“On voit bien les deux clochers , qui sont très-aigus ; mais ondulations excessives . Ces ondulations n'étoient pas toujours très-rapides ; elles portoient quelquefois la pointe du clocher d'un côté ou d'un autre , et l'y retenoient près d'une minute de temps.”
In English: “The two steeples, which are very sharp, are well seen; but excessive undulations. These undulations were not always very rapid; they sometimes carried the tip of the steeple to one side or the other and held it there nearly a minute of time.”
Méchain & Delambre (1806), T1 p. 201 · text layer
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1806). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 1). Paris: Baudouin.
Example of a rejected series at Fiefs (angle with Sauti): strong wind, Sauti faint and curved, undulations at Mesnil.
“Grand vent ; Sauti foible et courbe ; ondulations au Mesnil ; fils en + série à rejeter.”
In English: “Strong wind; Sauti faint and curved; undulations at Mesnil; wires in +; series to be rejected.”
Méchain & Delambre (1806), T1 p. 33
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1806). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 1). Paris: Baudouin.
Rieupeyroux area (La Bastide/Aubassin): if increasing evening refraction raised each signal by one minute, the reduced angle would fall by 2.8 seconds.
“Le soir approchoit. Supposons que la réfraction terrestre augmentant , ait élevé les signaux chacun d'une minute , l'angle réduit à l'horizon diminuera de 2"8”
In English: “Evening was approaching. Suppose that the increasing terrestrial refraction raised the signals one minute each; the angle reduced to the horizon will decrease by 2"8”
Méchain & Delambre (1806), T1 p. 272 · text layer
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1806). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 1). Paris: Baudouin.

Vertical angles and heights

Reciprocal zenith distances, reduced to the signal tops and for the thread half-thickness, give level differences and the refraction factor n with r = nC, assumed equal at both ends. Table of n for 189 pairs; heights computed with n = 0.08 C where only one distance was observed; Montjouy sea-horizon heights checked against a direct levelling. Refraction varied with fog, season, time of day and ground grazing.

Formula: observed distances are lessened by terrestrial refraction, assumed proportional to the angle between the normals and equal for the two observations.
“Ces dernières sont diminuées de la réfraction terrestre , qui est supposée proportionnelle à l'angle des deux normales ou à l'angle M, et qui par conséquent est égale pour les deux observations. Soit r cette réfraction”
In English: “These are diminished by terrestrial refraction, which is supposed proportional to the angle between the two normals or to the angle M, and which is therefore equal for the two observations. Let r be this refraction”
Méchain & Delambre (1807), T2 p. 739
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1807). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 2). Paris: Baudouin.
The coefficient n assumed 0.08 can vary from 0.07 to 0.09 and sometimes 0.10, giving one or two toises error at 18000 toises.
“Le coefficient n, que nous avons supposé 0.08, pouvant varier de 0.07 à 0.09, et même quelquefois à 0.10, on voit que notre coefficient 0.42 = 0.50 − 0.08 peut varier de 42 à 40, 41 et 43”
In English: “The coefficient n, which we have supposed 0.08, being able to vary from 0.07 to 0.09, and even sometimes to 0.10, one sees that our coefficient 0.42 = 0.50 - 0.08 can vary from 42 to 40, 41 and 43”
Méchain & Delambre (1807), T2 p. 768
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1807). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 2). Paris: Baudouin.
Heights determined from one point only may be doubtful because refraction may have differed from the mean.
“celles qui n'ont été déterminées que d'un seul point peuvent être douteuses , non seulement parce que la réfraction peut avoir été assez différente de la moyenne”
In English: “those determined from a single point may be doubtful, not only because the refraction may have been rather different from the mean”
Méchain & Delambre (1807), T2 p. 768
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1807). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 2). Paris: Baudouin.
Delambre states that it appears to be fog above all that affects refraction, giving n of 0.14 to 0.17. At Boiscommun he repeated the observations many times and could see no effect of barometer and hygrometer changes.
“Il paroît que c'est le brouillard qui influe surtout sur les réfractions , et qui donne pour n des valeurs de 0.14 à 0.17.”
In English: “It appears that it is fog above all that affects refractions, and that gives for n values of 0.14 to 0.17.”
Méchain & Delambre (1807), T2 p. 775
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1807). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 2). Paris: Baudouin.
Ennordre to Méri: sight line grazing a heath-covered plain, excessive undulations.
“D'Ennordre à Méri le rayon visuel rasoit une plaine couverte de bruyères , et les ondulations étoient excessives .”
In English: “From Ennordre to Méri the line of sight grazed a plain covered with heather, and the undulations were excessive.”
Méchain & Delambre (1807), T2 p. 775
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1807). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 2). Paris: Baudouin.
From no. 135 on, Méchain's observations: several series per distance, hardly any after October, mostly on mountains where the sight line did not graze the ground.
“A commencer de 135 toutes les observations sont de M. Méchain qui a toujours fait plusieurs séries pour la même distance , et qui , à l'exception de quelques stations de Nore au Vernet , n'a guère observé passé le mois d'octobre , et presque toujours sur des montagnes où le rayon visuel ne rasoit pas le sol.”
In English: “From 135 on, all observations are by M. Méchain, who always made several series for the same distance, and who, except for some stations from Nore to Le Vernet, hardly observed after October, and almost always on mountains where the line of sight did not graze the ground.”
Méchain & Delambre (1807), T2 p. 776
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1807). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 2). Paris: Baudouin.
Negative n (no. 12, Fiefs and Bonnières, July, very hot stormy weather at Fiefs, Bonnières steeple poorly seen): removing 9" from the observed distance would give n = 0.0020; Delambre states that refraction was nearly nil at the moment of the two observations. He adds that three observations, this one included, were made between ten and eleven in the morning.
“Le 12e est négatif . Les deux observations sont du mois de juillet . A Fiefs le temps étoit très-chaud et orageux”
In English: “The 12th is negative. The two observations are from July. At Fiefs the weather was very hot and stormy”
Méchain & Delambre (1807), T2 p. 773
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1807). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 2). Paris: Baudouin.
Sauti, in the middle of a wood with steeples seen across woods, indicates strong refraction in all combinations.
“Sauti , dans toutes les combinaisons , indique une forte réfraction . Il est au milieu d'un bois , et tous ces clochers se voient au-delà de bois d'une étendue assez considérable.”
In English: “Sauti, in all combinations, indicates a strong refraction. It is in the middle of a wood, and all these steeples are seen beyond woods of rather large extent.”
Méchain & Delambre (1807), T2 p. 774
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1807). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 2). Paris: Baudouin.
Montjouy sea-horizon series, all near sunset, disagree with the levelling; Delambre states that refraction must have raised the sea more than in the mean state; one isolated sea-horizon observation can be wrong by 5 to 6 toises; the levelling is kept.
“toutes ces observations ayant été faites vers le coucher du soleil , la réfraction devoit élever la mer plus que dans l'état moyen et faire paroître la hauteur de la tour trop petite . Elles prouvent qu'une observation de l'horizon de la mer prise isolément peut donner une erreur de 5 à 6 toises .”
In English: “all these observations having been made towards sunset, refraction must have raised the sea more than in the mean state and made the height of the tower appear too small. They prove that an isolated observation of the sea horizon can give an error of 5 to 6 toises.”
Méchain & Delambre (1807), T2 p. 759
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1807). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 2). Paris: Baudouin.
Puy de Cambatjou: very large variations of terrestrial refraction, attributed to sight lines grazing the ground over a long distance on a flat summit.
“J'ai remarqué à cette station , plus que par-tout ailleurs , de très-grandes variations dans la réfraction terrestre ; cela provenoit sans doute de ce que les rayons visuels rasoient le sol dans une grande longueur.”
In English: “I noticed at this station, more than anywhere else, very large variations in terrestrial refraction; this no doubt came from the lines of sight grazing the ground over a great length.”
Méchain & Delambre (1806), T1 p. 321 · text layer
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1806). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 1). Paris: Baudouin.
Evening effect: the Boiscommun steeple rose into view at sunset; terrestrial refraction raises the same object more or less at different times and hides or reveals it.
“Cet effet des réfractions terrestres qui , à différens temps ou à différentes heures du même jour , et , suivant les variations atmosphériques , élève plus ou moins le même objet , le cache ou le rend visible , est singulièrement curieux”
In English: “This effect of terrestrial refractions which, at different times or different hours of the same day, and according to atmospheric variations, raises the same object more or less, hides it or makes it visible, is singularly curious”
Méchain & Delambre (1806), T1 Discours préliminaire p. 37 · text layer
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1806). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 1). Paris: Baudouin.

Observing rules

One series of ten for each zenith distance, unless something made the observation doubtful. In fine weather Delambre preferred to repeat the angles of position; in bad weather, he says, the variations of terrestrial refraction would have made the zenith distances more uncertain. Distances above 91 or below 89 degrees should be observed at the same time as the angle, which was hardly possible, so they were taken just before or after, or at several hours of the day.
“Quand le temps étoit beau , j'aimois mieux répéter l'observation des angles de position , et , s'il étoit mauvais , les variations de la réfraction terrestre auroient rendu les distances au zénith plus incertaines.”
In English: “When the weather was fine, I preferred to repeat the observation of the angles of position, and, if it was bad, the variations of terrestrial refraction would have made the zenith distances more uncertain.”
Méchain & Delambre (1806), T1 Discours préliminaire p. 113
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1806). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 1). Paris: Baudouin.
For the reduction, choose the zenith distance observed at the hour nearest the angle, when refraction is believed least different.
“afin de choisir pour la réduction des angles à l'horizon la distance dont l'heure étoit la plus voisine , et où l'on avoit lieu de croire la réfraction terrestre moins différente.”
In English: “in order to choose for the reduction of the angles to the horizon the distance whose hour was nearest, and where one had reason to believe terrestrial refraction least different.”
Méchain & Delambre (1806), T1 Discours préliminaire p. 113
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1806). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 1). Paris: Baudouin.
Reverberatory lamps: best possible target in theory, but they need lit wires, night work on remote mountains and an attendant at each station; Méchain used them between Barcelona and Tortosa and near Cullera and Oropesa, with no better agreement than day signals.
“Dans ces cas le meilleur de tous les signaux , sans contredit , seroit une lampe à réverbère .”
In English: “In these cases the best of all signals, without doubt, would be a reverberatory lamp.”
Méchain & Delambre (1806), T1 Discours préliminaire p. 105 · text layer
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1806). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 1). Paris: Baudouin.
When placing a signal, look a few minutes above and below in the same vertical, since terrestrial refraction could change the background against which it is seen.
“Comme la réfraction terrestre pourroit changer un peu les apparences , examinez quelques minutes au-dessus et au-dessous de B dans le même vertical”
In English: “As terrestrial refraction could change the appearances a little, examine a few minutes above and below B in the same vertical”
Méchain & Delambre (1806), T1 Discours préliminaire p. 110 · text layer
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1806). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 1). Paris: Baudouin.
Signal height rule: height = 3/20000 of the distance, so it subtends at least 30 seconds; H = D sin 31" = 0.00015 D.
“Après plusieurs essais je me suis fait la règle de donner à mes signaux une hauteur égale à 3/20000 de la distance. De cette manière cette hauteur paroissoit sous un angle de 30" au moins.”
In English: “After several trials I made it my rule to give my signals a height equal to 3/20000 of the distance. In this way this height appeared under an angle of at least 30".”
Méchain & Delambre (1806), T1 Discours préliminaire p. 107
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1806). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 1). Paris: Baudouin.
Zenith distances observed before sunset are noted as surer, terrestrial refraction being smaller.
“Ces distances sont plus sûres que les précédentes . Le soleil n'étoit pas encore couché ; la réfraction terrestre étoit moindre.”
In English: “These distances are surer than the preceding ones. The sun had not yet set; terrestrial refraction was smaller.”
Méchain & Delambre (1806), T1 p. 220 · text layer
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1806). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 1). Paris: Baudouin.

Recommended practice

To get n more precisely, many simultaneous reciprocal observations are needed.
“Pour avoir quelque chose de plus précis il faudroit des observations réciproques , simultanées , en très-grand nombre.”
In English: “To have something more precise, reciprocal simultaneous observations in very great number would be needed.”
Méchain & Delambre (1807), T2 p. 776
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1807). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 2). Paris: Baudouin.
Reciprocal distances are surer than the sea horizon because they assume only equal refraction at both ends and allow compensation.
“Les distances réciproques doivent être plus sûres en ce qu'elles ne supposent que l'égalité des deux réfractions et qu'on a la chance des compensations.”
In English: “Reciprocal distances must be surer in that they assume only the equality of the two refractions and one has the chance of compensations.”
Méchain & Delambre (1807), T2 p. 781
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1807). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 2). Paris: Baudouin.
Against lateral refraction: wait for favourable moments, repeat at different hours and circumstances.
“l'un est d'attendre les instans favorables ; l'autre , de répéter les observations à des heures et dans des circonstances différentes.”
In English: “one is to wait for favourable moments; the other, to repeat the observations at different hours and in different circumstances.”
Méchain & Delambre (1806), T1 Discours préliminaire p. 106
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1806). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 1). Paris: Baudouin.
The method of reciprocal zenith distances for the Earth's radius (Kepler, Riccioli) is rejected because terrestrial refraction alters both angles.
“Mais il est visible que la réfraction terrestre altère les deux angles . On ne doit donc faire aucun fond sur cette méthode”
In English: “But it is evident that terrestrial refraction alters the two angles. One must therefore place no reliance on this method”
Méchain & Delambre (1806), T1 Discours préliminaire p. 6 · text layer
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1806). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 1). Paris: Baudouin.

Triangle closures and excess

Refraction enters through the reduction of the angles to the horizon (unequal elevation of the signals). No refraction term in the spherical excess is stated; the excess is discussed separately (T2 p. 713).
“il en résulte que les trois angles qui doivent former chacun des triangles, ont toujours été observés dans des plans différens . Avant que d'en faire usage , il faut donc les ramener au même plan , ou du moins à une même surface .”
In English: “hence the three angles which must form each triangle were always observed in different planes. Before using them, they must therefore be brought to the same plane, or at least to the same surface.”
Méchain & Delambre (1806), T1 Discours préliminaire p. 137
Méchain, P., & Delambre, J.-B. J. (1806). Base du système métrique décimal, ou mesure de l'arc du méridien compris entre les parallèles de Dunkerque et Barcelone (Vol. 1). Paris: Baudouin.

Not found in the pages read

  • No numerical correction of horizontal angles for lateral refraction.
  • No statement that refraction enters the spherical excess.
  • No fixed hour window for zenith distances; the text gives only hour choices (nearest to the angle, before sunset) and notes on evening and fog.
  • Méchain's own formulas for n were not found by Delambre; T2 pp. 777-779 reconstruct them from his calculations.
  • Dates and hours for each of the 189 pairs are not in the n table; they are in the station records of T1 and T2.

Parallel of Paris, Brest to Strasbourg (Henry east part, Bonne west part), Nouvelle carte de France

France · 1818-1823

Angles of the eastern part were observed at night from 1818 with lamps (réverbères) at Laplace's request, but the night zenith distances disagreed so much that level differences were taken instead from day observations at second-order stations. Puissant states that night horizontal angles near sunset gave larger discordances than day angles and are as much affected by refraction as day ones, more so in low places, as seen between Paris and Strasbourg. No refraction correction of horizontal angles is stated. Heights of the western part come from reciprocal and simultaneous zenith distances, day and night, Brest to the Panthéon; the printed table gives n per series from 0.061 to 0.090. The Instruction asks for zenith distances in at least three series at different hours, mainly near midday, simultaneous where possible, and for avoiding angle observations in vapours and undulations.

Horizontal angles corrected for refraction: no

No correction for lateral refraction. The Instruction prescribed night or twilight angles with lamps on the Brest-Strasbourg perpendicular; the night results showed larger discordances, and Puissant judges night angles at least as much affected by refraction as day angles.

Night observations with lamps on this line, shortly after sunset, often gave much larger discordances than day observations.
“C'est sur une grande partie de cette ligne, confiée, dès l'origine des travaux, à MM. les colonels Bonne et Henry, que des réverbères ont été employés pour signaux; mais les observations nocturnes, à peu d'intervalle du coucher du soleil, ont souvent présenté des discordances beaucoup plus considérables que celles faites de jour.”
In English: “It is on a large part of this line, entrusted from the start of the work to Colonels Bonne and Henry, that lamps were used as signals; but the night observations, shortly after sunset, often showed much larger discordances than those made by day.”
Puissant (1832), 35
Puissant, L. (1832). Nouvelle description géométrique de la France (Première partie). Paris: Ch. Picquet.
Night horizontal angles, recommended by Laplace, are as much influenced by atmospheric refraction as day ones, sometimes more in low places, as remarked between Paris and Strasbourg.
“ainsi les observations nocturnes des angles horizontaux qui avaient été recommandées par le célèbre Laplace sont tout aussi influencées par les effets de la réfraction atmosphérique que celles de jour, si même elles ne le sont quelquefois davantage dans les lieux bas, comme on l'a remarqué, ainsi que nous l'avons déjà dit, entre Paris et Strasbourg, lors de la mesure du parallèle compris entre ces deux villes.”
In English: “thus the night observations of horizontal angles, which had been recommended by the celebrated Laplace, are just as influenced by the effects of atmospheric refraction as those by day, if indeed they are not sometimes more so in low places, as was remarked, as we have already said, between Paris and Strasbourg, during the measurement of the parallel between these two towns.”
Puissant (1832), 156
Puissant, L. (1832). Nouvelle description géométrique de la France (Première partie). Paris: Ch. Picquet.
Instruction of the commission of the Dépôt de la Guerre: angles on the Brest-Strasbourg perpendicular only to be observed at night or in twilight, with signals able to carry one or more lamps.
“Les observations angulaires, pour cette perpendiculaire seulement, seront faites de nuit ou dans le crépuscule ; et les signaux seront disposés de manière, à pouvoir recevoir un ou plusieurs réverbères, pour remplacer, dans ce cas, l'autre point de mire, qui n'est visible que de jour.”
In English: “The angular observations, for this perpendicular only, will be made at night or in twilight; and the signals will be arranged so as to carry one or more lamps, to replace in that case the other target, which is visible only by day.”
Puissant (1832), 20
Puissant, L. (1832). Nouvelle description géométrique de la France (Première partie). Paris: Ch. Picquet.

Vertical angles and heights

Level differences by the formula with the refraction coefficient n; n found from reciprocal zenith distances. East part: night zenith distances rejected, heights from day observations at second-order stations. West part: reciprocal simultaneous zenith distances, day and night, from the Roche Herpin to the Panthéon (Bonne, Epailly, Béraud), with n listed per series.

East part: the night zenith distances disagreed so much that level differences were obtained from day observations at second-order stations.
“Mais l'énorme discordance que présentèrent sur cette ligne la plupart des distances zénithales comparables, mirent dans la nécessité de rejeter ces observations nocturnes pour déterminer les différences de niveau par d'autres mesures angulaires recueillies de jour, à différentes stations du second ordre, liées à celles du premier ordre.”
In English: “But the enormous discordance shown on this line by most of the comparable zenith distances made it necessary to reject these night observations and to determine the level differences by other angular measures taken by day at various second-order stations tied to the first-order ones.”
Puissant (1832), 208
Puissant, L. (1832). Nouvelle description géométrique de la France (Première partie). Paris: Ch. Picquet.
Formula for single zenith distances uses n, the refraction coefficient supposed known; its value is given by reciprocal zenith distances.
“n désignant le coefficient de la réfraction supposé connu. Sa valeur donnée par des distances zénithales réciproques est”
In English: “n denoting the refraction coefficient supposed known. Its value given by reciprocal zenith distances is”
Puissant (1832), 54
Puissant, L. (1832). Nouvelle description géométrique de la France (Première partie). Paris: Ch. Picquet.
The two zenith distances are assumed to belong to the same state of the air, which in general holds only when they are observed at the same time; they may also contain the constant error of the instrument.
“Il est à remarquer que les deux distances zénithales apparentes δ, δ' sont supposées répondre au même état de l'atmosphère; circonstance qui n'a lieu en général que quand ces distances sont observées à la même époque.”
In English: “Note that the two apparent zenith distances are supposed to correspond to the same state of the atmosphere; a circumstance which in general holds only when these distances are observed at the same time.”
Puissant (1832), 55
Puissant, L. (1832). Nouvelle description géométrique de la France (Première partie). Paris: Ch. Picquet.
West part: levelling Brest to the Panthéon by reciprocal and simultaneous zenith distances taken by day and by night.
“le nivellement trigonométrique exécuté depuis Brest jusqu'au Panthéon, par des distances zénithales réciproques et simultanées, prises de jour et de nuit, a donné la hauteur absolue du sommet de cet édifice”
In English: “the trigonometric levelling carried out from Brest to the Panthéon, by reciprocal and simultaneous zenith distances taken by day and by night, gave the absolute height of the top of this building”
Puissant (1832), 232
Puissant, L. (1832). Nouvelle description géométrique de la France (Première partie). Paris: Ch. Picquet.
Aim of the first-order zenith distances: errors on level differences not above two metres in the worst cases.
“aussi a-t-on observé à tous les sommets des triangles du 1er ordre autant de distances zénithales réciproques qu'il était nécessaire, pour que les erreurs commises sur les différences de niveau ne dépassassent pas deux mètres, dans les cas les plus défavorables.”
In English: “so at all the vertices of the first-order triangles as many reciprocal zenith distances were observed as necessary for the errors on level differences not to exceed two metres in the most unfavourable cases.”
Puissant (1832), 232
Puissant, L. (1832). Nouvelle description géométrique de la France (Première partie). Paris: Ch. Picquet.

Observing rules

Instruction: zenith distances in at least three series at different hours, above all near midday, simultaneous where possible, with barometer readings.
“Les distances zénithales relatives à ces chaînes seront elles-mêmes le résultat de trois séries au moins, prises à des heures différentes, et surtout vers le milieu du jour, afin qu'elles puissent donner les différences de niveau avec toute l'exactitude désirable, et, autant qu'il sera possible, par le concours d'observations simultanées : celles-ci seront accompagnées d'observations barométriques.”
In English: “The zenith distances for these chains will themselves be the result of at least three series, taken at different hours, and above all towards the middle of the day, so that they can give the level differences with all desirable accuracy, and, as far as possible, by the combination of simultaneous observations: these will be accompanied by barometric observations.”
Puissant (1832), 28
Puissant, L. (1832). Nouvelle description géométrique de la France (Première partie). Paris: Ch. Picquet.
Instruction: avoid observing angles when there are many vapours and undulations, or when the sun gives the signals marked phases.
“On évitera, autant que possible, de faire des observations d'angles dans les momens où il y a beaucoup de vapeurs et d'ondulations, et dans ceux où le soleil donnerait aux signaux des phases prononcées qui rendraient le pointé trop incertain.”
In English: “One will avoid, as far as possible, observing angles at moments when there are many vapours and undulations, and at those when the sun would give the signals marked phases that would make the pointing too uncertain.”
Puissant (1832), 28
Puissant, L. (1832). Nouvelle description géométrique de la France (Première partie). Paris: Ch. Picquet.
Instruction: first-order series carried, as far as possible, to the twentieth conjugate observation, second-order series to the tenth.
“Toutes les séries du premier ordre seront, autant que possible, prolongées jusqu'à la vingtième observation conjuguée; toutes celles du deuxième ordre s'arrêteront à la dixième.”
In English: “All first-order series will, as far as possible, be carried to the twentieth conjugate observation; all those of the second order will stop at the tenth.”
Puissant (1832), 28
Puissant, L. (1832). Nouvelle description géométrique de la France (Première partie). Paris: Ch. Picquet.

Recommended practice

For trigonometric levelling, sides K should not exceed 12000 m, so pointing is surer and refraction variability has less effect on level differences.
“En général il est nécessaire que K ne dépasse pas 12000 m, afin que le pointé soit plus certain et que la variabilité de la réfraction ait moins d'influence sur les différences de niveau.”
In English: “In general K must not exceed 12000 m, so that the pointing is surer and the variability of refraction has less influence on the level differences.”
Puissant (1832), 167 · text layer
Puissant, L. (1832). Nouvelle description géométrique de la France (Première partie). Paris: Ch. Picquet.
Puissant's conclusion on night angles: as much affected by refraction as day angles, sometimes more in low places.
“sont tout aussi influencées par les effets de la réfraction atmosphérique que celles de jour, si même elles ne le sont quelquefois davantage dans les lieux bas”
In English: “are just as influenced by the effects of atmospheric refraction as those by day, if indeed they are not sometimes more so in low places”
Puissant (1832), 156
Puissant, L. (1832). Nouvelle description géométrique de la France (Première partie). Paris: Ch. Picquet.

Brousseaud, arc of the mean parallel (45 N) across France, Cordouan to Mont Granier/Colombier, with link to the Austro-Sardinian chain

France · 1811-1827

Horizontal angles were not corrected for refraction; the response to varying results was more repetition, at least thirty per series, at different hours and days, with up to twelve or fifteen series when the first six differed by 15 to 20 seconds. Zenith distances of the signal tops were always taken between eleven and one o'clock, in at least six or eight series of ten. Heights come from reciprocal zenith distances from the tower of Cordouan to Colombier and Granier; the coefficient of refraction lay between 0.0563 and 0.097 (Puissant prints 0.0563 and 0.0907) and was generally 0.08, as Delambre had found. A night fire-signal longitude link between La Jonchère and Saint-Preuil was attributed to extraordinary refraction, and vapours over the Landes limited the alignment of the Bordeaux base to 4000 m sights.

Horizontal angles corrected for refraction: no

No refraction correction. Series of at least thirty repetitions at different hours and days; the number of series was raised when early results disagreed. Unexplained differences of 25 to 35 decimal seconds between series at the same hour on different days are reported.

Angles multiplied at least thirty times per series, series repeated at different hours and days; number of series set by the spread of the first five or six results.
“on prit la résolution, ainsi qu'il a été déjà signalé page 32, de multiplier les observations d'angles entre les objets terrestres trente fois au moins par chaque série, et de répéter ces séries à des heures et à des jours différens.”
In English: “it was resolved, as already noted on page 32, to multiply the observations of angles between terrestrial objects at least thirty times per series, and to repeat these series at different hours and on different days.”
Brousseaud (Colonel). (1839), 157
Brousseaud (Colonel). (1839). Mesure d'un arc du parallèle moyen entre le pôle et l'équateur. Limoges: F. Chapoulaud.
Differences of 25 to 35 decimal seconds between series of one angle at the same hour on different days, with both objects seen perfectly.
“On a remarqué des différences de vingt-cinq à trente-cinq secondes décimales entre les résultats des séries d'un même angle observé à la même heure et à des jours différens, quoique les deux objets sur lesquels on pointait se vissent parfaitement dans des circonstances semblables.”
In English: “Differences of twenty-five to thirty-five decimal seconds were noticed between the results of series of the same angle observed at the same hour and on different days, although the two objects pointed at were seen perfectly in similar circumstances.”
Brousseaud (Colonel). (1839), 158
Brousseaud (Colonel). (1839). Mesure d'un arc du parallèle moyen entre le pôle et l'équateur. Limoges: F. Chapoulaud.
La Ferlanderie azimuth mark (south, 4969 m): agitation of the air kept displacing it; attributed to vapours from the cut through the woods or the sight line grazing ground or treetops; the mark could not be used.
“celle du sud a offert constamment des difficultés à être bien vue à cause de l'agitation de l'air, qui semblait la déplacer sans cesse du fil méridien de la lunette.”
In English: “the southern one constantly gave difficulty in being seen well because of the agitation of the air, which seemed to displace it ceaselessly from the meridian wire of the telescope.”
Brousseaud (Colonel). (1839), 52 · text layer
Brousseaud (Colonel). (1839). Mesure d'un arc du parallèle moyen entre le pôle et l'équateur. Limoges: F. Chapoulaud.
Bordeaux base alignment: vapours and strong undulations over the Landes limited clear sight of the mark to 4000 m; only two to three hours a day were usable.
“Mais les vapeurs et les fortes ondulations dans l'air qu'elles produisent à la surface des landes n'ont permis de voir distinctement la mire portative qui a servi à cette opération qu'à 4000 mètres au plus”
In English: “But the vapours and the strong undulations in the air which they produce at the surface of the Landes allowed the portable mark used in this operation to be seen distinctly only at 4000 metres at most”
Brousseaud (Colonel). (1839), 143 · text layer
Brousseaud (Colonel). (1839). Mesure d'un arc du parallèle moyen entre le pôle et l'équateur. Limoges: F. Chapoulaud.

Vertical angles and heights

Reciprocal zenith distances, taken between 11 h and 13 h in six or eight series of ten, carried heights from the tower of Cordouan (68.445 m above mean sea) to Colombier and Granier. A 9 m discordance from long sights from Bossivre to Montoncelle and Pierre-sur-Autre was removed by Puissant using other stations. The coefficient of refraction stayed between 0.0563 and 0.097, generally 0.08.

Zenith distances of signal tops always taken between eleven and one o'clock, repeated in at least six or eight series of ten.
“Les distances au zénith de l'extrémité des signaux ont toujours été prises entre onze heures et une heure du soir, et répétées au moins par six ou huit séries de dix angles simples chaque.”
In English: “The zenith distances of the tops of the signals were always taken between eleven o'clock and one in the afternoon, and repeated in at least six or eight series of ten single angles each.”
Brousseaud (Colonel). (1839), 158
Brousseaud (Colonel). (1839). Mesure d'un arc du parallèle moyen entre le pôle et l'équateur. Limoges: F. Chapoulaud.
Range and usual value of the refraction coefficient over the whole levelling.
“2° que, sur toute son étendue, la valeur du coefficient de la réfraction est renfermée entre 0,0563 et 0,097, et se trouve généralement de 0,08, ainsi que Delambre l'avait déduit de ses observations.”
In English: “2nd that, over its whole extent, the value of the refraction coefficient lies between 0.0563 and 0.097, and is generally 0.08, as Delambre had deduced from his observations.”
Brousseaud (Colonel). (1839), 160
Brousseaud (Colonel). (1839). Mesure d'un arc du parallèle moyen entre le pôle et l'équateur. Limoges: F. Chapoulaud.
The 9 m discordance came partly from the small caps on the far signals, too small to see at such distances one hour either side of noon, when these zenith observations were always made.
“de ce que les chapeaux placés à l'extrémité des flèches de ces signaux, et qui servaient de point de mire, étaient trop petits pour être bien vus à de si grandes distances une heure avant et une heure après midi, époques du jour où ces observations zénithales ont toujours été faites (page 158).”
In English: “because the caps placed at the tips of these signals, which served as targets, were too small to be seen well at such great distances one hour before and one hour after noon, the times of day when these zenith observations were always made (page 158).”
Brousseaud (Colonel). (1839), 160
Brousseaud (Colonel). (1839). Mesure d'un arc du parallèle moyen entre le pôle et l'équateur. Limoges: F. Chapoulaud.
Puissant (1832) on the same levelling: coefficient between 0.0563 and 0.0907, generally n = 0.08 as from Delambre's observations.
“il est en outre assez remarquable que, dans toute son étendue, la valeur du coefficient de la réfraction est renfermée entre 0,0563 et 0,0907 ; et que généralement n = 0,08, ainsi qu'il résulte des observations de Delambre.”
In English: “it is moreover quite remarkable that, over its whole extent, the value of the refraction coefficient lies between 0.0563 and 0.0907; and that generally n = 0.08, as follows from Delambre's observations.”
Puissant (1832), 292
Puissant, L. (1832). Nouvelle description géométrique de la France (Première partie). Paris: Ch. Picquet.
