Observing rules

Hours for zenith distances: between 11 h and 13 h.
“Les distances au zénith de l'extrémité des signaux ont toujours été prises entre onze heures et une heure du soir”
In English: “The zenith distances of the tops of the signals were always taken between eleven o'clock and one in the afternoon”
Brousseaud (Colonel). (1839), 158
Brousseaud (Colonel). (1839). Mesure d'un arc du parallèle moyen entre le pôle et l'équateur. Limoges: F. Chapoulaud.
Signals were mostly low wooden pyramids; their construction left little interval between the instrument centre and the cap used as target, and the pyramid axis could be set vertical.
“La construction de ces signaux laissait peu d'intervalle entre le centre de l'instrument et le chapeau, point de mire des signaux correspondant”
In English: “The construction of these signals left little interval between the centre of the instrument and the cap, the target of the corresponding signals”
Brousseaud (Colonel). (1839), 158
Brousseaud (Colonel). (1839). Mesure d'un arc du parallèle moyen entre le pôle et l'équateur. Limoges: F. Chapoulaud.
Austro-Sardinian and French parts: zenith distances repeated 60 to 100 times between eleven and noon for the heights of the high mountain signals.
“Les distances au zénith ont aussi été répétées de soixante à cent fois, entre onze heures et midi, afin d'avoir avec exactitude la hauteur absolue des signaux placés sur les hautes montagnes de la France et de la Savoie.”
In English: “The zenith distances were also repeated sixty to a hundred times, between eleven and noon, to obtain accurately the absolute height of the signals placed on the high mountains of France and Savoy.”
Brousseaud (Colonel). (1839), 36 · text layer
Brousseaud (Colonel). (1839). Mesure d'un arc du parallèle moyen entre le pôle et l'équateur. Limoges: F. Chapoulaud.

Recommended practice

Night fire signals La Jonchère to Saint-Preuil: the difficulties were attributed to extraordinary refraction; the two stations were mutually visible only through it, which was recognised only afterwards.
“En réunissant toutes les circonstances de cette opération on a supposé que les difficultés qu'elle a présentées se liaient au phénomène des réfractions extraordinaires. Sans doute le choix des deux stations, qui n'ont été réciproquement visibles que par l'effet de ce phénomène, est une circonstance malheureuse”
In English: “Putting together all the circumstances of this operation, it was supposed that its difficulties were linked to the phenomenon of extraordinary refractions. No doubt the choice of the two stations, which were mutually visible only through this phenomenon, is an unfortunate circumstance”
Brousseaud (Colonel). (1839), 12 · text layer
Brousseaud (Colonel). (1839). Mesure d'un arc du parallèle moyen entre le pôle et l'équateur. Limoges: F. Chapoulaud.

Not found in the pages read

  • No per-line table of refraction coefficients in Brousseaud (1839); only the range and usual value.
  • No correction of horizontal angles for lateral refraction.
  • No use of heliotropes or lamps for the geodetic angles; lamps and fires appear only for longitude signals.
  • No statement that refraction enters the triangle closures or the spherical excess.
  • Brousseaud cites the 6th number of the Mémorial du Dépôt de la Guerre, pp. 291-292, for this result; Puissant (1832) printed pp. 291-292 carry the same statement, with no per-line values.
Pages read: Brousseaud (Colonel). (1839): pdf pp. 7, 17-18, 42, 58, 157, 171-174 (text layer; page images 172, 174); Puissant (1832): pdf pp. 176, 302-303 (Puissant's remark on the levelling of the mean parallel)

Trigonometrical Survey of England and Wales (Ordnance), Roy 1784-1788 and Williams, Mudge and Dalby 1791-1799

Britain · 1784-1799

Horizontal angles were not corrected for refraction. The observers controlled it by observing only when the air was steady (mornings, evenings, close cloudy days) and by using white lights and reverberatory lamps at night. Heights came from reciprocal angles of elevation and depression, with terrestrial refraction expressed as a fraction of the contained arc: Roy found 1/2 to 1/24 on his lines and used 1/10 for single observations; Williams, Mudge and Dalby found a mean of 1/12 (1795), a range of 1/7 to 1/34 (1797), and used 1/12 or 1/15 for single observations. Reciprocal observations were not simultaneous until Mudge's hourly simultaneous series of 1799, which gave about 1/10 to 1/11. The authors state that refraction decreases with station height, is irregular when rays pass near the ground (moist vapours given as the main cause), and that single observations cannot be relied on.

Horizontal angles corrected for refraction: no

No correction of horizontal angles for refraction is printed. Unsteady air ('tremulous motion or boiling', 'quivering motion') was handled by waiting for steady air and by night observation of lights.

Roy: in hot weather the air was too tremulous for accurate pointing except for short periods in the mornings and evenings; a whole day could pass for one good observation.
“At particular times, especially in hot weather, there was such a tremulous motion or boiling in the air, that it was only during a very short space, chiefly in the mornings and evenings, that the objects were sufficiently distinct to be observed with accuracy. So difficult it is to do any thing perfectly good in this way, that a whole day has frequently been spent, after watching with anxious care, in obtaining a single one that was perfectly satisfactory!”
Roy (1790), p. 170
Roy, W. (1790). An account of the trigonometrical operation, whereby the distance between the meridians of the Royal Observatories of Greenwich and Paris has been determined. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 80, 111-270 (with Dalby, I., Remarks, pp. 593-614).
Roy: a bad observation was left blank in the column of observed angles.
“Sometimes an observation has been entirely lost, or at least that which had been obtained was not thought a very good one. In such cases a blank has been left in the column of observed angles, and also in that expressing the error.”
Roy (1790), p. 170
Roy, W. (1790). An account of the trigonometrical operation, whereby the distance between the meridians of the Royal Observatories of Greenwich and Paris has been determined. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 80, 111-270 (with Dalby, I., Remarks, pp. 593-614).
Williams, Mudge and Dalby 1795: bearings of intersected objects were taken in the morning or evening, free of vapour and of the midday quivering.
“That the bearings of those might be taken with precision, the observations were made either in the morning or evening, when the air was free from vapour, and without that quivering motion, which, in summer, it generally has in the middle of the day.”
Williams et al. (1795), pp. 456-457
Williams, E., Mudge, W., & Dalby, I. (1795). An account of the trigonometrical survey carried on in the years 1791, 1792, 1793, and 1794. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 85, 414-591.
1795: at Beachy Head the meridian staff could not be seen distinctly in sunshine because of heat.
“owing to the effects of heat, the air was not sufficiently steady for the staff to be seen distinctly, when the star came to its greatest elongation in the day time, if the sun shone out.”
Williams et al. (1795), p. 461 · text layer
Williams, E., Mudge, W., & Dalby, I. (1795). An account of the trigonometrical survey carried on in the years 1791, 1792, 1793, and 1794. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 85, 414-591.
Mudge 1801: pole-star azimuths were observed in April, when the sun causes little tremor at the times of elongation.
“the star comes to its greatest elongations from the meridian at those times, when the sun produces little tremor in the air, by which means, the staff to which the Pole Star is referred, in good weather, is easily perceived.”
Mudge (1801), p. 5 · text layer
Mudge, W. (1801). An account of the operations carried on for accomplishing a trigonometrical survey of England and Wales, continued from the year 1797, to the end of the year 1799 (Vol. 2, from the Philosophical Transactions). London: W. Faden.

Vertical angles and heights

Reciprocal angles of elevation and depression were observed with the great theodolite at different times, reduced to the telescope axis, and the mean refraction found as half the difference between the contained arc and the sum of the depressions (or the corresponding rule with an elevation). Refraction is stated as a fraction of the contained arc. Heights were carried from levelled points (Dover Castle, High Nook, Dunnose, Maker, Trevose Head). Single observations used an adopted fraction (Roy 1/10; 1795 1/12; 1797 1/15). No barometer or thermometer correction was applied.

Roy: earlier authorities gave 1/9 (Bouguer), 1/10 (Maskelyne), 1/14 (Lambert); Roy's observations give 1/3 to 1/24, and he expected it to vanish on much higher stations.
“for from these observations it will appear, that terrestrial refraction, instead of being 1/9th of the comprehended arc, according to M. Bouguer, 1/10th according to Dr. Maskelyne, 1/14th according to M. Lambert, varies from 1/3 to 1/24th part of that arc; and perhaps, if it had been possible for us to have tried it on heights considerably more elevated, we should have found it almost wholly to vanish.”
Roy (1790), p. 236
Roy, W. (1790). An account of the trigonometrical operation, whereby the distance between the meridians of the Royal Observatories of Greenwich and Paris has been determined. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 80, 111-270 (with Dalby, I., Remarks, pp. 593-614).
Roy: conclusive work would need levelled heights, barometers and thermometers at both stations, and two observers and instruments for simultaneous reciprocal angles.
“Besides barometers and thermometers at both stations, two observers and two instruments of the same kind would have been necessary, for taking at the same instant the reciprocal angle of elevation and depression.”
Roy (1790), p. 235 · text layer
Roy, W. (1790). An account of the trigonometrical operation, whereby the distance between the meridians of the Royal Observatories of Greenwich and Paris has been determined. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 80, 111-270 (with Dalby, I., Remarks, pp. 593-614).
Roy: west of Greenwich only single observations; heights there were computed with 1/10 of the arc.
“to the westward of Greenwich, no double but only single observations were obtained; wherefore, the relative heights of these stations have been determined by taking 1/10th of the arc of distance for the effect of terrestrial refraction.”
Roy (1790), pp. 237-238
Roy, W. (1790). An account of the trigonometrical operation, whereby the distance between the meridians of the Royal Observatories of Greenwich and Paris has been determined. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 80, 111-270 (with Dalby, I., Remarks, pp. 593-614).
Roy: no barometer or thermometer correction was applied.
“But we have not attempted to apply any correction on that account, because it could not be done in a satisfactory manner, and consequently could not be useful, unless the circumstances had permitted reciprocal observations to have been made at corresponding times with double sets of instruments, which in our situation was impossible.”
Roy (1790), p. 245
Roy, W. (1790). An account of the trigonometrical operation, whereby the distance between the meridians of the Royal Observatories of Greenwich and Paris has been determined. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 80, 111-270 (with Dalby, I., Remarks, pp. 593-614).
Roy: refraction decreases as station height increases, and at times reaches 1/2 to 1/3 of the arc.
“terrestrial as well as celestial refraction certainly diminishes as the heights of the stations above the sea increase; and that, at particular times at least, it is much greater than has hitherto been supposed, even to between 1/2 and 1/3 part of the arc of distance, instead of being only 1/9th or 1/14th part.”
Roy (1790), p. 245
Roy, W. (1790). An account of the trigonometrical operation, whereby the distance between the meridians of the Royal Observatories of Greenwich and Paris has been determined. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 80, 111-270 (with Dalby, I., Remarks, pp. 593-614).
Williams, Mudge and Dalby 1795: the 240 ft against 321 ft disagreement for St. Ann's Hill comes from variable refraction; the elevation from Hampton Poor House was 17' 39" in 1787 and 8' 11" in 1792.
“this very great disagreement, however, principally arises from the variableness in the terrestrial refraction. In 1787, at the station near Hampton Poor House, the ground at St. Ann's Hill was elevated 17' 39"; but at the same station in 1792, when the axis of the instrument was at the same height above the ground, the elevation was only 8' 11".”
Williams et al. (1795), p. 584
Williams, E., Mudge, W., & Dalby, I. (1795). An account of the trigonometrical survey carried on in the years 1791, 1792, 1793, and 1794. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 85, 414-591.
1795: heights from vertical angles are not reliable to better than about 10 ft unless reciprocal observations are simultaneous; single observations cannot be relied on.
“that relative heights deduced from elevations, or depressions, cannot always be depended upon to less than about 10 feet, even supposing those heights are the means of two or three independent results, except, perhaps, reciprocal observations were made exactly at the same time. The very great difference in the observed elevations of St. Ann's Hill, proves that no dependance can be placed on single observations.”
Williams et al. (1795), p. 585
Williams, E., Mudge, W., & Dalby, I. (1795). An account of the trigonometrical survey carried on in the years 1791, 1792, 1793, and 1794. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 85, 414-591.
1795: refraction near the ground does not follow barometer and thermometer; examples of looming at Hounslow Heath and in the Isle of Wight are given, attributed to moist vapours.
“It has been conjectured, that the variations in terrestrial refraction, depend on the changes in the atmosphere indicated by the barometer and thermometer: this, however, cannot be the case when the rays of light pass near the earth's surface for any considerable distance.”
Williams et al. (1795), p. 586
Williams, E., Mudge, W., & Dalby, I. (1795). An account of the trigonometrical survey carried on in the years 1791, 1792, 1793, and 1794. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 85, 414-591.
1795: moist vapours given as the main cause of the unusual refractions near the ground.
“it is more than probable, that moist vapours were the principal cause of the very unusual refractions”
Williams et al. (1795), p. 587 · text layer
Williams, E., Mudge, W., & Dalby, I. (1795). An account of the trigonometrical survey carried on in the years 1791, 1792, 1793, and 1794. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 85, 414-591.
1797: rules for the mean refraction from reciprocal angles.
“2. Then, if both are depressions, subtract their sum from the contained arc, and half the remainder is the mean refraction.”
Williams et al. (1797), p. 470 · text layer
Williams, E., Mudge, W., & Dalby, I. (1797). An account of the trigonometrical survey carried on in the years 1795 and 1796. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 87, 432-541.
1797: residual height differences at Charton Common (29 ft between routes) were ascribed to the mean refractions and spread equally over the lines.
“Now, supposing the difference, or the errors, to arise from the mean refractions, and those errors to be nearly the same between every two stations, we shall obtain the corrected heights in the following manner”
Williams et al. (1797), p. 472 · text layer
Williams, E., Mudge, W., & Dalby, I. (1797). An account of the trigonometrical survey carried on in the years 1795 and 1796. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 87, 432-541.
Mudge 1801: refractions of rays high above the ground are smaller than theory had assumed; reciprocal angles are observed at different times.
“The refractions contained in this account, like those in our former Papers, tend to prove, that when rays of light pass horizontally, and considerably distant from the surface of the earth, they are less bent or refracted from their rectilinear courses, than theory and opinion have laid down as fact.”
Mudge (1801), p. 181
Mudge, W. (1801). An account of the operations carried on for accomplishing a trigonometrical survey of England and Wales, continued from the year 1797, to the end of the year 1799 (Vol. 2, from the Philosophical Transactions). London: W. Faden.
Mudge 1801: in June 1795 at Pilsden Hill, from about 4 o'clock, the top of Glastonbury Tor rose 10' 45" above its first apparent place; the depression observed the following evening was 29' 50"; the line passes over Sedgemoor.
“I perceived the top of the building gradually rise above the micrometer wire, and so continue to do, till it was elevated 10',45" above its first apparent situation; it then remained stationary, and as night drew on, the object became indistinct.”
Mudge (1801), p. 182 (note)
Mudge, W. (1801). An account of the operations carried on for accomplishing a trigonometrical survey of England and Wales, continued from the year 1797, to the end of the year 1799 (Vol. 2, from the Philosophical Transactions). London: W. Faden.
Mudge 1801: at Whiteham Hill the elevation changed by 44" and 42" while the reciprocal depression at White Horse Hill stayed nearly constant, which Mudge ascribes to local floating strata; a long series is needed.
“prove that some partial alteration, from floating strata, had taken place in the refraction near the former station.”
Mudge (1801), p. 188 · text layer
Mudge, W. (1801). An account of the operations carried on for accomplishing a trigonometrical survey of England and Wales, continued from the year 1797, to the end of the year 1799 (Vol. 2, from the Philosophical Transactions). London: W. Faden.
Mudge 1801: the second and third arcs are the most reliable because they are long and the ray passes about 300 ft above the ground.
“The refractions on the second and third arcs, I consider as most accurate, on account of the great distance between the stations; and also as more to be depended on, from the circumstance of the ray generally passing 300 feet above the ground.”
Mudge (1801), p. 189
Mudge, W. (1801). An account of the operations carried on for accomplishing a trigonometrical survey of England and Wales, continued from the year 1797, to the end of the year 1799 (Vol. 2, from the Philosophical Transactions). London: W. Faden.

Observing rules

Roy: the most distant stations could be made visible only by the white lights at night.
“It was, however, by the assistance of the white lights only, that the most distant stations could be rendered visible; and there cannot be a doubt, that, in great trigonometrical operations of this sort, they will be universally adopted hereafter.”
Roy (1790), p. 170
Roy, W. (1790). An account of the trigonometrical operation, whereby the distance between the meridians of the Royal Observatories of Greenwich and Paris has been determined. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 80, 111-270 (with Dalby, I., Remarks, pp. 593-614).
Roy: reverberatory lamps with 10 inch specula seen at 20 to 24 miles.
“wherefore single reverberatories were provided, with specula of ten inches diameter, and they were supplied with still larger burners, which could be seen at the distance of twenty or twenty-four miles.”
Roy (1790), p. 163 · text layer
Roy, W. (1790). An account of the trigonometrical operation, whereby the distance between the meridians of the Royal Observatories of Greenwich and Paris has been determined. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 80, 111-270 (with Dalby, I., Remarks, pp. 593-614).
Roy: temporary scaffolds were erected for the instrument at Greenwich, Shooter's Hill and Dover Castle; Lydd and Tenterden were steeple stations.
“temporary scaffolds to be erected at Greenwich Observatory, Shooters Hill, and Dover Castle, for the reception of the instrument.”
Roy (1790), p. 114 · text layer
Roy, W. (1790). An account of the trigonometrical operation, whereby the distance between the meridians of the Royal Observatories of Greenwich and Paris has been determined. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 80, 111-270 (with Dalby, I., Remarks, pp. 593-614).
1795: lamps preferred to white lights when distances are not too great.
“For these reasons, lamps are greatly preferable to white lights, when the distances are not too great.”
Williams et al. (1795), p. 450
Williams, E., Mudge, W., & Dalby, I. (1795). An account of the trigonometrical survey carried on in the years 1791, 1792, 1793, and 1794. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 85, 414-591.
1795: the vertical-angle tables rest on observations made on close cloudy days or toward evening.
“The observations, however, on which the tables are founded, were made in close cloudy days, or toward the evenings, when the tremulous motion in the air is commonly the least.”
Williams et al. (1795), p. 586
Williams, E., Mudge, W., & Dalby, I. (1795). An account of the trigonometrical survey carried on in the years 1791, 1792, 1793, and 1794. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 85, 414-591.
1797: telescope axis about 5 1/2 ft above ground; at St. Ann's Hill and Hampton Poor House the instrument stood on a half scaffold (axis 20 1/2 ft) and a whole scaffold (axis 36 1/2 ft).
“the axis of the telescope was always about 5 1/2 feet from the ground, unless the contrary is specified.”
Williams et al. (1797), p. 473
Williams, E., Mudge, W., & Dalby, I. (1797). An account of the trigonometrical survey carried on in the years 1795 and 1796. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 87, 432-541.
1797: lamps and staffs replaced white lights after white lights were often not seen.
“we have since substituted lamps and staffs in their stead.”
Williams et al. (1797), p. 443 · text layer
Williams, E., Mudge, W., & Dalby, I. (1797). An account of the trigonometrical survey carried on in the years 1795 and 1796. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 87, 432-541.
Mudge 1801: instructions for simultaneous refraction observations: hourly from 6 a.m., watches regulated by sun altitudes, weather noted, instrument sheltered, level adjusted, thermometer and barometer read.
“to observe the elevation or depression of the corresponding station at the expiration of every hour, beginning at six A. M. and to have the watch well regulated from observed altitudes of the sun's limb.”
Mudge (1801), p. 183
Mudge, W. (1801). An account of the operations carried on for accomplishing a trigonometrical survey of England and Wales, continued from the year 1797, to the end of the year 1799 (Vol. 2, from the Philosophical Transactions). London: W. Faden.
Mudge 1801: lamps used for the night hours (9 and 10 o'clock).
“At White Horse Hill and Whiteham Hill, lamps were used at the hours of 9 and 10”
Mudge (1801), p. 186 · text layer
Mudge, W. (1801). An account of the operations carried on for accomplishing a trigonometrical survey of England and Wales, continued from the year 1797, to the end of the year 1799 (Vol. 2, from the Philosophical Transactions). London: W. Faden.

Recommended practice

Roy: refraction work should be a separate operation with very fine levels, on high mountains near the sea such as Ben Nevis and Cruachan Ben.
“But, in order to be enabled to make conclusive observations of this sort, the operation should become a distinct one, or at most only comprehend such others as are connected with the modifications of the atmosphere. For purposes of this kind very fine levels would be requisite”
Roy (1790), p. 246 · text layer
Roy, W. (1790). An account of the trigonometrical operation, whereby the distance between the meridians of the Royal Observatories of Greenwich and Paris has been determined. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 80, 111-270 (with Dalby, I., Remarks, pp. 593-614).
Roy: night operations with white lights should not be undertaken late in the season.
“operations of this sort, where the most important observations could only be made at night, by means of the white lights, should never be undertaken in the latter season.”
Roy (1790), p. 115 · text layer
Roy, W. (1790). An account of the trigonometrical operation, whereby the distance between the meridians of the Royal Observatories of Greenwich and Paris has been determined. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 80, 111-270 (with Dalby, I., Remarks, pp. 593-614).
Maskelyne (quoted by Roy): note the thermometer at the object as well as at the observer to correct refraction.
“it would be of use to have a person to note the thermometer at the object as well as at the station of the observer”
Roy (1790), p. 235 (note) · text layer
Roy, W. (1790). An account of the trigonometrical operation, whereby the distance between the meridians of the Royal Observatories of Greenwich and Paris has been determined. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 80, 111-270 (with Dalby, I., Remarks, pp. 593-614).
Mudge 1801: accurate conclusions need a long series of simultaneous observations.
“this suggested the idea, that to afford any accurate conclusions in this way, a long series of observations would be necessary.”
Mudge (1801), p. 188 · text layer
Mudge, W. (1801). An account of the operations carried on for accomplishing a trigonometrical survey of England and Wales, continued from the year 1797, to the end of the year 1799 (Vol. 2, from the Philosophical Transactions). London: W. Faden.

Triangle closures and excess

Roy computed the spherical excess from the plane area of each triangle and compared it with the excess of the observed sum; refraction is not mentioned in this calculation.
“a column is inserted containing the spherical excess; and another for the difference or error between that and the excess of the sum of the observed angles above 180°”
Roy (1790), p. 169 · text layer
Roy, W. (1790). An account of the trigonometrical operation, whereby the distance between the meridians of the Royal Observatories of Greenwich and Paris has been determined. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 80, 111-270 (with Dalby, I., Remarks, pp. 593-614).
1795: triangle errors were distributed among the angles (equally where angles seemed equally good); refraction is not given as a cause.
“when any triangle has been found in excess or defect, and all the angles have appeared to be determined with equal accuracy, the corrections”
Williams et al. (1795), p. 493 · text layer
Williams, E., Mudge, W., & Dalby, I. (1795). An account of the trigonometrical survey carried on in the years 1791, 1792, 1793, and 1794. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 85, 414-591.

Not found in the pages read

  • Any correction of horizontal angles for lateral refraction (searched 'lateral', 'refraction' in the angle sections of Roy 1790, 1795, 1797, 1801).
  • A link between refraction and triangle closure or spherical excess in any of the four sources.
  • Heliotropes (not used in these years; lights and lamps only).
  • A numeric coefficient for the 1801 Table (Art. XLIV, pp. 177-178) beyond the zero for Lugshorn Corner and Greylock's Foss-way: the fractions in this Google scan are too small to read.

Anglo-French triangulation of 1787 (Greenwich to Paris junction across the Channel)

Britain · 1787-1788

Neither Roy nor Legendre corrected horizontal angles for refraction; Legendre reduced them to the horizon with the observed elevations and depressions. The cross-Channel and coast angles were taken at night on white lights and reverberatory lamps, by agreed times of reciprocal observation. Roy measured terrestrial refraction on the Kent lines and on three single-observation lines to the French coast: Dover to Calais between 1/15 and 1/16, Folkestone Turnpike to Calais 1/6 to 1/7, Folkestone Turnpike to Montlambert 1/17 of the contained arc. Legendre took refraction as about 1/14 of the distance in arc and recommended applying it to computed depressions and elevations on long lines.

Horizontal angles corrected for refraction: no

No refraction correction of horizontal angles. Legendre gives the reduction of an angle to the horizon from the elevations or depressions of its two sides; Roy observed at night by lights and waited for steady air by day.

Legendre: reduction of an angle to the horizon uses the depressions of its sides, observed directly with the circle or computed from known heights.
“Si on a observé immédiatement avec le cercle les angles de hauteur ou de dépression des deux objets, on les réduira en secondes, & on aura α & β; mais si par d'autres mesures on connoît les hauteurs absolues des deux stations par rapport à celle où l'on observe, & si on connoît en même temps la distance approchée de ces stations, on déterminera comme il suit chacun des angles α & β.”
In English: “If the angles of elevation or depression of the two objects were observed directly with the circle, they are reduced to seconds, giving α and β; but if the absolute heights of the two stations relative to the station of observation are known by other measures, together with their approximate distance, each of the angles α and β is found as follows.”
checked on the page image
Legendre (1789), p. 354
Legendre, A.-M. (1789). Mémoire sur les opérations trigonométriques, dont les résultats dépendent de la figure de la Terre. Histoire de l'Académie royale des sciences, année 1787, Mémoires, 352-383.
Legendre: the reduction to the horizon often reaches several seconds and must be computed because the circle gives angles to two seconds.
“il faut tenir compte sur-tout de la réduction à l'horizon qui monte assez souvent à plusieurs secondes”
In English: “above all, the reduction to the horizon must be taken into account, which quite often amounts to several seconds”
checked on the page image
Legendre (1789), p. 352
Legendre, A.-M. (1789). Mémoire sur les opérations trigonométriques, dont les résultats dépendent de la figure de la Terre. Histoire de l'Académie royale des sciences, année 1787, Mémoires, 352-383.
Roy: tremulous air in hot weather limited accurate observation to mornings and evenings.
“At particular times, especially in hot weather, there was such a tremulous motion or boiling in the air, that it was only during a very short space, chiefly in the mornings and evenings, that the objects were sufficiently distinct to be observed with accuracy.”
Roy (1790), p. 170
Roy, W. (1790). An account of the trigonometrical operation, whereby the distance between the meridians of the Royal Observatories of Greenwich and Paris has been determined. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 80, 111-270 (with Dalby, I., Remarks, pp. 593-614).

Vertical angles and heights

Roy computed refraction from elevations and depressions against heights levelled at Dover Castle (469 ft axis) and High Nook, and stated it as a fraction of the contained arc. On the three lines to the French coast the values come from single observations combined with the Dover and Folkestone heights. Legendre adopted about 1/14 of the distance in great-circle arc, checked by reciprocal zenith distances from the Méridienne vérifiée.

Legendre: the directly measured depression (α) is always found smaller than the computed one, because terrestrial refraction raises the ray; the amount is about 1/14 of the distance in great-circle arc.
“Si on comparoit la valeur de α avec celle qu'auroit donnée la mesure immédiate, on trouveroit toujours celle-ci moindre, à cause de la réfraction terrestre qui élève le rayon parti d'un objet éloigné. La quantité de cette réfraction est à peu-près [1/14] de la distance du lieu observé, réduite en degrés & minutes de grand cercle”
In English: “If the value of α were compared with that given by direct measurement, the latter would always be found smaller, because terrestrial refraction raises the ray coming from a distant object. The amount of this refraction is about 1/14 of the distance of the observed place, reduced to degrees and minutes of a great circle”
text layer only (pdf_page 0); no local page image for this page; checked against the text layer. The fraction on p. 355 is lost in the text layer; the denominator 14 is legible in the p. 356 repetition, and the p. 356 example (14000 toises, about 1' 3") fits 1/14.
Legendre (1789), pp. 355-356 · text layer
Legendre, A.-M. (1789). Mémoire sur les opérations trigonométriques, dont les résultats dépendent de la figure de la Terre. Histoire de l'Académie royale des sciences, année 1787, Mémoires, 352-383.
Legendre: with reciprocal zenith distances, their sum falls short of 180° plus the arc by twice the refraction; this shows the refraction is about 1/14 of the distance.
“on trouvera que la somme des deux distances au zénith, qui, sans la réfraction, seroit égale à 180d, plus l'arc de grand cercle compris entre les deux stations, sera moindre que cette quantité du double de la réfraction à chaque lieu”
In English: “one finds that the sum of the two zenith distances, which without refraction would equal 180° plus the great-circle arc between the stations, is less than this quantity by twice the refraction at each place”
text layer only (pdf_page 0); no local page image for this page; checked against the text layer
Legendre (1789), p. 356 · text layer
Legendre, A.-M. (1789). Mémoire sur les opérations trigonométriques, dont les résultats dépendent de la figure de la Terre. Histoire de l'Académie royale des sciences, année 1787, Mémoires, 352-383.
Roy, Dover Castle to Calais: depression 17' 59" against 19' 27".1 without refraction; difference 1' 28".1; arc 22' 28".2 divided by it gives 15.3.
“the difference is 1' 28".1, by which, dividing the length of the arc of distance 22'. 28".2, we have in this instance 15.3, or between 1/15th and 1/16th part, for the quantity of terrestrial refraction.”
Roy (1790), p. 241
Roy, W. (1790). An account of the trigonometrical operation, whereby the distance between the meridians of the Royal Observatories of Greenwich and Paris has been determined. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 80, 111-270 (with Dalby, I., Remarks, pp. 593-614).
Roy, Dover Castle to Folkestone Turnpike (reciprocal): mean refraction 12".8, about 1/24 of the arc.
“And 5' 9".4 the contained arc of a great circle or arc of distance being divided by 12".8, the mean refraction at the two stations, we have in this instance about 1/24th part for the quantity of terrestrial refraction.”
Roy (1790), p. 240
Roy, W. (1790). An account of the trigonometrical operation, whereby the distance between the meridians of the Royal Observatories of Greenwich and Paris has been determined. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 80, 111-270 (with Dalby, I., Remarks, pp. 593-614).
Roy: no barometer or thermometer correction was applied.
“But we have not attempted to apply any correction on that account, because it could not be done in a satisfactory manner”
Roy (1790), p. 245
Roy, W. (1790). An account of the trigonometrical operation, whereby the distance between the meridians of the Royal Observatories of Greenwich and Paris has been determined. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 80, 111-270 (with Dalby, I., Remarks, pp. 593-614).
Roy: refraction at the cliff foot, cliff top and castle top would probably differ on the Dover to Calais line.
“If the circumstances had permitted the refraction on the distance between Dover and Calais to have been repeatedly tried, at the bottom of the cliff, at the top of the cliff, and again at the top of the castle, we should probably have found the refraction at the three stations considerably different”
Roy (1790), p. 246
Roy, W. (1790). An account of the trigonometrical operation, whereby the distance between the meridians of the Royal Observatories of Greenwich and Paris has been determined. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 80, 111-270 (with Dalby, I., Remarks, pp. 593-614).

Observing rules

Roy: white lights for long distances and reverberatory lamps were supplied to both sides of the Channel; times of reciprocal observation were agreed at Dover.
“A great number of white lights, fitted for long distances, and several reverberatory lamps had been previously provided. Having been supplied with such a proportion of the lights as seemed necessary for their side of the channel, and one of the lamps, the French Gentlemen departed for Calais on the 25th”
Roy (1790), p. 113 · text layer
Roy, W. (1790). An account of the trigonometrical operation, whereby the distance between the meridians of the Royal Observatories of Greenwich and Paris has been determined. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 80, 111-270 (with Dalby, I., Remarks, pp. 593-614).
Roy: the Dover and Fairlight Down nights were favourable, so Blancnez and Montlambert were intersected.
“the nights happened very fortunately to be favourable, so as to enable us to intersect with great accuracy, the two distant points on the French coast of Blancnez and Montlambert”
Roy (1790), p. 114 · text layer
Roy, W. (1790). An account of the trigonometrical operation, whereby the distance between the meridians of the Royal Observatories of Greenwich and Paris has been determined. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 80, 111-270 (with Dalby, I., Remarks, pp. 593-614).
Roy: in good weather a day observation on a flag-staff is preferable to a night observation of white lights, which is hurried.
“In very favourable circumstances of the weather a good observation by day is preferable to one by the white lights at night”
Roy (1790), p. 170
Roy, W. (1790). An account of the trigonometrical operation, whereby the distance between the meridians of the Royal Observatories of Greenwich and Paris has been determined. Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London, 80, 111-270 (with Dalby, I., Remarks, pp. 593-614).
Legendre: if distinct signals are hard to obtain, use fires observed at night behind a mast at the station centre; a reverberatory lamp is visible at seven to eight leagues.
“on pourroit y suppléer par des feux qu'on observeroit la nuit, & qui seroient allumés derrière un gros mât placé au centre de la station; une simple lampe à reverbère dirigée d'une station à l'autre, peut être vue assez distinctement à une distance de sept à huit lieues.”
In English: “one could replace them by fires observed at night, lit behind a large mast placed at the centre of the station; a simple reverberatory lamp directed from one station to the other can be seen quite distinctly at a distance of seven to eight leagues.”
text layer only (pdf_page 0); no local page image for this page; checked against the text layer; "vue" is OCR "Patte" in the text layer
Legendre (1789), p. 375 · text layer
Legendre, A.-M. (1789). Mémoire sur les opérations trigonométriques, dont les résultats dépendent de la figure de la Terre. Histoire de l'Académie royale des sciences, année 1787, Mémoires, 352-383.

Recommended practice

Legendre: on long lines, when the depression α is computed from heights and not measured with the circle, do not omit the refraction correction.
“Si on n'a pas la valeur de α immédiatement mesurée par le cercle, & par conséquent affectée de la réfraction, il sera bon de ne pas omettre cette correction dans les grandes distances”
In English: “If the value of α measured directly with the circle, and therefore affected by refraction, is not available, it is advisable not to omit this correction at great distances”
text layer only (pdf_page 0); no local page image for this page; checked against the text layer
Legendre (1789), p. 356 · text layer
Legendre, A.-M. (1789). Mémoire sur les opérations trigonométriques, dont les résultats dépendent de la figure de la Terre. Histoire de l'Académie royale des sciences, année 1787, Mémoires, 352-383.
Legendre: elevations need less precision than horizontal angles, but each point must be observed at least once so all angles can be reduced to the horizon.
“Il n'est pas nécessaire de mettre dans ces sortes d'observations la même précision que dans les angles horizontaux; mais il faut que la hauteur de chaque point ait été observée au moins une fois de quelque station, pour qu'on soit en état de réduire tous les angles à l'horizon.”
In English: “It is not necessary to put the same precision into these observations as into the horizontal angles; but the elevation of each point must have been observed at least once from some station, so that all the angles can be reduced to the horizon.”
Legendre (1789), p. 356 · text layer
Legendre, A.-M. (1789). Mémoire sur les opérations trigonométriques, dont les résultats dépendent de la figure de la Terre. Histoire de l'Académie royale des sciences, année 1787, Mémoires, 352-383.
Legendre: reciprocal observations at great distances would be very useful; those in the Méridienne vérifiée are not exact enough by the author's own statement.
“Les observations de ce genre, faites à de grandes distances, seroient très-intéressantes”
In English: “Observations of this kind, made at great distances, would be very interesting”
Legendre (1789), p. 356 · text layer
Legendre, A.-M. (1789). Mémoire sur les opérations trigonométriques, dont les résultats dépendent de la figure de la Terre. Histoire de l'Académie royale des sciences, année 1787, Mémoires, 352-383.
Legendre: measure elevations and depressions frequently, to reduce the chain to a constant horizon such as sea level.
“Il faudroit avoir soin de mesurer fréquemment les hauteurs ou abaissemens des stations vues les unes des autres, afin d'être à portée, par cette espèce de nivellement, de réduire toute la chaîne de triangles à un horizon constant, tel que le niveau de la mer.”
In English: “Care should be taken to measure frequently the elevations or depressions of the stations seen from one another, so as to be able, by this kind of levelling, to reduce the whole chain of triangles to a constant horizon such as sea level.”
text layer only (pdf_page 0); no local page image for this page; checked against the text layer
Legendre (1789), p. 375 · text layer
Legendre, A.-M. (1789). Mémoire sur les opérations trigonométriques, dont les résultats dépendent de la figure de la Terre. Histoire de l'Académie royale des sciences, année 1787, Mémoires, 352-383.

Triangle closures and excess

Legendre: the reduction to the horizon produces triangles very slightly curved, whose excess over 180° must be kept; refraction is not linked to the excess.
“Car en les considérant comme rectilignes, on négligeroit le petit excès de la somme des trois angles sur 180 degrés”
In English: “For by treating them as rectilinear one would neglect the small excess of the sum of the three angles over 180 degrees”
checked on the page image

Ordnance Trigonometrical Survey of Great Britain and Ireland, Principal Triangulation

Britain · 1783-1853 (published 1858)

Horizontal angles were not corrected for refraction; observations were taken in arcs, seldom under unfavourable conditions, with atmospheric remarks (very hazy, much motion, faint) written against them, and long lines were observed on heliostats. Heights came from reciprocal but not simultaneous zenith distances. Clarke derived the coefficient k (refraction at one station divided by the arc subtended at the earth's centre) from 31 coefficients on 16 lines with levelled heights (mean 0.0792) and 112 reciprocal pairs, and adopted k = 0.0809 for rays crossing the sea and 0.0750 for rays not crossing the sea. He states that short rays near the ground give at least 0.1000 with large uncertainty, that refraction is large in the early morning and evening, and that no vertical observations should be taken early in the morning.

Horizontal angles corrected for refraction: no

No refraction term in the reduction of horizontal angles. Bad conditions were met by noting atmospheric circumstances against each observation and by not observing under clearly unfavourable conditions; all observations were given equal weight unless the observer rejected them.

Observations are given equal weight unless rejected by the observer; they are seldom taken under decidedly unfavourable conditions.
“All observations, when received in the office, are considered of equal weight, unless a remark of the observer against any observation condemns it as to be rejected. Observations under favourable circumstances are doubtless more valuable than observations under less favourable circumstances; but how to assign their relative numerical value is a question that admits of no general solution. Observations are seldom taken under decidedly unfavourable circumstances, if it can be avoided.”
Clarke (1858), p. 50 · text layer
Clarke, A. R. (1858). Ordnance trigonometrical survey of Great Britain and Ireland: Account of the observations and calculations of the principal triangulation. London: Eyre and Spottiswoode.
Abbreviations in the observation book record atmospheric conditions such as very hazy and much motion.
“This, together with some other similar abbreviations, as (m.m.) (f.) are used to denote any atmospheric circumstance peculiar to the observation, as very hazy, much motion, faint.”
Clarke (1858), p. 51 (note) · text layer
Clarke, A. R. (1858). Ordnance trigonometrical survey of Great Britain and Ireland: Account of the observations and calculations of the principal triangulation. London: Eyre and Spottiswoode.

Vertical angles and heights

Zenith distances were read with the horizontal angles, to the top of the pile, the ground, or a heliostat; telescope height assumed 5.5 ft where not recorded. k was found from zenith distances to levelled points (Z - z = k v) and from reciprocal pairs (1 - 2k = (z + z' - 180°)/v), weighted, and heights then solved by least squares. Clarke treats heights by vertical angles as secondary in the principal triangulation.

Heights by vertical angles are secondary; k is so variable that at great distances no average can be fully relied on.
“The co-efficient of refraction is a quantity so variable, that at great distances the average of any number of observations, however large, cannot be entirely relied upon.”
Clarke (1858), p. 509
Clarke, A. R. (1858). Ordnance trigonometrical survey of Great Britain and Ireland: Account of the observations and calculations of the principal triangulation. London: Eyre and Spottiswoode.
Reciprocal zenith distances in the Ordnance Survey are not simultaneous.
“The precision of the results is very much increased by simultaneous and reciprocal zenith distances. This case, however, does not exist in the Ordnance Survey, for the observations, though generally reciprocal, are not simultaneous.”
Clarke (1858), p. 509
Clarke, A. R. (1858). Ordnance trigonometrical survey of Great Britain and Ireland: Account of the observations and calculations of the principal triangulation. London: Eyre and Spottiswoode.
Uncertainty in k grows with distance; an error of .02 in k gives 5.1 ft at 14 miles and 127.4 ft at 70 miles.
“for the uncertainty of refraction being as the distance, the uncertainty of difference of height is as the square of the distance.”
Clarke (1858), p. 509
Clarke, A. R. (1858). Ordnance trigonometrical survey of Great Britain and Ireland: Account of the observations and calculations of the principal triangulation. London: Eyre and Spottiswoode.
Daily variation: refraction greater by night; early-morning zenith distances are smaller; no vertical observations should be taken early in the morning; Hart Fell showed double refraction from 1 to 3 p.m. in September.
“When observations are recorded as having been taken at some early hour in the morning, as 5 A.M., and from that time until 8 A.M., they almost invariably show a smaller zenith distance than during any other part of the day; sometimes the difference is very considerable, and shows that no vertical observations should be taken early in the morning.”
Clarke (1858), p. 511
Clarke, A. R. (1858). Ordnance trigonometrical survey of Great Britain and Ireland: Account of the observations and calculations of the principal triangulation. London: Eyre and Spottiswoode.
Range of k from .50 to .00 and negative; smaller altitude gives larger k; large values at the Lough Foyle base.
“The co-efficient of refraction has been observed of all magnitudes from ·50 to ·00, and even negative refraction has been observed in a few instances. The smaller the altitude the larger, generally, will be the mean co-efficient of refraction.”
Clarke (1858), p. 512
Clarke, A. R. (1858). Ordnance trigonometrical survey of Great Britain and Ireland: Account of the observations and calculations of the principal triangulation. London: Eyre and Spottiswoode.
Introduction: in a normal atmosphere refraction varied from 1/15.4 to 1/9.4 of the arc, mean 1/12.6; it reaches 1/2 in some cases, is absent in others, and was negative in a few.
“The refraction was found to vary in a normal state of the atmosphere from 1/15·4 to 1/9·4 of the angles subtended by the Stations from the centre of the Earth; the mean refraction is 1/12·6, but we have cases in which the refraction reaches 1/2, and others in which there is no refraction, and a few in which the refraction was actually negative.”
Clarke (1858), pp. xv-xvi
Clarke, A. R. (1858). Ordnance trigonometrical survey of Great Britain and Ireland: Account of the observations and calculations of the principal triangulation. London: Eyre and Spottiswoode.
Introduction: morning and evening refraction attributed to aqueous vapour near the ground.
“The great amount of refraction in the morning, its diminution towards the middle of the day, and its increase again towards evening, is obviously caused by the greater amount of aqueous vapour in the lower portion of the atmosphere in the morning and evening, as compared with the amount in the middle of the day.”
Clarke (1858), p. xvi · text layer
Clarke, A. R. (1858). Ordnance trigonometrical survey of Great Britain and Ireland: Account of the observations and calculations of the principal triangulation. London: Eyre and Spottiswoode.
Using the difference of reciprocal zenith distances (eliminating k) reconciled Mamsuil and Ben Lawers (124 ft) and Ben Lawers and Ben Macdui (311 ft).
“If, however, we calculate the difference of height by means of the difference of the observed zenith distances, instead of using a value of the coefficient of refraction, we get 124 feet, which agrees satisfactorily with the heights we have assigned.”
Clarke (1858), p. 555 · text layer
Clarke, A. R. (1858). Ordnance trigonometrical survey of Great Britain and Ireland: Account of the observations and calculations of the principal triangulation. London: Eyre and Spottiswoode.
Corryhabbie gave heights over 100 ft wrong from 80 miles; Clarke relates the unusual refraction to the extensive flat top of the hill.
“This extraordinary refraction may perhaps be satisfactorily explained by the circumstance that the top of the hill is an extensive flat.”
Clarke (1858), p. 556 · text layer
Clarke, A. R. (1858). Ordnance trigonometrical survey of Great Britain and Ireland: Account of the observations and calculations of the principal triangulation. London: Eyre and Spottiswoode.

Observing rules

Vertical observations taken to pile top, ground or heliostat; heliostat observations more regular; 5.5 ft assumed telescope height.
“The observations to a heliostat are generally more regular than the observations to the ground at the same point, the former being a better object for bisection. In some cases the height of the observing telescope above the ground has been omitted to be recorded, and consequently a mean height of 5.5 feet has been assumed.”
Clarke (1858), p. 510 · text layer
Clarke, A. R. (1858). Ordnance trigonometrical survey of Great Britain and Ireland: Account of the observations and calculations of the principal triangulation. London: Eyre and Spottiswoode.
Heliostats reflect the sun to distant stations; sizes 5 inch to 20 by 16 inch.
“Heliostats are used for reflecting the sun's rays to distant stations.”
Clarke (1858), pp. 46-47 · text layer
Clarke, A. R. (1858). Ordnance trigonometrical survey of Great Britain and Ireland: Account of the observations and calculations of the principal triangulation. London: Eyre and Spottiswoode.
A watch is kept for distant heliostats, with money rewards graded by distance.
“A watch is always kept in the camp for distant heliostats.”
Clarke (1858), p. 52 · text layer
Clarke, A. R. (1858). Ordnance trigonometrical survey of Great Britain and Ireland: Account of the observations and calculations of the principal triangulation. London: Eyre and Spottiswoode.
A lamp is placed behind the referring object for azimuth observations.
“In these observations a lamp is placed behind the referring-object.”
Clarke (1858), p. 49 · text layer
Clarke, A. R. (1858). Ordnance trigonometrical survey of Great Britain and Ireland: Account of the observations and calculations of the principal triangulation. London: Eyre and Spottiswoode.
Observers were given approximate depressions so they could judge whether intervening ground cut off a low or distant point.
“the approximate depression is also given to the observer, so that on a fine day he may be able, by the observed depression of the furthest ground visible in the direction of the given point, to ascertain whether it is entirely cut off by this intervening ground, or whether it may not under peculiar atmospheric circumstances be visible.”
Clarke (1858), p. 52 · text layer
Clarke, A. R. (1858). Ordnance trigonometrical survey of Great Britain and Ireland: Account of the observations and calculations of the principal triangulation. London: Eyre and Spottiswoode.
Ben Nevis: unusually calm atmosphere for about a fortnight during the observations.
“the state of the atmosphere was unusually calm; so much so, that a lighted candle could often be carried from the tents of the men to the observatory”
Clarke (1858), p. 543
Clarke, A. R. (1858). Ordnance trigonometrical survey of Great Britain and Ireland: Account of the observations and calculations of the principal triangulation. London: Eyre and Spottiswoode.

Recommended practice

When heights matter, stations must be close together.
“When the determination of altitudes becomes a matter of primary importance, the stations must be within a short distance of each other, or no very consistent or accurate results can be obtained”
Clarke (1858), p. 509
Clarke, A. R. (1858). Ordnance trigonometrical survey of Great Britain and Ireland: Account of the observations and calculations of the principal triangulation. London: Eyre and Spottiswoode.
No vertical observations should be taken early in the morning.
“shows that no vertical observations should be taken early in the morning.”
Clarke (1858), p. 511
Clarke, A. R. (1858). Ordnance trigonometrical survey of Great Britain and Ireland: Account of the observations and calculations of the principal triangulation. London: Eyre and Spottiswoode.
Combine k values with Bessel's weight and solve heights by least squares; keep levelled-height values separate from reciprocal ones.
“but such values should be combined with similar, and kept separate from the determinations arising from reciprocal azimuths.”
Clarke (1858), p. 513 · text layer
Clarke, A. R. (1858). Ordnance trigonometrical survey of Great Britain and Ireland: Account of the observations and calculations of the principal triangulation. London: Eyre and Spottiswoode.
Shorter distances reduce the effect of refraction error on heights.
“Fortunately, however, the shorter the distance the less will the resulting altitudes be affected by error in the assumed refraction.”
Clarke (1858), p. 550
Clarke, A. R. (1858). Ordnance trigonometrical survey of Great Britain and Ireland: Account of the observations and calculations of the principal triangulation. London: Eyre and Spottiswoode.
Clarke quotes Hossard's Angoulême series (1844): refraction greatest at daybreak, nearly constant from 10 a.m. to 4 p.m.
“from 10 A.M to 4 P.M. the curve remains nearly parallel to the axis of abscissas, or the refraction is nearly constant”
Clarke (1858), p. 511 (note)
Clarke, A. R. (1858). Ordnance trigonometrical survey of Great Britain and Ireland: Account of the observations and calculations of the principal triangulation. London: Eyre and Spottiswoode.
Preface (H. James): Seul Vein judged unsuitable for attraction work partly because of expected irregular refraction.
“as I fear we should have great irregularity in the refraction if observations were taken near it”
Clarke (1858), p. xvii · text layer
Clarke, A. R. (1858). Ordnance trigonometrical survey of Great Britain and Ireland: Account of the observations and calculations of the principal triangulation. London: Eyre and Spottiswoode.
