European arc of the parallel in 52 N, Greenwich (Feaghmain) to Warschau

Europe · 1825-1893 (national triangulations compiled by Helmert 1893 and Börsch and Krüger 1896)

These volumes compile and re-adjust national triangulations; they do not apply a refraction correction to horizontal angles. Lateral refraction is named as an error source: by Helmert for systematic direction errors in general (p. 35) and for the Rhenish and Hessian nets of the Prussian Geodetic Institute (p. 97), and as a reason to lower the weight of one French channel-net angle at Coldham (p. 60). For the Polish (Russian) chains under Tenner, 18 to 24 sets were to be taken in unfavourable air and strong wind (p. 29). Zenith distances enter only for reducing the French repeating-circle oblique angles to the horizon, and Helmert calls their inexact knowledge an error source not to be underestimated (p. 58). No terrestrial refraction coefficient and no trigonometric heights are given.

Horizontal angles corrected for refraction: no

No correction for lateral refraction is applied. It is named as an error source; at Coldham it is one reason for a lower weight of one angle of the French channel net. For the Polish chains more sets were to be taken in unfavourable air and strong wind.

Polish (Russian) triangulation under General Tenner, chains to Warschau: the closures of all 219 Polish triangles give a mean angle error of +-0.73". Tenner found this error somewhat high; the high signals and the measurement in unfavourable air are given as the explanation. In unfavourable air and strong wind 18 to 24 sets were to be taken.
“Als Erklärungsgrund werden die hohen Signale, welche meist nothwendig waren, sowie die mehrfach nicht zu vermeidende Messung bei ungünstiger Luft angeführt. (Bei ungünstiger Luft und starkem Wind sollten 18—24 Sätze genommen werden.)”
In English: “The high signals, which were mostly necessary, and the measurement in unfavourable air, which several times could not be avoided, are given as the explanation. (In unfavourable air and strong wind 18 to 24 sets were to be taken.)”
Helmert (1893), p. 29
Helmert, F. R. (Ed.). (1893). Die europäische Längengradmessung in 52 Grad Breite von Greenwich bis Warschau. I. Heft: Hauptdreiecke und Grundlinienanschlüsse von England bis Polen. P. Stankiewicz.
Helmert lists lateral refraction of the light rays in the air among the constant error sources of a direction, which act systematically when they change from group to group of the angle measurements of a station.
“ferner zeitliche Veränderungen in der Lage der Stationen aus den verschiedensten Gründen und seitliche Brechungen der Lichtstrahlen in der Luft. Wenn diese Fehler aber unter Umständen bei verschiedenen Gruppen der Winkelmessungen einer Station in von Gruppe zu Gruppe wechselnder Weise wirken, so ist ihr Einfluss ein systematischer.”
In English: “further, changes with time in the position of the stations for the most different reasons, and lateral refractions of the light rays in the air. But if these errors act, in some cases, in a way that changes from group to group of the angle measurements of a station, their influence is a systematic one.”
Helmert (1893), p. 35
Helmert, F. R. (Ed.). (1893). Die europäische Längengradmessung in 52 Grad Breite von Greenwich bis Warschau. I. Heft: Hauptdreiecke und Grundlinienanschlüsse von England bis Polen. P. Stankiewicz.
Large differences between measurements of different years at the same station: Helmert names a difference in the state of the air as one possible cause, and is inclined to give a large share of v2 to the personal perception of the heliotrope lights and to their appearance, which changes with the atmospheric conditions. For this reason measurements of different years at one station were treated as measurements at different stations in the net adjustment.
“Für diesen Vorgang sprach das Auftreten grosser Differenzen selbst bei den besten Messungen verschiedener Jahre, wobei es allerdings unaufgeklärt geblieben ist, welches die wesentlichste Ursache dieser Unterschiede sein mag, ob die meist vorhandene Verschiedenheit der Instrumente und Beobachter, oder ein Unterschied der Luftzustände, oder in einzelnen Fällen Centrirungsmängel bezw. Nichtidentität der Punkte. Ganz abgesehen von den bekanntlich als Zielpunkte sehr ungünstigen Pyramiden und Thurmspitzen bin ich geneigt, der persönlichen Auffassung der Heliotropenlichter und der mit den atmosphärischen Zuständen wechselnden Erscheinung derselben einen grossen Antheil an v2 zuzuschreiben.”
In English: “This procedure was supported by the occurrence of large differences even in the best measurements of different years, though it has remained unexplained which is the main cause of these differences: the difference of instruments and observers that is mostly present, or a difference in the states of the air, or in single cases faults of centring or non-identity of the points. Quite apart from pyramids and spires, which are known to be very unfavourable targets, I am inclined to give a large share of v2 to the personal perception of the heliotrope lights and to their appearance, which changes with the atmospheric conditions.”
Helmert (1893), p. 37
Helmert, F. R. (Ed.). (1893). Die europäische Längengradmessung in 52 Grad Breite von Greenwich bis Warschau. I. Heft: Hauptdreiecke und Grundlinienanschlüsse von England bis Polen. P. Stankiewicz.
French channel net 1861-62 (Levret, Beaux, Perrier; repeating circles): at Coldham the reciprocal weight q of angle 50.47 was raised from 3 to 5 because of the worsening state of instrument No. 1 and the danger of lateral refraction in the direction of St. Peters. Helmert made this weighting in his new reduction.
“der hieraus folgende Werth 3 wurde wegen des mehr und mehr verschlechterten Zustandes des Instruments und wegen der Gefahr von Lateralrefraktion in Richtung nach St. Peters (p. 55) auf 5 erhöht.”
In English: “the value 3 that follows from this was raised to 5 because of the more and more deteriorated state of the instrument and because of the danger of lateral refraction in the direction of St. Peters (p. 55).”
Helmert (1893), p. 60
Helmert, F. R. (Ed.). (1893). Die europäische Längengradmessung in 52 Grad Breite von Greenwich bis Warschau. I. Heft: Hauptdreiecke und Grundlinienanschlüsse von England bis Polen. P. Stankiewicz.
Rhenish and Hessian nets of the Prussian Geodetic Institute: the mean error of a station-adjusted direction from the net adjustment is nearly +-0.7"; Helmert gives lateral refractions, errors in perceiving the heliotrope lights, centring and above all circle graduation errors as the causes.
“Als Ursachen jener Erscheinung müssen, da fast ausschliesslich Heliotrope als Einstellungsobjekte benutzt sind, nächst Seitenrefraktionen des Lichtes und Auffassungsfehlern der Heliotropenlichter, sowie vielleicht einzelnen Centrirungsunsicherheiten, besonders die Theilungsfehler der Kreise betrachtet werden.”
In English: “As causes of that effect, since heliotropes were used almost exclusively as targets, one must consider, after lateral refractions of the light and errors of perception of the heliotrope lights, and perhaps single centring uncertainties, above all the graduation errors of the circles.”
Helmert (1893), p. 97
Helmert, F. R. (Ed.). (1893). Die europäische Längengradmessung in 52 Grad Breite von Greenwich bis Warschau. I. Heft: Hauptdreiecke und Grundlinienanschlüsse von England bis Polen. P. Stankiewicz.
Station Golmberg, Hanoverian-Saxon chain of the Prussian Landesaufnahme, 1880: an angle difference of 2.61" against 1871 was examined. Helmert notes that the Albertsthurm on Collm, the heliotrope station, was brightly lit, that the heliotrope light must have appeared 9.5" east of the middle of the 31" wide tower image, and that an influence of the tower image on the pointing could be put forward as a possibility, especially as the observations would, as usual, have been made in the afternoon. The 1889 remeasurement confirmed the 1880 result to 0.85", and Helmert concludes that no special error causes need be supposed (pp. 186-187).
“zumal da wie üblich die Beobachtungen des Nachmittags erfolgt sein werden und also die Westseite besonders hervortretend gewesen sein muss; sie würde dem Sinne nach mit den Messungsergebnissen stimmen, indem sie den Winkel Collm—Grossberg verkleinern müsste.”
In English: “especially since, as usual, the observations will have been made in the afternoon and so the west side must have stood out especially; this would agree in sense with the measured results, since it would have to make the angle Collm-Grossberg smaller.”
Helmert (1893), p. 186
Helmert, F. R. (Ed.). (1893). Die europäische Längengradmessung in 52 Grad Breite von Greenwich bis Warschau. I. Heft: Hauptdreiecke und Grundlinienanschlüsse von England bis Polen. P. Stankiewicz.
Same station: in the 1889 remeasurement the first 16 observations were rejected because of poor lights, and the heliotrope lights often hardly came through.
“(Die ersten 16 Beobachtungen sind wegen mangelhafter Lichter verworfen; sie geben 49"74.)”
In English: “(The first 16 observations were rejected because of poor lights; they give 49.74".)”
Helmert (1893), p. 186
Helmert, F. R. (Ed.). (1893). Die europäische Längengradmessung in 52 Grad Breite von Greenwich bis Warschau. I. Heft: Hauptdreiecke und Grundlinienanschlüsse von England bis Polen. P. Stankiewicz.

Vertical angles and heights

No terrestrial refraction coefficient and no height computation. Zenith distances are used only to reduce oblique angles (French repeating circles, channel net 1861-62) to the horizon. The only refraction computation stated is astronomic refraction in a latitude determination (Göttingen).

French channel net: the oblique angles of the repeating circles were reduced to the horizon with the zenith distances. Helmert states that an incomplete knowledge of these zenith distances was a difficulty, and that an inexact knowledge of them is an error source not to be underestimated.
“Nach p. 46 und 47 ist möglichst mit den zur Zeit der betreffenden Tageswinkelmessung stattfindenden Zenitdistanzen reducirt, doch sind in der Publikation die Stunden der Beobachtung nicht mitgetheilt. Uebrigens zeigte sich gelegentlich der Neureduktion, dass eine nicht genaue Kenntnis der Zenitdistanzen eine nicht zu unterschätzende Fehlerquelle bildet.”
In English: “According to pp. 46 and 47 the reduction was made as far as possible with the zenith distances that held at the time of the angle measurement of that day, but the hours of observation are not given in the publication. Moreover, in the new reduction it was found that an inexact knowledge of the zenith distances is a source of error not to be underestimated.”
Helmert (1893), p. 58 (footnote)
Helmert, F. R. (Ed.). (1893). Die europäische Längengradmessung in 52 Grad Breite von Greenwich bis Warschau. I. Heft: Hauptdreiecke und Grundlinienanschlüsse von England bis Polen. P. Stankiewicz.
French channel net, Coldham: the weight of angle 46.48 of 1862 was halved partly because no zenith distances were measured in 1862.
“jedoch ist q anstatt gleich 1 gleich 2 genommen, einestheils um dem bereits erwähnten Zustande des benutzten Instruments Nr. 2 Rechnung zu tragen, sowie auch weil 1862 keine Zenitdistanzen gemessen sind.”
In English: “but q is taken equal to 2 instead of 1, partly to allow for the condition of instrument No. 2 mentioned above, and also because no zenith distances were measured in 1862.”
Helmert (1893), p. 60
Helmert, F. R. (Ed.). (1893). Die europäische Längengradmessung in 52 Grad Breite von Greenwich bis Warschau. I. Heft: Hauptdreiecke und Grundlinienanschlüsse von England bis Polen. P. Stankiewicz.
French channel net, Saint Inglevert: the 1861 results were not used; for the fourth angle a zenith distance is missing.
“für den vierten Winkel fehlt eine Zenitdistanz.”
In English: “for the fourth angle a zenith distance is missing.”
Helmert (1893), p. 61 · text layer
Helmert, F. R. (Ed.). (1893). Die europäische Längengradmessung in 52 Grad Breite von Greenwich bis Warschau. I. Heft: Hauptdreiecke und Grundlinienanschlüsse von England bis Polen. P. Stankiewicz.
Göttingen (Börsch and Krüger): the only refraction calculation stated in Heft 2 is the astronomic refraction for Borgen's latitude from zenith distances of Polaris; his value is 48.35" or 48.31", depending on whether the outdoor or indoor thermometer readings were used.
“je nachdem zur Berechnung der Refraktion die Thermometerangaben im Freien oder im Beobachtungszimmer benutzt wurden”
In English: “depending on whether the thermometer readings in the open or in the observing room were used for the computation of the refraction”
Börsch & Krüger (1896), p. 39 · text layer
Börsch, A., & Krüger, L. (1896). Die europäische Längengradmessung in 52 Grad Breite von Greenwich bis Warschau. II. Heft: Geodätische Linien, Parallelbogen und Lothabweichungen zwischen Feaghmain und Warschau. P. Stankiewicz.

Observing rules

Polish triangulation: pyramids 6 to 20 m high and, in 148 cases, signals 20 to 50 m high.
“Zur Signalisirung dienten in Polen Pyramiden von 6 bis 20 m Höhe und in 148 Fällen Signale von 20 bis 50 m Höhe; nur 6 Kirchthürme waren für die Messungen brauchbar”
In English: “In Poland pyramids 6 to 20 m high served as signals, and in 148 cases signals 20 to 50 m high; only 6 church towers were usable for the measurements”
Helmert (1893), p. 29
Helmert, F. R. (Ed.). (1893). Die europäische Längengradmessung in 52 Grad Breite von Greenwich bis Warschau. I. Heft: Hauptdreiecke und Grundlinienanschlüsse von England bis Polen. P. Stankiewicz.
Rhenish, Hessian and Märkisch-Thuringian nets (Prussian Geodetic Institute): observed by Baeyer's instruction, 36 pointings in 18 sets for each object, with a zero mark in every set.
“Im rheinischen, hessischen und märkisch-thüringischen Netz wurden die Beobachtungen nach einer Instruktion des Generallieutenants Baeyer vom 9.”
In English: “In the Rhenish, Hessian and Märkisch-Thuringian nets the observations were made according to an instruction of Lieutenant General Baeyer of the 9th”
Helmert (1893), p. 20 · text layer
Helmert, F. R. (Ed.). (1893). Die europäische Längengradmessung in 52 Grad Breite von Greenwich bis Warschau. I. Heft: Hauptdreiecke und Grundlinienanschlüsse von England bis Polen. P. Stankiewicz.
Prussian Landesaufnahme main net (Schreiber's method): signals were heliotropes only; points partly on towers, often on stone pillars a few metres high, at five stations on wooden pillars 10 to 23 m high.
“Zur Signalisirung dienten ausschliesslich Heliotrope. Die Punkte befanden sich zum Theil auf Thürmen und Bauwerken, vielfach auf Steinpfeilern von wenigen Metern Höhe, auf Ahlsdorf, Ettersberg, Wurzelberg, Grossberg und Golmberg aber auf Holzpfeilern von 10 bis 23 m Höhe.”
In English: “Heliotropes only served as signals. The points were partly on towers and buildings, often on stone pillars a few metres high, but on Ahlsdorf, Ettersberg, Wurzelberg, Grossberg and Golmberg on wooden pillars 10 to 23 m high.”
Helmert (1893), p. 21 · text layer
Helmert, F. R. (Ed.). (1893). Die europäische Längengradmessung in 52 Grad Breite von Greenwich bis Warschau. I. Heft: Hauptdreiecke und Grundlinienanschlüsse von England bis Polen. P. Stankiewicz.
Silesian chain and Tarnowitz connection: heliotropes for the longer distances, pyramids for the shorter; Struve's method, as a rule 12 sets at 7.5 degree circle intervals.
“Zur Signalisirung dienten bei den grösseren Entfernungen Heliotrope, bei den kleineren in der Nähe der Basis und in den gemeinsamen Dreiecken bei Tarnowitz Pyramiden.”
In English: “For the larger distances heliotropes served as signals, for the smaller ones near the base and in the common triangles at Tarnowitz, pyramids.”
Helmert (1893), p. 24 · text layer
Helmert, F. R. (Ed.). (1893). Die europäische Längengradmessung in 52 Grad Breite von Greenwich bis Warschau. I. Heft: Hauptdreiecke und Grundlinienanschlüsse von England bis Polen. P. Stankiewicz.
Chains Berlin to Thorn (Prussian): instrument mostly on moderately high pillars; in four cases wooden pillars 14 to 26 m high were needed; heliotropes, partly helmet poles and black boards.
“nur in vier Fällen wurden in dem hier in Betracht kommenden Theil Holzpfeiler von 14 bis 26 m erforderlich. Zur Signalisirung dienten Heliotrope, zum Theil auch Helmstangen und bei kurzen Entfernungen schwarze Tafeln”
In English: “only in four cases were wooden pillars 14 to 26 m needed in the part considered here. Heliotropes served as signals, partly also helmet poles and, at short distances, black boards”
Helmert (1893), p. 26 · text layer
Helmert, F. R. (Ed.). (1893). Die europäische Längengradmessung in 52 Grad Breite von Greenwich bis Warschau. I. Heft: Hauptdreiecke und Grundlinienanschlüsse von England bis Polen. P. Stankiewicz.
Earlier English-French connection of 1825: observed at night with a large theodolite on light signals.
“Die zweite Verbindung wurde französischerseits 1825 bewirkt, wobei mit einem grossen Theodolit des Nachts nach Lichtsignalen unter sehr günstigen Umständen beobachtet wurde.”
In English: “The second connection was made on the French side in 1825, observing at night with a large theodolite on light signals under very favourable circumstances.”
Helmert (1893), p. 16 (footnote)
Helmert, F. R. (Ed.). (1893). Die europäische Längengradmessung in 52 Grad Breite von Greenwich bis Warschau. I. Heft: Hauptdreiecke und Grundlinienanschlüsse von England bis Polen. P. Stankiewicz.
French channel net 1861-62: to get sights over the very long distances, Gauss's heliotrope was used, with Silbermann heliostats and simple heliotropes.
“Um die Visuren auf die zum Theil sehr grossen Distanzen zu erzielen, erinnerte man sich des Gaussischen Heliotrops („Jonction des réseaux géod.“ p. 6, 16/22) und wandte Heliostaten von Silbermann sowie einfache Heliotrope zur Signalisirung an.”
In English: “To obtain the sights over the partly very long distances, Gauss's heliotrope was remembered, and Silbermann heliostats and simple heliotropes were used as signals.”
Helmert (1893), pp. 16-17 · text layer
Helmert, F. R. (Ed.). (1893). Die europäische Längengradmessung in 52 Grad Breite von Greenwich bis Warschau. I. Heft: Hauptdreiecke und Grundlinienanschlüsse von England bis Polen. P. Stankiewicz.
French channel net, Harlettes 1862: observed in wind on a 21 m scaffold under very unfavourable conditions; Helmert doubled all reciprocal weights q.
“Die Beobachtungen wurden 1862 von Beaux wegen Wind unter sehr ungünstigen Umständen auf einem 21 m hohen Gerüst ausgeführt, weshalb ich hier alle q verdoppelt habe.”
In English: “The observations were made in 1862 by Beaux, because of wind under very unfavourable conditions, on a 21 m high scaffold, for which reason I have doubled all q here.”
Helmert (1893), p. 62
Helmert, F. R. (Ed.). (1893). Die europäische Längengradmessung in 52 Grad Breite von Greenwich bis Warschau. I. Heft: Hauptdreiecke und Grundlinienanschlüsse von England bis Polen. P. Stankiewicz.
English channel net 1861 (Ordnance Survey): reciprocal weights from the scatter of the observations, which Helmert says takes account also of the particular atmospheric conditions.
“Diese Art der Gewichtsbestimmung erledigt in praktisch bequemer Weise die Frage nach der relativen Güte der Beobachtungen mit den verschiedenen Instrumenten und berücksichtigt dabei zugleich die besonderen atmosphärischen Verhältnisse und die anderen, die Güte der Messungen beeinflussenden Umstände”
In English: “This way of fixing the weights settles in a practically convenient way the question of the relative quality of the observations with the different instruments, and takes account at the same time of the particular atmospheric conditions and the other circumstances that affect the quality of the measurements”
Helmert (1893), p. 52 · text layer
Helmert, F. R. (Ed.). (1893). Die europäische Längengradmessung in 52 Grad Breite von Greenwich bis Warschau. I. Heft: Hauptdreiecke und Grundlinienanschlüsse von England bis Polen. P. Stankiewicz.
Clarke 1867, Kilbeg (Ordnance Survey, Ireland): the north meridian mark had a vertical slit for a lamp to shine through by night; observations with a remark of an unsatisfactory bisection were given half weight.
“those observations to which the observer has appended a remark, indicating a not very satisfactory bisection of the object from whatever cause, are considered as having half weight”
Clarke (1867), p. 4 · text layer
Clarke, A. R. (1867). Determination of the positions of Feaghmain and Haverfordwest, longitude stations on the great European arc of parallel: Being an appendix to the account of the principal triangulation of Great Britain and Ireland. Eyre and Spottiswoode for HMSO.

Recommended practice

Polish triangulation rule: in unfavourable air and strong wind 18 to 24 sets were to be taken (see horizontal).
“(Bei ungünstiger Luft und starkem Wind sollten 18—24 Sätze genommen werden.)”
In English: “(In unfavourable air and strong wind 18 to 24 sets were to be taken.)”
Helmert (1893), p. 29
Helmert, F. R. (Ed.). (1893). Die europäische Längengradmessung in 52 Grad Breite von Greenwich bis Warschau. I. Heft: Hauptdreiecke und Grundlinienanschlüsse von England bis Polen. P. Stankiewicz.

Triangle closures and excess

Spheroidal excess computed from the area and the curvature K/a2 at the mean latitude of the triangle; Legendre's theorem for the sides. No refraction term is stated.
“Zur Berechnung der sphäroidischen Excesse der beobachteten Dreiecke konnte immer die einfache Formel”
In English: “For the computation of the spheroidal excesses of the observed triangles the simple formula could always be used”
Helmert (1893), p. 32 · text layer
Helmert, F. R. (Ed.). (1893). Die europäische Längengradmessung in 52 Grad Breite von Greenwich bis Warschau. I. Heft: Hauptdreiecke und Grundlinienanschlüsse von England bis Polen. P. Stankiewicz.

Not found in the pages read

  • No terrestrial refraction coefficient (Refraktionskoefficient, Strahlenbrechung, Höhenwinkel refraction) in Helmert 1893, Börsch and Krüger 1896 or Clarke 1867 (text layers searched for Refraktion, Refraction, Brechung, Zenitdistanz, Lateral, Seitenrefraktion, atmosph, Luft).
  • No trigonometric heights or reciprocal zenith distance analysis in any of the three volumes.
  • No correction of horizontal angles for lateral refraction.
  • No statement that refraction enters the triangle closures or the spherical excess.
  • No stated observing hours for most nets; the only time-of-day statement is the 'as usual in the afternoon' remark for Golmberg (p. 186) and the night observation of 1825 (p. 16).
  • Clarke 1867: no terrestrial refraction discussion; the volume treats astronomic latitude, longitude and azimuth at Feaghmain, Kilbeg and Haverfordwest.
  • Helmert 1893, publisher's list at the end of the volume (pdf p. 277, unnumbered), names A. Fischer (1882), Der Einfluss der Lateralrefraktion auf das Messen von Horizontwinkeln; that work itself is not among the local files.
Pages read: Helmert (1893): text layer all pages searched; pdf pp. 26-27, 29-39, 42, 45, 47, 62, 67-72, 107, 193-194 read; Börsch & Krüger (1896): text layer all pages searched; pdf p. 49 read; Clarke (1867): text layer all pages searched; pdf pp. 10-11 read

Struve Geodetic Arc (Russo-Scandinavian arc of the meridian, Danube to Fuglenaes)

Europe · 1816-1855

No refraction correction of horizontal angles is stated in the pages read. Struve names lateral refraction, "if it exists", as a possible external disturbance and states that the Baltic arc gives no sign of it (T. I p. 148). Image quality was controlled by measuring in Livonia almost only in the 2-hour afternoon period of calm images, and in Finland by rejecting indistinct or too undulating images; on the low rocks of the Gulf of Bothnia the angle measurement became possible only by using extraordinary vertical refractions. Vertical angles were observed at all stations of the Tenner and Struve sections, and corresponding zenith distances at all Swedish stations from Haparanda to the Norwegian border, for trigonometric levelling. In Finmarken Lindhagen solved a refraction constant together with the heights from mostly non-reciprocal zenith distances; its value is not printed on T. II pp. 479-481, and no numerical refraction coefficient was found in the pages read.

Horizontal angles corrected for refraction: no

No refraction correction of horizontal angles is stated in the pages read. Lateral refraction is discussed as a possible external error; Struve finds no sign of it in the Baltic arc. Unsteady images are handled by observing hours and by rejecting observations.

Baltic arc (Struve, 1822-1827): the probable angle error from the triangle closures (0.392") is larger than that from the agreement of the repeated sets (0.338"). Struve gives the extra part to external disturbances: first the phases of the signals under side illumination, then lateral refraction 'if it exists'. He states that the Baltic arc gives no sign of lateral refraction.
“Parmi les causes des perturbations extérieures, il faut placer en premier lieu les phases des signaux, produites par une illumination latérale, puis la réfraction latérale, si elle existe. On voit par notre résultat que l'effet des phases a été réduit à un minimum, par suite de la forme et la figure exacte des signaux employés. Voyez p. 14. Quant aux réfractions latérales nous osons dire, que les opérations de l'arc Baltique n'en donnent pas le moindre indice.”
In English: “Among the causes of the external disturbances one must place first the phases of the signals, produced by a side illumination, then lateral refraction, if it exists. One sees from our result that the effect of the phases was reduced to a minimum, because of the form and exact shape of the signals used. See p. 14. As for lateral refractions, we dare say that the operations of the Baltic arc do not give the slightest sign of them.”
Struve (1857), T. I, p. 148
Struve, F. G. W. (1857). Arc du méridien de 25° 20' entre le Danube et la Mer Glaciale mesuré depuis 1816 jusqu'en 1855 (Vol. 1). Imprimerie de l'Académie impériale des sciences.
Southern arc: Struve estimates the external disturbances on the angles of Lithuania, Volhynia, Bessarabia and triangles T. 460 and T. 461 as double those in the Livonian angles, mainly from signal phases on the pyramid signals, and adds 4 x 0.0863 = 0.345 to their squared mean errors; the weights 1/omega^2 printed on the page include this.
“Les phases des signaux étant l'agent principal dans ces perturbations, l'effet en a dû être plus fort sur les angles 2) à 6) que sur les 1), à cause de la forme piramidale des signaux construits depuis la Duna jusqu'au Danube.”
In English: “The phases of the signals being the main agent in these disturbances, their effect must have been stronger on the angles 2) to 6) than on 1), because of the pyramid form of the signals built from the Duna to the Danube.”
Struve (1857), T. I, p. 154
Struve, F. G. W. (1857). Arc du méridien de 25° 20' entre le Danube et la Mer Glaciale mesuré depuis 1816 jusqu'en 1855 (Vol. 1). Imprimerie de l'Académie impériale des sciences.
Finland: the instruction made any observation inadmissible if the images were indistinct or too undulating. Struve states that the instruction was followed so strictly in Finland that in only two cases an object is wholly missing from a set.
“Enfin l'instruction fixait, que toute observation était inadmissible, si les images des objets étaient indistinctes ou trop ondulantes.”
In English: “Finally the instruction laid down that any observation was inadmissible if the images of the objects were indistinct or too undulating.”
Struve (1860), T. II, p. 21
Struve, F. G. W. (1860). Arc du méridien de 25° 20' entre le Danube et la Mer Glaciale mesuré depuis 1816 jusqu'en 1855 (Vol. 2). Imprimerie de l'Académie impériale des sciences.
Finland, Gulf of Bothnia between Uleaborg and Tornea: Karl-öe and Ajos are 15 and 12 toises above the sea, Rontti and Ulkogrunni only 1.2 and 3.2 toises, and Woldstedt very often had to use extraordinary refractions to see the stations; at Ulkogrunni he stayed nearly two months.
“M. Woldstedt a dû très-souvent profiter des réfractions extraordinaires pour atteindre son but; aussi voyons nous, dans ses journaux, que pour achever les observations d'une seule de ces stations, Ulkogrunni, il a dû faire un séjour de près de deux mois sur cette île déserte.”
In English: “Mr Woldstedt very often had to take advantage of the extraordinary refractions to reach his aim; we also see in his journals that, to complete the observations of only one of these stations, Ulkogrunni, he had to stay nearly two months on this deserted island.”
Struve (1860), T. II, p. 5
Struve, F. G. W. (1860). Arc du méridien de 25° 20' entre le Danube et la Mer Glaciale mesuré depuis 1816 jusqu'en 1855 (Vol. 2). Imprimerie de l'Académie impériale des sciences.
Same, in the Introduction: on the low rocks of the Gulf of Bothnia the angle measurement became possible only by using extraordinary and very large vertical refractions.
“En effet, la mesure des angles sur plusieurs des rocs (Skär) qui ne s'élèvent que peu de toises au-dessus de l'eau, ne devint possible qu'en faisant usage des réfractions verticales extraordinaires et très-considérables”
In English: “In fact, the measurement of the angles on several of the rocks (Skär), which rise only a few toises above the water, became possible only by making use of the extraordinary and very large vertical refractions”
Struve (1857), T. I, Introduction p. XVII
Struve, F. G. W. (1857). Arc du méridien de 25° 20' entre le Danube et la Mer Glaciale mesuré depuis 1816 jusqu'en 1855 (Vol. 1). Imprimerie de l'Académie impériale des sciences.

Vertical angles and heights

Zenith distances observed at all stations of the Tenner, Struve and Swedish sections for trigonometric levelling. Finland: zenith distances taken in the order of the pointings, with the true solar time of each set, and reduced in the table to the centre, as a rule the signal top; height method and coefficient not stated in the pages read. Finmarken: refraction constant solved with the heights by trial; value not printed in the pages read.

Both sections of the southern arc (Tenner and Struve) observed vertical angles at every trigonometric station to obtain successive level differences. Tenner's trigonometric levelling ran from the Baltic near Polangen to the Black Sea; Struve's from the Duna to the Gulf of Finland and on through Finland to Tornea.
“Dans les deux sections de nos opérations, dirigées par M. de Tenner et par Struve, un soin particulier a été voué à l'observation des angles terrestres verticaux, sur toutes les stations trigonométriques sans exception, pour en déduire les différences de niveau successives.”
In English: “In the two sections of our operations, directed by Mr de Tenner and by Struve, particular care was given to the observation of terrestrial vertical angles at all trigonometric stations without exception, to deduce from them the successive differences of level.”
Struve (1857), T. I, Introduction p. XXI
Struve, F. G. W. (1857). Arc du méridien de 25° 20' entre le Danube et la Mer Glaciale mesuré depuis 1816 jusqu'en 1855 (Vol. 1). Imprimerie de l'Académie impériale des sciences.
Swedish part (Haparanda to the Norwegian border, 40 Swedish miles), from Selander's report quoted by Struve: with Agardh taking part, corresponding zenith distances were observed at all stations, Agardh with a universal instrument and Selander with a 12-inch vertical circle; the number of zenith distances per station was never below 40 to 50, and above 100 at some.
“des observations correspondantes des distances zénithales ont été exécutées sur toutes les stations situées depuis Haparanda jusqu'à la frontière de Norvége”
In English: “corresponding observations of zenith distances were made at all stations from Haparanda to the Norwegian border”
Struve (1857), T. I, Introduction p. XXIV
Struve, F. G. W. (1857). Arc du méridien de 25° 20' entre le Danube et la Mer Glaciale mesuré depuis 1816 jusqu'en 1855 (Vol. 1). Imprimerie de l'Académie impériale des sciences.
Finland: zenith distances were observed in the order of the pointings, so that all values on one line belong to the same true solar time; the zenith place Z from each object should agree within a set if the variation of terrestrial refraction had been uniform during the set. The scatter of Z is used as the accuracy check.
“Les Z qui appartiennent à la même ligne horizontale c.-à-d. à une même mise, auraient dû être identiques, si les observations avaient été d'une exactitude parfaite, et si la variation de la réfraction terrestre avait été uniforme pendant la durée de chaque mise.”
In English: “The Z belonging to the same horizontal line, i.e. to the same set, should have been identical if the observations had been perfectly exact and if the variation of terrestrial refraction had been uniform during each set.”
Struve (1860), T. II, pp. 232-233
Struve, F. G. W. (1860). Arc du méridien de 25° 20' entre le Danube et la Mer Glaciale mesuré depuis 1816 jusqu'en 1855 (Vol. 2). Imprimerie de l'Académie impériale des sciences.
Finland: 693 zenith distances of 335 objects at 84 stations; probable error of a single zenith distance (two positions) +-1.49". No vertical observations on the stations of triangle 225 and on Kemdele and Linnunsilmä.
“Sur les autres 84 stations, depuis 1 à 86 de notre tableau, il y a en tout 693 distances au zénith observées de 335 objets.”
In English: “At the other 84 stations, from 1 to 86 of our table, there are in all 693 zenith distances observed of 335 objects.”
Struve (1860), T. II, p. 233
Struve, F. G. W. (1860). Arc du méridien de 25° 20' entre le Danube et la Mer Glaciale mesuré depuis 1816 jusqu'en 1855 (Vol. 2). Imprimerie de l'Académie impériale des sciences.
Finland: the table gives the zenith distances reduced to the centre, which with few exceptions is the top of the signal (p. 230); the signal top was as a rule 1.7 to 2.0 toises above the ground. Only at three stations (Mäki-päälys, Svartvira, Ristisaari) were the vertical angles reduced to the ground itself.
“Nous voyons que cette hauteur a été d'ordinaire de 1,7 à 2,0 toises, peu de fois au delà de 2,0 toises jusqu'à 3,25 toises; mais trois fois, à Hypen-mäki, Ulkogrunni et à Rontti de 6,68, 6,05 et 5,76 toises.”
In English: “We see that this height was as a rule 1.7 to 2.0 toises, a few times above 2.0 up to 3.25 toises; but three times, at Hypen-mäki, Ulkogrunni and Rontti, 6.68, 6.05 and 5.76 toises.”
Struve (1860), T. II, p. 231
Struve, F. G. W. (1860). Arc du méridien de 25° 20' entre le Danube et la Mer Glaciale mesuré depuis 1816 jusqu'en 1855 (Vol. 2). Imprimerie de l'Académie impériale des sciences.
Finland heights: the starting height is Lovisa, fixed by two geometric levellings from the Gulf of Finland (signal top 22.861 toises); Klemola (Uleaborg base) by geometric levelling from the Gulf of Bothnia; Rontti from a pole set in the water. Station heights h are printed as round toises; the method of computing them from the zenith distances and the refraction coefficient are not stated in the pages read.
“C'est le chiffre de départ pour l'hypsométrie de Finlande.”
In English: “This is the starting figure for the hypsometry of Finland.”
Struve (1860), T. II, p. 227 · text layer
Struve, F. G. W. (1860). Arc du méridien de 25° 20' entre le Danube et la Mer Glaciale mesuré depuis 1816 jusqu'en 1855 (Vol. 2). Imprimerie de l'Académie impériale des sciences.
Finmarken (Norwegian part; observer Klouman; memoir by Lindhagen): trigonometric levelling with the Repsold universal instrument used for the horizontal angles. Lindhagen states that the most favourable times of day were not always used. He says that when reciprocal zenith distances cannot be measured simultaneously, the reciprocal method is still the most favourable if the same, most favourable time of day is chosen for both directions; for the reason given, even this could not be followed regularly.
“so bleibt die Methode der reciproken Vertikalwinkelmessungen, wenn sie auch nicht gleichzeitig sein können, doch immer die vortheilhafteste, wenn nur dieselbe und die günstigste Tageszeit für das Messen der beiden reciproken Zenithdistanzen gewählt werden kann.”
In English: “the method of reciprocal vertical angle measurements, even if they cannot be simultaneous, still remains the most favourable, provided only that the same and the most favourable time of day can be chosen for measuring the two reciprocal zenith distances.”
Struve (1860), T. II, pp. 479-480
Struve, F. G. W. (1860). Arc du méridien de 25° 20' entre le Danube et la Mer Glaciale mesuré depuis 1816 jusqu'en 1855 (Vol. 2). Imprimerie de l'Académie impériale des sciences.
Finmarken: the large heights of the stations made the refraction very constant. Surplus, mostly non-reciprocal, zenith distances gave the material to derive the refraction constant. Barometer and thermometer readings were regularly recorded at the times of the vertical angle measurements and were used, with the higher powers of the angle between the plumb lines, in the main net; not in the connecting net (short distances).
“Indessen bewirkten die beträchtlichen Höhen der Dreieckspunkte, dass die Refraction eine grosse Constanz hatte, und dass somit die Höhenunterschiede der Dreieckspunkte sich aus dem vorhandenen Beobachtungsmaterial mit einer verhältnissmässig recht befriedigenden Genauigkeit ableiten lassen. Eine Menge überzähliger, zur Bestimmung der Höhenunterschiede nicht nothwendiger, und meistens nicht reciprok beobachteter Zenithdistanzen liefern das nöthige Material, um die Refractionsconstante mit hinlänglicher Schärfe abzuleiten”
In English: “However, the considerable heights of the triangle points caused the refraction to be very constant, so that the height differences of the triangle points can be derived from the available observations with a relatively quite satisfactory accuracy. A large number of surplus zenith distances, not needed for the height differences and mostly not observed reciprocally, give the material needed to derive the refraction constant with sufficient sharpness”
Struve (1860), T. II, p. 480
Struve, F. G. W. (1860). Arc du méridien de 25° 20' entre le Danube et la Mer Glaciale mesuré depuis 1816 jusqu'en 1855 (Vol. 2). Imprimerie de l'Académie impériale des sciences.
Finmarken: the refraction constant and the heights were solved together from the condition equations by trial (Tâtonnement), not by a strict adjustment. The height differences are computed with the definitive value of the constant; the value itself is not printed on pp. 479-481. Probable error of a height difference on average +-0.49 toise (p. 481).
“Nachdem die strengen, den beobachteten Höhenunterschieden entsprechenden Bedingungsgleichungen zwischen einer Constante der Refraction, und den Höhen der Dreieckspunkte über dem Ausgangspunkte gebildet waren, wurden durch Tâtonnement die wahrscheinlichsten Werthe der Unbekannten näherungsweise aus diesen Gleichungen ermittelt.”
In English: “After the strict condition equations, corresponding to the observed height differences, between a constant of refraction and the heights of the triangle points above the starting point had been formed, the most probable values of the unknowns were found approximately from these equations by trial.”
Struve (1860), T. II, p. 480
Struve, F. G. W. (1860). Arc du méridien de 25° 20' entre le Danube et la Mer Glaciale mesuré depuis 1816 jusqu'en 1855 (Vol. 2). Imprimerie de l'Académie impériale des sciences.

Observing rules

Livonia (Baltic arc): angles were measured almost only in the 2-hour period of calm images that as a rule occurs each afternoon.
“En Livonie la règle a été strictement observée, de ne jamais faire des mesures que par des images suffisamment distinctes des objets, c'est-à-dire presque exclusivement pendant la période de 2 heures des images calmes, qui d'ordinaire a lieu chaque après-midi (Gradmessung I. p. 88).”
In English: “In Livonia the rule was strictly kept never to measure except with sufficiently distinct images of the objects, that is, almost exclusively during the 2-hour period of calm images that usually occurs each afternoon (Gradmessung I. p. 88).”
Struve (1857), T. I, p. 32
Struve, F. G. W. (1857). Arc du méridien de 25° 20' entre le Danube et la Mer Glaciale mesuré depuis 1816 jusqu'en 1855 (Vol. 1). Imprimerie de l'Académie impériale des sciences.
Baltic arc: measurement stopped before sunset because objects near sunset are always very undulating; in summer the calm of the images falls between 0.5 and 0.8 of the interval from noon to sunset. Signals were painted black and the vertical beam, not the pyramid, was pointed, to avoid phase.
“Mais à cette époque la mesure des angles a déjà cessé, parce que près du coucher du soleil les objets sont toujours très ondulants, le calme des images ayant lieu en été, dans nos climats, entre 0,5 à 0,8 de l'intervalle de temps entre midi et le coucher du soleil.”
In English: “But at that time the measurement of the angles has already stopped, because near sunset the objects are always very undulating, the calm of the images occurring in summer, in our climates, between 0.5 and 0.8 of the interval of time between noon and sunset.”
Struve (1857), T. I, p. 14
Struve, F. G. W. (1857). Arc du méridien de 25° 20' entre le Danube et la Mer Glaciale mesuré depuis 1816 jusqu'en 1855 (Vol. 1). Imprimerie de l'Académie impériale des sciences.
Baltic arc: the heliotrope was used for 27 of 138 rays, for the longest distances or when a signal projected on the ground. Struve gives as reasons against its exclusive use that overcast days were more numerous than clear days, or the most favourable for observation through the longer calm of the images.
“Un usage exclusif de l'héliotrope aurait trop arrêté la marche de l'opération, les jours couverts étant soit plus nombreux que les jours sereins, dans notre climat, soit les plus avantageux pour l'observation, par la plus longue durée du calme des images.”
In English: “An exclusive use of the heliotrope would have held up the progress of the operation too much, the overcast days being either more numerous than the clear days in our climate, or the most favourable for observation, through the longer duration of the calm of the images.”
Struve (1857), T. I, p. 14
Struve, F. G. W. (1857). Arc du méridien de 25° 20' entre le Danube et la Mer Glaciale mesuré depuis 1816 jusqu'en 1855 (Vol. 1). Imprimerie de l'Académie impériale des sciences.
Tenner's signals (Lithuania to the Danube): simple pyramids 3 to 6 toises high, and scaffolds of 11 toises and, with a second storey, 16.3 toises, with the instrument one toise below the signal point.
“Fig. 8 représente un second étage ajouté sur le haut de la charpente Fig. 7, pour produire un échafaud de 15 sajènes = 16,3 toises de haut.”
In English: “Fig. 8 shows a second storey added on top of the frame Fig. 7, to make a scaffold 15 sazhen = 16.3 toises high.”
Struve (1857), T. I, p. 15 · text layer
Struve, F. G. W. (1857). Arc du méridien de 25° 20' entre le Danube et la Mer Glaciale mesuré depuis 1816 jusqu'en 1855 (Vol. 1). Imprimerie de l'Académie impériale des sciences.
Finland: heliotropes were used for 37 of 282 directions, for very distant stations or those seen against dark ground; the Gulf of Bothnia triangles would hardly have succeeded without them, even through fog lying on the sea.
“Aussi la mesure des triangles sur le Golfe Bothnique, depuis Laton-mäki jusqu'à Tornea, n'aurait-elle guères réussi, sans l'application de ce puissant moyen de rendre visibles les stations les plus éloignées, même à travers les brouillards couchés à la surface de la mer.”
In English: “So the measurement of the triangles on the Gulf of Bothnia, from Laton-mäki to Tornea, would hardly have succeeded without the use of this powerful means of making the most distant stations visible, even through the fogs lying on the surface of the sea.”
Struve (1860), T. II, p. 11 · text layer
Struve, F. G. W. (1860). Arc du méridien de 25° 20' entre le Danube et la Mer Glaciale mesuré depuis 1816 jusqu'en 1855 (Vol. 2). Imprimerie de l'Académie impériale des sciences.
Lapland: the heliotrope was not used.
“L'héliotrope n'a pas été employé dans les opérations de Laponie.”
In English: “The heliotrope was not used in the operations of Lapland.”
Struve (1860), T. II, p. 12 · text layer
Struve, F. G. W. (1860). Arc du méridien de 25° 20' entre le Danube et la Mer Glaciale mesuré depuis 1816 jusqu'en 1855 (Vol. 2). Imprimerie de l'Académie impériale des sciences.
Northern arc vertical angle journal (station Zhjara-vaara, 1846): each set of zenith distances carries date, hours, barometer, thermometer, wind and air state; examples include 'air épais', 'objets ondulants'.
“1846. Août 19,20. Depuis 22h jusqu'à 1h. β = 686,4 à 686,45 millim.; τ = + 10,0 à + 12,9 R. Vent SO fort. Air épais. Objets ondulants.”
In English: “1846. August 19-20. From 22h to 1h. β = 686.4 to 686.45 mm; τ = +10.0 to +12.9 R. Wind SW strong. Air thick. Objects undulating.”
Struve (1860), T. II, p. 380 (station Zhjara-vaara)
Struve, F. G. W. (1860). Arc du méridien de 25° 20' entre le Danube et la Mer Glaciale mesuré depuis 1816 jusqu'en 1855 (Vol. 2). Imprimerie de l'Académie impériale des sciences.
Finmarken: the observer, Klouman, treated the levelling as secondary, so the most favourable times of day were not always used for vertical angles.
“Diesem Umstande ist es zuzuschreiben, dass nicht immer die vortheilhaftesten Tageszeiten für Messungen vertikaler Winkel benutzt sind”
In English: “To this circumstance it is due that the most favourable times of day were not always used for measurements of vertical angles”
Struve (1860), T. II, p. 479
Struve, F. G. W. (1860). Arc du méridien de 25° 20' entre le Danube et la Mer Glaciale mesuré depuis 1816 jusqu'en 1855 (Vol. 2). Imprimerie de l'Académie impériale des sciences.

Recommended practice

Rule (Livonia, Baltic arc): measure only with distinct images, in practice in the 2-hour afternoon period of calm images.
“de ne jamais faire des mesures que par des images suffisamment distinctes des objets”
In English: “never to measure except with sufficiently distinct images of the objects”
Struve (1857), T. I, p. 32
Struve, F. G. W. (1857). Arc du méridien de 25° 20' entre le Danube et la Mer Glaciale mesuré depuis 1816 jusqu'en 1855 (Vol. 1). Imprimerie de l'Académie impériale des sciences.
Rule (Finland instruction): reject observations with indistinct or too undulating images.
“toute observation était inadmissible, si les images des objets étaient indistinctes ou trop ondulantes.”
In English: “any observation was inadmissible if the images of the objects were indistinct or too undulating.”
Struve (1860), T. II, p. 21
Struve, F. G. W. (1860). Arc du méridien de 25° 20' entre le Danube et la Mer Glaciale mesuré depuis 1816 jusqu'en 1855 (Vol. 2). Imprimerie de l'Académie impériale des sciences.
Lindhagen (Finmarken): when simultaneous reciprocal zenith distances by two observers, or observation from a point near the middle, are not possible, reciprocal zenith distances at the same, most favourable time of day remain the most favourable method.
“wenn nur dieselbe und die günstigste Tageszeit für das Messen der beiden reciproken Zenithdistanzen gewählt werden kann.”
In English: “provided only that the same and the most favourable time of day can be chosen for measuring the two reciprocal zenith distances.”
Struve (1860), T. II, pp. 479-480
Struve, F. G. W. (1860). Arc du méridien de 25° 20' entre le Danube et la Mer Glaciale mesuré depuis 1816 jusqu'en 1855 (Vol. 2). Imprimerie de l'Académie impériale des sciences.

Triangle closures and excess

The closure scatter exceeds the set-agreement error; Struve assigns the difference (0.0863 in the squared mean error) to external disturbances, signal phases first and lateral refraction if it exists; the weights of the angle classes on T. I p. 154 include this effect. The spherical excess (T. I Chap. VIII) is not read here.
“Mais ces perturbations doivent se prononcer dans la somme des trois angles; et nous trouvons que le carré de l'erreur moyenne d'un angle a éprouvé, par cet effet, une augmentation de 0,3375 — 0,2512 = 0,0863.”
In English: “But these disturbances must show in the sum of the three angles; and we find that the square of the mean error of an angle has received, through this effect, an increase of 0.3375 - 0.2512 = 0.0863.”
Struve (1857), T. I, p. 148
Struve, F. G. W. (1857). Arc du méridien de 25° 20' entre le Danube et la Mer Glaciale mesuré depuis 1816 jusqu'en 1855 (Vol. 1). Imprimerie de l'Académie impériale des sciences.

Not found in the pages read

  • No numerical terrestrial refraction coefficient in the pages read of T. I or T. II (OCR searched for réfraction, refraction, Refraction, coefficient, Constante).
  • Lindhagen's definitive refraction constant for Finmarken: referred to on T. II p. 480 but no value printed on pp. 479-481; pp. 482-488 (connecting net heights) OCR shows no value.
  • Method of computing the Finnish station heights from zenith distances (T. II pp. 211-233 read): not stated.
  • Tenner's trigonometric levelling: only summarised in the Introduction (T. I p. XXI); details are in the Zapiski of the Military Topographic Depot, not among the local files.
  • No observing hours or refraction discussion for Tenner's angle measurements in T. I Chap. IV pages read.
  • T. I Chap. VIII (spherical excess, pp. 77-87) and T. II Chap. XIV were not read; no statement linking refraction to the excess was found.
  • No use of night observations or lamps for the triangulation angles in the pages read.
Pages read: Struve (1857): OCR of table of contents (existing text file) and pdf pp. 29-36, 160-215, 296-316; Struve (1860): OCR of pdf pp. 1-40, 215-240, 330-345, 380-400, 478-496

Russian arc of the parallel of 47.5 N, Kishinev to Astrakhan (New Russia and Privolzhsk triangulations)

Europe · 1849-1858 (field), published 1861 and 1893

No source read corrects horizontal angles for refraction or discusses lateral refraction. For the Privolzhsk part, angles observed in fog at Nomardzhin were remeasured after a closure above 7" (p. 13), and the base discrepancy is put down, as the authors suppose, mostly to the illumination of targets (p. 131). Zenith distances were measured at all first-class points with double pointing in two circle positions, often reciprocally, with barometer, thermometer and image steadiness recorded. Heights were computed by Vronchenko's method from the New Russia survey, which is published in Zapiski XIX (cited in Zapiski L, p. 3; not held); no refraction coefficient is printed in the volumes read. Zapiski L (1893) repeats only the instruments and Struve's method of 12 sets.

Horizontal angles corrected for refraction: no

No refraction correction or discussion. Unreliable angles (fog) were remeasured; the base discrepancy is attributed to illumination of targets.

Privolzhsk triangulation (eastern part of the 47.5 N arc, 1855-1858): triangle no. 64 of the main row closed with an error above 7"; the angles at Nomardzhin were remeasured because they had been observed in foggy weather.
“При вычисленіи главнаго ряда треугольниковъ оказалось, что погрѣшность суммы 3-хъ угловъ въ треугольникѣ № 64 составляла свыше 7", по этому и почтено за необходимость переизмѣрить углы на пунктѣ Номарджинъ, оказавшіеся неблагонадежными, потому, что наблюденія на этомъ пунктѣ произведены въ туманную погоду.”
In English: “In computing the main row of triangles it was found that the error of the sum of the 3 angles in triangle no. 64 was above 7", and for this reason it was thought necessary to remeasure the angles at the point Nomardzhin, which proved unreliable because the observations at this point had been made in foggy weather.”
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861), Zapiski XXII, Otd. II, p. 13
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861). Zapiski Voenno-topograficheskago depo, Chast' XXII, Otd. II: Privolzhskoe trigonometricheskoe izmerenie. Voenno-topograficheskoe depo.
Privolzhsk: the difference between the base length carried through the net and the measured Astrakhan base is put down to the accumulation of small angle errors, most of all, it is supposed, to the illumination of the targets. Refraction is not named.
“происходитъ отъ накопленія весьма малыхъ погрѣшностей, не избѣжныхъ при измѣреніи угловъ, зависящихъ частію отъ неточности визированія наблюдателя, невѣрности дѣленій лимба, не совершенно-точнаго отсчитыванія по ноніусамъ, а наиболѣе, какъ можно полагать, отъ освѣщенія предметовъ.”
In English: “arises from the accumulation of very small errors, unavoidable in the measurement of angles, depending partly on inexact pointing by the observer, incorrect divisions of the circle, not quite exact reading of the verniers, and most of all, as one may suppose, on the illumination of the targets.”
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861), Zapiski XXII, Otd. II, p. 131
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861). Zapiski Voenno-topograficheskago depo, Chast' XXII, Otd. II: Privolzhskoe trigonometricheskoe izmerenie. Voenno-topograficheskoe depo.
Privolzhsk horizontal-angle field journal (1856, Captain Napersnikov): each set records date, hour and visibility, e.g. clear, wind, objects seen very well.
“Ясно, вѣтеръ, предметы видно очень хорошо.”
In English: “Clear, wind, objects seen very well.”
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861), Zapiski XXII, Otd. II, p. 23 · text layer
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861). Zapiski Voenno-topograficheskago depo, Chast' XXII, Otd. II: Privolzhskoe trigonometricheskoe izmerenie. Voenno-topograficheskoe depo.
Zapiski L (1893) states for all rows of the 47.5 N arc only the instrument and Struve's method with not fewer than 12 sets (6 in side rows); no refraction discussion.
“Углы треугольниковъ этого ряда измѣрялись универсальнымъ инструментомъ Эртеля, по способу В. Струве не менѣе, какъ 12-ю пріемами.”
In English: “The angles of the triangles of this row were measured with Ertel's universal instrument, by W. Struve's method, with not fewer than 12 sets.”
Voenno-topograficheskii otdel Glavnago shtaba. (1893), Zapiski L, Otd. I, p. 8 · text layer
Voenno-topograficheskii otdel Glavnago shtaba. (1893). Zapiski Voenno-topograficheskago otdela Glavnago shtaba, Chast' L: Iuzhno-russkoe gradusnoe izmerenie dugi paralleli 47 1/2° severnoi shiroty ot g. Kishineva do g. Astrakhani. Chast' II: Raboty geodezicheskiia. Voennaia tipografiia.

Vertical angles and heights

Reciprocal zenith distances at first-class points, double pointing in two circle positions, barometer and thermometer recorded. Heights by Vronchenko's method (Zapiski XIX); coefficient not printed in the volumes read.

Privolzhsk: zenith distances were measured at all first-class points, and at second-class points only in the Sarpinsky row.
“Зенитныя разстоянія измѣрены на всѣхъ первоклассныхъ точкахъ, а на второклассныхъ только въ Сарпинскомъ ряду.”
In English: “Zenith distances were measured at all first-class points, and at second-class points only in the Sarpinsky row.”
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861), Zapiski XXII, Otd. II, p. 20
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861). Zapiski Voenno-topograficheskago depo, Chast' XXII, Otd. II: Privolzhskoe trigonometricheskoe izmerenie. Voenno-topograficheskoe depo.
Privolzhsk: zenith distances for the height differences were measured with a double pointing on the objects and in two positions of the vertical circle.
“Зенитныя разстоянія, для опредѣленія разностей высотъ пунктовъ, измѣрялись двойнымъ наведеніемъ на предметы и въ двухъ различныхъ положеніяхъ вертикальнаго круга.”
In English: “The zenith distances, for determining the height differences of the points, were measured with a double pointing on the objects and in two different positions of the vertical circle.”
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861), Zapiski XXII, Otd. II, p. 22
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861). Zapiski Voenno-topograficheskago depo, Chast' XXII, Otd. II: Privolzhskoe trigonometricheskoe izmerenie. Voenno-topograficheskoe depo.
Privolzhsk: heights above the sea were computed by the method adopted by General Vronchenko in the New Russia survey, starting from Popovka and Kadamovka; zenith distances corrected for the level readings were used. The method and any refraction coefficient are not given in the volumes read. The New Russia survey is published in Zapiski XIX, Otd. II (cited in Zapiski L, Otd. I, p. 3), which is not among the local files.
“Возвышеніе пунктовъ надъ моремъ исчислялось по методѣ, принятой Генераломъ Вронченко при его Новороссійскомъ измѣреніи.”
In English: “The elevation of the points above the sea was computed by the method adopted by General Vronchenko in his New Russia survey.”
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861), Zapiski XXII, Otd. II, p. 131
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861). Zapiski Voenno-topograficheskago depo, Chast' XXII, Otd. II: Privolzhskoe trigonometricheskoe izmerenie. Voenno-topograficheskoe depo.
Privolzhsk Table B (heights): for each point the zenith distance measured from the point to the neighbour and the reciprocal one from the neighbour, the elevation from each, and their mean. No refraction column is described.
“Зенитное разстояніе, измѣренное съ пункта опредѣляемаго на пунктъ, котораго номеръ выставленъ въ столбцѣ третьемъ.”
In English: “Zenith distance measured from the point being determined to the point whose number is given in the third column.”
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861), Zapiski XXII, Otd. II, p. 165
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861). Zapiski Voenno-topograficheskago depo, Chast' XXII, Otd. II: Privolzhskoe trigonometricheskoe izmerenie. Voenno-topograficheskoe depo.
Privolzhsk zenith-distance field journal (1855, Staff Captain Bubnov): Svetlyi Yar to Sarepta, 19 September at 3 3/4 p.m., clear, object steady, with barometer and thermometer.
“Наблюденіе на Сарепту — (вершина пирамиды). Сентября 19 въ 3¾ часа пополудни. Ясно, предметъ спокоенъ. Баром. = 674, 2. Терм. = + 21°, 4 Р.”
In English: “Observation on Sarepta (top of the pyramid). September 19 at 3 3/4 p.m. Clear, object steady. Barom. = 674.2. Therm. = +21.4 R.”
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861), Zapiski XXII, Otd. II, p. 24
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861). Zapiski Voenno-topograficheskago depo, Chast' XXII, Otd. II: Privolzhskoe trigonometricheskoe izmerenie. Voenno-topograficheskoe depo.
Same journal: Kopanskaya bell tower, 9 October at 2 1/2 p.m., clear, object did not oscillate.
“Октября 9, въ 2½ часа пополудни. Верхній край окна верхняго этажа колокольни, въ станицѣ Ветланинской. Ясно, предметъ (не колебался) спокоенъ.”
In English: “October 9, at 2 1/2 p.m. Upper edge of the window of the upper storey of the bell tower in the stanitsa Vetlaninskaya. Clear, object (did not oscillate) steady.”
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861), Zapiski XXII, Otd. II, p. 24
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861). Zapiski Voenno-topograficheskago depo, Chast' XXII, Otd. II: Privolzhskoe trigonometricheskoe izmerenie. Voenno-topograficheskoe depo.
Sarpinsky second-class row: errors found in the computation, above all in the zenith distances, led to remeasurement in 1858; the instruction prescribed remeasuring angles and zenith distances and finding the elevation of the river Manych by corresponding zenith distances from two points to a mark on the bank.
“предписано опредѣлить возвышенія рѣки Манычъ соотвѣтственными зенитными разстояніями съ двухъ пунктовъ, посредствомъ выставленной на берегу марки.”
In English: “it was prescribed to determine the elevations of the river Manych by corresponding zenith distances from two points, by means of a mark set up on the bank.”
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861), Zapiski XXII, Otd. II, p. 18 · text layer
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861). Zapiski Voenno-topograficheskago depo, Chast' XXII, Otd. II: Privolzhskoe trigonometricheskoe izmerenie. Voenno-topograficheskoe depo.
Zapiski XLIX (astronomic): latitudes at the north end of the Bagai (Vol'sk) base, 1860, used zenith distances corrected for refraction taken from W. Struve's tables. This is astronomic, not terrestrial, refraction.
“При вычисленіи наблюденій, зенитныя разстоянія исправлялись за наклонность и рефракцію, которая заимствовалась изъ таблицъ В. Струве.”
In English: “In computing the observations, the zenith distances were corrected for inclination and for refraction, which was taken from W. Struve's tables.”
Voenno-topograficheskii otdel Glavnago shtaba. (1893), Zapiski XLIX, p. 241
Voenno-topograficheskii otdel Glavnago shtaba. (1893). Zapiski Voenno-topograficheskago otdela Glavnago shtaba, Chast' XLIX: Iuzhno-russkoe gradusnoe izmerenie dugi paralleli 47 1/2° severnoi shiroty. Chast' I: Raboty astronomicheskiia. Voennaia tipografiia.

Observing rules

Privolzhsk: for zenith distances a pole of known length was sometimes fixed vertically to the top of the pyramid, or a mound 2 to 3 arshin high was built under the pyramid for the instrument.
“При измѣреніи зенитныхъ разстояній, прикрѣплялась иногда къ головкѣ пирамиды извѣстной длины вѣха—вертикально, или же дѣлалась подъ пирамидою насыпь, вышиною въ 2, 3 и болѣе аршина, для установки инструмента.”
In English: “In measuring zenith distances, a pole of known length was sometimes fixed vertically to the head of the pyramid, or a mound 2, 3 and more arshin high was made under the pyramid for setting up the instrument.”
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861), Zapiski XXII, Otd. II, p. 22
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861). Zapiski Voenno-topograficheskago depo, Chast' XXII, Otd. II: Privolzhskoe trigonometricheskoe izmerenie. Voenno-topograficheskoe depo.
Privolzhsk: when observing from the pyramid platform the outer pyramid was covered with canvas screens against wind; under small pyramids screens 2 to 3 arshin high of straw or linen were used; the instrument was shaded from the sun by an umbrella.
“Для защиты отъ вѣтра, въ случаѣ если наблюденія производились съ помоста пирамиды, то наружная пирамида обтягивалась съ надлежащихъ сторонъ отъ пола до земли, большими кусками парусины (щитами), подъ малою же дѣлали щиты вышиною отъ 2-хъ до 3-хъ аршинъ, или изъ соломы, или же изъ холста; сверхъ того, отъ солнца закрывался инструментъ обыкновеннымъ зонтикомъ.”
In English: “For protection from wind, if observations were made from the platform of the pyramid, the outer pyramid was covered on the proper sides from the floor to the ground with large pieces of sailcloth (screens); under a small pyramid screens 2 to 3 arshin high were made of straw or linen; in addition the instrument was shaded from the sun by an ordinary umbrella.”
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861), Zapiski XXII, Otd. II, p. 22
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861). Zapiski Voenno-topograficheskago depo, Chast' XXII, Otd. II: Privolzhskoe trigonometricheskoe izmerenie. Voenno-topograficheskoe depo.
Sarpinsky second-class row: points marked by poles 2, 3 and sometimes more sazhen high.
“для означенія вершинъ треугольниковъ выставлялись большею частію вѣхи, вышиною въ 2, 3, а иногда и болѣе саженъ.”
In English: “for marking the vertices of the triangles poles were mostly set up, 2, 3 and sometimes more sazhen high.”
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861), Zapiski XXII, Otd. II, p. 22
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861). Zapiski Voenno-topograficheskago depo, Chast' XXII, Otd. II: Privolzhskoe trigonometricheskoe izmerenie. Voenno-topograficheskoe depo.
Main row: not fewer than 12 sets per angle by Struve's method (6 in the Kizlyar row); 32 large and 87 small pyramids.
“дѣлая для каждаго угла въ Главномъ и Красноярскомъ ряду не менѣе 12 пріемовъ, а въ Кизлярскомъ не менѣе 6.”
In English: “making for each angle in the Main and Krasnoyarsk rows not fewer than 12 sets, and in the Kizlyar row not fewer than 6.”
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861), Zapiski XXII, Otd. II, p. 19 · text layer
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861). Zapiski Voenno-topograficheskago depo, Chast' XXII, Otd. II: Privolzhskoe trigonometricheskoe izmerenie. Voenno-topograficheskoe depo.

Triangle closures and excess

Privolzhsk Table A prints spherical angles with the spherical excess beneath; Zapiski L prints the closure 'попр.' = 180 + ε - Σ. No refraction term in the excess or closures is stated.
“Сферическіе углы (однѣ секунды). Подъ ними сферическое излишество.”
In English: “Spherical angles (seconds only). Under them the spherical excess.”
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861), Zapiski XXII, Otd. II, p. 134 (Table A column 7) · text layer
Voenno-topograficheskoe depo. (1861). Zapiski Voenno-topograficheskago depo, Chast' XXII, Otd. II: Privolzhskoe trigonometricheskoe izmerenie. Voenno-topograficheskoe depo.

Not found in the pages read

  • No terrestrial refraction coefficient in Zapiski XXII, L or XLIX (text layers searched for рефракц, преломлен, коэффиц and OCR variants).
  • No mention of lateral refraction, heliotropes or night lamps in the three volumes.
  • Vronchenko's height method and any coefficient: the New Russia survey is published in Zapiski XIX, Otd. II (cited in Zapiski L, Otd. I, p. 3), which is not among the local files.
  • No observing hours rule; the journal samples show afternoon observations (Zapiski XXII pp. 23-24) only as examples.

Great Arc of India, Lambton's meridional arc (Coromandel coast to Punnae and north to about 18 N)

India · 1802-1820

Lambton gave each principal-station height from reciprocal elevations and depressions over the contained arc, and printed the terrestrial refraction for each pair as a fraction of the contained arc. The printed fractions run from 1/3 to 1/40 of the arc. His first sea-level reduction (Madras base, 1802) allowed 5.5 seconds of refraction on a 3' 9" arc. He did not correct horizontal angles for refraction; he repeated and rejected horizontal angles when haze and vapour made the flags unsteady or dull. Astronomical zenith distances were corrected for refraction in the column 'Refraction, &c.'.

Horizontal angles corrected for refraction: no

No lateral refraction correction is stated. Vapour over the flat ground near the Madras base made angles difficult; angles were repeated, and the flag-staff appeared distinctly only on one clear morning. Lambton's rule was to repeat hazy observations and to reject any angle ten seconds from the others until the three angles of the triangle had been observed.

Thick vapour near the ground at the Madras base from just after daylight to near sunset; angles repeatedly taken; the flag-staff was distinct only on the morning of 7 October.
“It may be necessary to notice here, that there was great difficulty in taking all these angles, on account of the very thick vapour which constantly floated near the surface of the flat where the base line runs, almost immediately after day-light, to very near the time of sun-setting. All the angles, and particularly at the north and south end of the base line, have been repeatedly taken, and the only time when the flag-staff appeared distinctly, was in the morning of the 7th of October,”
Lambton (1808), p. 165
Lambton, W. (1808). An account of the measurement of an arc on the meridian on the coast of Coromandel, and the length of a degree deduced therefrom in the latitude 12° 32'. Asiatic Researches, 8, 137-.
Clear morning removed the unsteadiness caused by vapour on earlier days.
“the morning being perfectly clear, no unsteadiness or uncertainty arose from the effects of the vapour, which had occasioned the difference between the angles on the 24th and 26th.”
Lambton (1808), p. 154 · text layer
Lambton, W. (1808). An account of the measurement of an arc on the meridian on the coast of Coromandel, and the length of a degree deduced therefrom in the latitude 12° 32'. Asiatic Researches, 8, 137-.
Lambton's rule for hazy angles (quoted by Walker): repeat; reject any angle differing by ten seconds until the three angles of the triangle had been observed.
“Difficulty however very frequently arose from the haziness of the weather, which rendered the objects at the very distant points extremely dull, and occasioned some irregularity in the angles. Whenever that happened the observations were often repeated, and in case any one in particular was different from the other so much as ten seconds, it was rejected till the three angles of the triangle had been observed.”
Walker (1870), p. XVIII · text layer
Walker, J. T. (1870). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. I: Standards of measure and base-lines. Dehra Dun: Great Trigonometrical Survey.
Heliotropes and lamps were not used in the survey until after 1832 (introduced by Everest). Before that, signals were masts and flagstaves, and it became the practice to take the field after the first heavy fall of rain, the rainy season being the most favourable for viewing such objects.
“such as heliotropes and lamps, to erect on the stations which he was observing; but such signals were not employed in the operations of the survey until after the year 1832, when they were introduced by Colonel Everest. For very many years the signals were “ masts, flagstaves, and other opaque objects, and then days and days often passed away without a glimpse of the distant object.” As the atmosphere in India is usually most favorable during the rainy season, for viewing such objects, it became the practice to wait for the first heavy fall of rain and then take the field”
Walker (1870), p. XIX
Walker, J. T. (1870). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. I: Standards of measure and base-lines. Dehra Dun: Great Trigonometrical Survey.

Vertical angles and heights

Heights from reciprocal apparent elevations and depressions between principal stations; refraction per pair = half the excess of the contained arc over the sum of the reciprocal depressions (method shown in 1808), printed as a fraction of the contained arc. The 1816 and 1820 tables list stations at / observed, apparent elevations and depressions, contained arc, refraction, height above the sea. Coast checks: the southern chain closed on Punnae with 10.2 ft difference from measured height above low water.

1808 derivation of terrestrial refraction at Madras (race-stand to beach).
“the difference between the depression and elevation being 2' 58", we have (3' 9" - 2' 58")/2 = 5",5 for the terrestrial refraction.”
Lambton (1808), p. 148
Lambton, W. (1808). An account of the measurement of an arc on the meridian on the coast of Coromandel, and the length of a degree deduced therefrom in the latitude 12° 32'. Asiatic Researches, 8, 137-.
Height of north end of Madras base found with allowance for terrestrial refraction.
“the height of the latter was determined by observations made at the race-stand and on the sea-beach, where allowance has been made for the terrestrial refraction.”
Lambton (1808), p. 147 · text layer
Lambton, W. (1808). An account of the measurement of an arc on the meridian on the coast of Coromandel, and the length of a degree deduced therefrom in the latitude 12° 32'. Asiatic Researches, 8, 137-.
1816 appendix table of elevations, depressions, contained arcs, refractions and heights (southern arc).
“CONTAINED Arcs, terrestrial refractions, together with the heights, above the level of the Sea, of the principal stations.”
Lambton (1816), p. 100
Lambton, W. (1816). An account of the measurement of an arc on the meridian extending from latitude 10° 59' 49" to 15° 6' 0" north. Asiatic Researches, 12, 286-356.
Check on the sea at Punnae.
“Punnae Station above the low water mark by measurement, ... 44.8 ... Difference or Error, ... 10.2”
Lambton (1816), p. 101
Lambton, W. (1816). An account of the measurement of an arc on the meridian extending from latitude 10° 59' 49" to 15° 6' 0" north. Asiatic Researches, 12, 286-356.
Heights of every great station in the usual manner by contained arcs; refraction at every station given in Asiatic Researches vol. 10.
“The perpendicular height above the sea of every great station, was determined in the usual manner, by using the contained arcs between two stations, a method so well known, that it is needless to explain it here. An account of those heights, with the terrestrial refraction as observed at every station in going direct from sea to sea, is given in the 10th volume of the Asiatick Researches.”
Lambton (1816), p. 289
Lambton, W. (1816). An account of the measurement of an arc on the meridian extending from latitude 10° 59' 49" to 15° 6' 0" north. Asiatic Researches, 12, 286-356.
1820 table of the same form for the northern section.
“20. Elevations and Depressions, contained Arcs, Terrestrial Refractions, together with the heights above the level of the Sea, of the principal stations.”
Lambton (1820), p. 119
Lambton, W. (1820). [Continuation of the measurement of the meridional arc]. Asiatic Researches, 13.
Lambton (letter of 1814, quoted in Vol. XII): terrestrial refraction varied from 1/4 to 1/20 of the contained arc, which he attributed to moisture; he proposed hygrometer work and a table of low-altitude refractions from observation.
“In the course of my observations during the last ten years, I have at times found the terrestrial refraction to be as much as 1/4 of the contained arc, and occasionally as low as 1/20. This great irregularity I attribute to the different degrees of moisture in the atmosphere at different times.”
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890), p. 37 (Appendix)
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. XII: The Southern Trigon. Dehra Dun: Trigonometrical Branch, Survey of India.

Observing rules

Elevations and depressions taken with the circular instrument from a mean of several observations; collimation removed by reversing.
“The angles of elevation and depression were taken by the circular instrument, from a mean of several observations, and the error of collimation was corrected by turning the transit over, and the horizontal plate half-round. But the weather was rather dull during the whole of these operations.”
Lambton (1808), p. 148
Lambton, W. (1808). An account of the measurement of an arc on the meridian on the coast of Coromandel, and the length of a degree deduced therefrom in the latitude 12° 32'. Asiatic Researches, 8, 137-.
Heliotropes and lamps were not used in the survey until after 1832 (introduced by Everest). Before that, signals were masts and flagstaves, and it became the practice to take the field after the first heavy fall of rain, the rainy season being the most favourable for viewing such objects.
“such as heliotropes and lamps, to erect on the stations which he was observing; but such signals were not employed in the operations of the survey until after the year 1832, when they were introduced by Colonel Everest. For very many years the signals were “ masts, flagstaves, and other opaque objects, and then days and days often passed away without a glimpse of the distant object.” As the atmosphere in India is usually most favorable during the rainy season, for viewing such objects, it became the practice to wait for the first heavy fall of rain and then take the field”
Walker (1870), p. XIX
Walker, J. T. (1870). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. I: Standards of measure and base-lines. Dehra Dun: Great Trigonometrical Survey.
GTS rule from about 1836: vertical angles restricted to the time of minimum refraction, 1 to 3 p.m. apparent time, with back and forward angles at the same interval from noon. Between 1800 and 1836 vertical angles were taken at any hour, often at night.
“During the immediately preceding period of about twenty years the rule had been followed of restricting the observations of vertical angles as closely as possible to the time of minimum refraction, which occurs daily between the hours of 1 and 3 P.M. apparent time ; the back angle was also measured at nearly the same interval from apparent noon as the forward angle. But during the operations of the period between 1800 and 1836 the observations were taken at any hour of the day and often during the night.”
Walker (1870), p. 102
Walker, J. T. (1870). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. I: Standards of measure and base-lines. Dehra Dun: Great Trigonometrical Survey.

Recommended practice

Lambton (letter of 1814, quoted in Vol. XII): terrestrial refraction varied from 1/4 to 1/20 of the contained arc, which he attributed to moisture; he proposed hygrometer work and a table of low-altitude refractions from observation.
“In the course of my observations during the last ten years, I have at times found the terrestrial refraction to be as much as 1/4 of the contained arc, and occasionally as low as 1/20. This great irregularity I attribute to the different degrees of moisture in the atmosphere at different times.”
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890), p. 37 (Appendix)
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. XII: The Southern Trigon. Dehra Dun: Trigonometrical Branch, Survey of India.
Lambton planned an investigation of the laws of terrestrial refraction; it was carried out after his death by Everest and Waugh.
“he had formed a project for the investigation of the laws of terrestrial refraction, and was making arrangements for the determination, by pendulum observations, of the relative force of gravity at the several stations of the Great Arc ... The investigations into the laws of terrestrial refraction were made a few years after his death by Colonel Everest and Captain Waugh.”
Walker (1870), p. XXXII · text layer
Walker, J. T. (1870). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. I: Standards of measure and base-lines. Dehra Dun: Great Trigonometrical Survey.
Warren (Asiatic Researches v10) cites the experiments on terrestrial refraction in Asiatic Researches vol. 9, art. 1: refraction at night was something more than double the daytime value.
“See Asiatic Researches Volume IX. Article 1st, Page 13, the experiments on terrestrial refraction, where the refraction at night was something more than double what it was in the day-time, owing (it is supposed) to the increased moisture of the atmosphere.”
, p. 525 (footnote)
