Lambton, trigonometrical operations across the Peninsula, Fort St. George to Mangalore

India · 1802-1808

Heights of all great stations from coast to coast were found from reciprocal elevations and depressions, and the terrestrial refraction is printed for each pair as a fraction of the contained arc, from 1/7 to 1/40. The coast-to-coast chain from St. Thomas's Mount to the beach near Mangalore closed with 8.6 ft difference from the measured height above low water. Horizontal angles were not corrected for refraction. Zenith distances were corrected from mean refraction tables, with no barometer or thermometer, as it was doubtful what corrections to apply until tables of refraction were made for that climate. Vapour kept the pole star invisible by day, so no double azimuths were taken.

Horizontal angles corrected for refraction: no

No lateral refraction correction stated. Atmospheric vapour limited star observations for azimuth.

Pole star not visible by day because of vapour; no double azimuths.
“It may be remarked here, that no double azimuths have been taken. The pole-star being so low, and the vapour in the atmosphere so great in general, that I have never, except in two instances, been able to discern it while the sun was above the horizon.”
Warren (1811), p. 362 · text layer
Warren, J. (1811). An account of astronomical observations near Fort St. George. Asiatic Researches, 10.
Heliotropes and lamps were not used in the survey until after 1832 (introduced by Everest). Before that, signals were masts and flagstaves, and it became the practice to take the field after the first heavy fall of rain, the rainy season being the most favourable for viewing such objects.
“such as heliotropes and lamps, to erect on the stations which he was observing; but such signals were not employed in the operations of the survey until after the year 1832, when they were introduced by Colonel Everest. For very many years the signals were “ masts, flagstaves, and other opaque objects, and then days and days often passed away without a glimpse of the distant object.” As the atmosphere in India is usually most favorable during the rainy season, for viewing such objects, it became the practice to wait for the first heavy fall of rain and then take the field”
Walker (1870), p. XIX
Walker, J. T. (1870). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. I: Standards of measure and base-lines. Dehra Dun: Great Trigonometrical Survey.

Vertical angles and heights

Reciprocal elevations and depressions, contained arc and refraction (fraction of arc) for each pair; heights carried from 18.7 ft at S. end of the St. Thomas's Mount base to the beach near Mangalore, then compared with levelling from low water. A second table gives stations off the direct line, from Kylasghur.

Table heading.
“ELEVATIONS and DEPRESSIONS, contained Arcs, terrestrial Refractions, together with the heights above the level of sea, of all the principal Stations.”
Warren (1811), p. 382
Warren, J. (1811). An account of astronomical observations near Fort St. George. Asiatic Researches, 10.
Sea-to-sea closure.
“The station on the beach above the low-water mark by measurement ... 14.0 ... Difference or error ... 8.6”
Warren (1811), p. 382
Warren, J. (1811). An account of astronomical observations near Fort St. George. Asiatic Researches, 10.
Text summary of the closure and refraction table.
“the ultimate comparisons, of the height of a station on the beach, near Mangalore, as had by computing from this coast, and by measuring from the low water mark on the other, where there appears an error only of 8,5 feet. This table also contains the terrestrial refractions.”
Warren (1811), p. 298 · text layer
Warren, J. (1811). An account of astronomical observations near Fort St. George. Asiatic Researches, 10.
Zenith distances: mean refraction tables, no barometer or thermometer, no height correction.
“The refraction is had from the tables of mean refraction, and no notice taken of the barometer or thermometer, or of the heights of the stations above the level of the sea, considering it doubtful what corrections to apply until observations are made, and tables of refraction constructed, for this climate, and for different elevations.”
Warren (1811), p. 355 · text layer
Warren, J. (1811). An account of astronomical observations near Fort St. George. Asiatic Researches, 10.

Observing rules

Heliotropes and lamps were not used in the survey until after 1832 (introduced by Everest). Before that, signals were masts and flagstaves, and it became the practice to take the field after the first heavy fall of rain, the rainy season being the most favourable for viewing such objects.
“such as heliotropes and lamps, to erect on the stations which he was observing; but such signals were not employed in the operations of the survey until after the year 1832, when they were introduced by Colonel Everest. For very many years the signals were “ masts, flagstaves, and other opaque objects, and then days and days often passed away without a glimpse of the distant object.” As the atmosphere in India is usually most favorable during the rainy season, for viewing such objects, it became the practice to wait for the first heavy fall of rain and then take the field”
Walker (1870), p. XIX
Walker, J. T. (1870). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. I: Standards of measure and base-lines. Dehra Dun: Great Trigonometrical Survey.

Recommended practice

Warren: refraction at noon (sun) and at night (stars) may differ materially; a footnote cites experiments on terrestrial refraction (Asiatic Researches vol. 9, art. 1) with night refraction more than double the day value.
“the declination of the sun being deduced from observations taken at noon, and that of the stars at night time, the effects of refraction at these different periods may possibly vary materially, and what is allowed for zenith distances of the stars, be too much for zenith distances of the sun”
Warren (1811), p. 525
Warren, J. (1811). An account of astronomical observations near Fort St. George. Asiatic Researches, 10.
Footnote to the same.
“See Asiatic Researches Volume IX. Article 1st, Page 13, the experiments on terrestrial refraction, where the refraction at night was something more than double what it was in the day-time, owing (it is supposed) to the increased moisture of the atmosphere.”
Warren (1811), p. 525 (footnote)
Warren, J. (1811). An account of astronomical observations near Fort St. George. Asiatic Researches, 10.
GTS rule from about 1836: vertical angles restricted to the time of minimum refraction, 1 to 3 p.m. apparent time, with back and forward angles at the same interval from noon. Between 1800 and 1836 vertical angles were taken at any hour, often at night.
“During the immediately preceding period of about twenty years the rule had been followed of restricting the observations of vertical angles as closely as possible to the time of minimum refraction, which occurs daily between the hours of 1 and 3 P.M. apparent time ; the back angle was also measured at nearly the same interval from apparent noon as the forward angle. But during the operations of the period between 1800 and 1836 the observations were taken at any hour of the day and often during the night.”
Walker (1870), p. 102
Walker, J. T. (1870). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. I: Standards of measure and base-lines. Dehra Dun: Great Trigonometrical Survey.

Not found in the pages read

  • No lateral refraction correction or discussion for horizontal angles.
  • No hour-of-observation rule for vertical angles.
  • Lambton (1807) vol. 9 refraction experiments not in local files.
  • Text says 8,5 ft; the table prints 8.6 ft (both recorded as printed).

GTS Karachi Longitudinal Series (North-West Quadrilateral)

India · 1849-1853

Heights come from reciprocal vertical angles; each pair is printed with the refraction in seconds and in decimals of the contained arc, with signal and instrument heights and mean time of observation. Mean times run from 2 h 32 m to 6 h 43 m, mostly between 3 h and 5 h. Most coefficients are .04 to .07; some are near zero, two exceed .15 (.153, .168), and some refractions are negative, down to -64″ (.119 of the arc). Mirage over the Runn of Cutch made vertical angles to the Runn untrustworthy. No lateral refraction correction to horizontal angles is stated; ray tracing and towers were used in flat country.

Horizontal angles corrected for refraction: no

Not corrected. Flat country needed ray tracing and towers; bad signal lights hindered observing; a 25-day halt at Chotli was due to the atmosphere.

Flat desert: ray tracing, towers, bad signal lights.
“a country is very unfavourable to trigonometrical operations. Ray tracing and clearing check the progress of the approximate work; the necessity of building towers causes further delay and expense, and bad signal lights embarrass the observations.”
Walker (1873), p. XI-B
Walker, J. T. (Ed.). (1873). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. III: The principal triangulation, North-West Quadrilateral. Dehra Dun: Great Trigonometrical Survey.
25-day detention at Chotli from the state of the atmosphere.
“Here a wearisome detention of 25 days took place from the unfavourable state of the atmosphere.”
Walker (1873), p. XII-B · text layer
Walker, J. T. (Ed.). (1873). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. III: The principal triangulation, North-West Quadrilateral. Dehra Dun: Great Trigonometrical Survey.
Heliotropes and lamps were not used in the survey until after 1832 (introduced by Everest). Before that, signals were masts and flagstaves, and it became the practice to take the field after the first heavy fall of rain, the rainy season being the most favourable for viewing such objects.
“such as heliotropes and lamps, to erect on the stations which he was observing; but such signals were not employed in the operations of the survey until after the year 1832, when they were introduced by Colonel Everest. For very many years the signals were “ masts, flagstaves, and other opaque objects, and then days and days often passed away without a glimpse of the distant object.” As the atmosphere in India is usually most favorable during the rainy season, for viewing such objects, it became the practice to wait for the first heavy fall of rain and then take the field”
Walker (1870), p. XIX
Walker, J. T. (1870). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. I: Standards of measure and base-lines. Dehra Dun: Great Trigonometrical Survey.

Vertical angles and heights

Differences of height from reciprocal vertical angles; tables give observed angle, number of observations, contained arc, object and eye correction (signal and instrument heights), terrestrial refraction in seconds and in decimals of contained arc, subtended angle, height difference. Method as in Vol. II, Chapter XIII.

Preface: means of vertical angles, refraction and coefficient printed.
“But the mean of the whole of the measures of each vertical angle, the mean value of the amount of refraction in each angle and of the co-efficient of refraction, the heights of the signal and of the telescope of the observer above the summits of the stations, and the differences of height of the said summits, are given.”
Walker (1873), p. xii · text layer
Walker, J. T. (Ed.). (1873). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. III: The principal triangulation, North-West Quadrilateral. Dehra Dun: Great Trigonometrical Survey.
Mirage over the Runn of Cutch.
“The desert was found perfectly free from mirage at the season it was visited; but the Runn of Cutch, on the southern flank of the series, was greatly affected by this atmospheric phenomenon, which prevented the ascertainment of the height of the Runn with respect to the sea level. ... the vertical angles were so affected by mirage, as to be untrustworthy.”
Walker (1873), p. XI-B
Walker, J. T. (Ed.). (1873). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. III: The principal triangulation, North-West Quadrilateral. Dehra Dun: Great Trigonometrical Survey.

Observing rules

GTS rule from about 1836: vertical angles restricted to the time of minimum refraction, 1 to 3 p.m. apparent time, with back and forward angles at the same interval from noon. Between 1800 and 1836 vertical angles were taken at any hour, often at night.
“During the immediately preceding period of about twenty years the rule had been followed of restricting the observations of vertical angles as closely as possible to the time of minimum refraction, which occurs daily between the hours of 1 and 3 P.M. apparent time ; the back angle was also measured at nearly the same interval from apparent noon as the forward angle. But during the operations of the period between 1800 and 1836 the observations were taken at any hour of the day and often during the night.”
Walker (1870), p. 102
Walker, J. T. (1870). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. I: Standards of measure and base-lines. Dehra Dun: Great Trigonometrical Survey.
Over level country, rays grazing the ground pass through refraction from a low negative to a high positive value during day and night; reciprocal angles were several days apart, so equal refraction at both ends was mistrusted. Spirit levels began in 1856 to control the trigonometrical heights.
“But this method, though susceptible of a high degree of accuracy in a hill region, is beset with many difficulties in a level country; the rays of light, grazing the surface of the ground, pass through various gradations of refraction, from a low negative to a high positive value, during the course of the day and night; the success of the method depends on the reciprocal angles being equally refracted, which probably would not be the case even if the observations were simultaneous ; whereas from the circumstance of there being only one large theodolite with each survey party, there is always an interval of several days between the reciprocating observations, and thus there is all the more reason to mistrust the assumed equality of refraction.”
Walker (1870), p. 102
Walker, J. T. (1870). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. I: Standards of measure and base-lines. Dehra Dun: Great Trigonometrical Survey.
Same volume, Indus Series: fear that rays grazing the plain would suffer large refraction variations led to spirit levelling.
“for it was feared that, with rays grazing the surface of the ground so closely, the vertical angles measured in the triangulation would be so much affected by the very considerable variations of terrestrial refraction to which they would be liable, that a considerable amount of error might be generated in the course of operations carried over so great a distance under such unfavorable circumstances.”
Walker (1873), p. VIII-D (Indus Series)
Walker, J. T. (Ed.). (1873). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. III: The principal triangulation, North-West Quadrilateral. Dehra Dun: Great Trigonometrical Survey.
Same volume, Indus Series: diagonals over flat country graze the ground.
“diagonals are unsuited for operations over a flat country, as the rays of light passing over them are liable to graze the surface of the ground and become obstructed”
Walker (1873), p. VIII-D (Indus Series)
Walker, J. T. (Ed.). (1873). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. III: The principal triangulation, North-West Quadrilateral. Dehra Dun: Great Trigonometrical Survey.

Recommended practice

Vol. XII formula assumes reciprocal angles equally refracted; modern vertical angles are generally measured between 1 and 3 p.m., when refraction is usually a minimum.
“In the preceding formulae it is assumed that the reciprocal angles are equally affected by refraction, and in order that this may be as nearly the case as possible, the vertical angles in all the modern operations are generally measured between the hours of 1 and 3 P.M., when the amount of refraction is usually a minimum.”
, p. 22
Coefficient of refraction from reciprocal vertical angles: phi = 1/2 {C - (Da + Db) + beta}; phi/C is the coefficient (terrestrial refraction in decimals of the contained arc). Selected values are used to reduce unreciprocated angles to secondary points.
“and φ/C gives the terrestrial refraction in decimals of contained arc—or in other words the co-efficient of refraction—for each pair of reciprocated observations. From the several values of the co-efficient thus determined, those which are deemed most suitable are selected for employment in the reduction of vertical angles to secondary points, at which reciprocal observations have not been taken.”
, p. 23

Not found in the pages read

  • No lateral refraction correction to horizontal angles.
  • No explicit hour rule printed in Vol. III for this series (hours appear in the tables only).
  • Vol. II Chapter XIII (method for vertical angles) not in local files.
Pages read: Walker (1873): pdf pp. 18, 66-70, 271-292, 524 (printed xii, IX-B to XII-B, 52-b to 70-b, VIII-D); Walker (1870): pdf pp. 27, 148

GTS Bombay Longitudinal Series (Southern Trigon)

India · 1822-1863

Heights come from reciprocal vertical angles; the volume states the formula, the 1 to 3 p.m. rule for minimum refraction, and the coefficient as phi over the contained arc, used for unreciprocated secondary points. Bombay Longitudinal coefficients on pp. 58-B to 62-B are .030 to .068, mostly .043 to .068, with one negative value (-33″ on a 90″ arc to an auxiliary station, 1885); mean times of observation are 1 h 8 m to 4 h 46 m, mostly 2 h to 3 h. Old heights of nearby meridional series were revised because they were taken outside the time of minimum refraction. Horizontal angles were not corrected; Vol. XII names lateral refraction as the main outside cause of angle error, larger over plains and jungle. Signals were poles and straw fires before 1837, then heliotropes by day and Argand lamps by night.

Horizontal angles corrected for refraction: no

Not corrected. Signals improved from poles and straw fires to heliotropes and lamps (1837). Lateral refraction is discussed as an influence on angle weights.

Early poles could be mistaken in hazy weather; distant points observed by night with straw fires that the wind moved.
“Other objects, such as tall trees nearly in the same direction might in hazy weather be mistaken for them, and in certain situations, such as at long distances or when projected against a dark background, they could not be seen at all. In this last case it became necessary to observe the point by night, and the plan adopted was to send a man to light a large straw fire on the spot, but this was also liable to cause error, as the flame might be driven about by the wind.”
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890), p. IV-B · text layer
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. XII: The Southern Trigon. Dehra Dun: Trigonometrical Branch, Survey of India.
From 1837 heliotropes by day and lamps by night.
“yet by the end of the rains most of them had become tolerably well acquainted with their new duties, and were competent to direct the heliotropes by day as well as the lamps by night. The lamps used were made up on the spot, and consisted of an Argand burner, behind which was placed a reflector made of plate brass ... In clear weather these could be seen at a distance of 40 miles.”
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890), p. VI-B · text layer
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. XII: The Southern Trigon. Dehra Dun: Trigonometrical Branch, Survey of India.
Vol. XII names lateral refraction as the most important outside influence on the weight modulus of horizontal angles; it is greater in plains than hills, and much greater over dense forest and jungle. Freedom from grazing rays depends on station heights above ground.
“for on this condition freedom from grazing rays—and their concomitant lateral refractions—greatly depends. ... and of these there can be little doubt that the most important is lateral refraction, which is liable to be greater in the plains than in the hills, and much greater in tracts of country which are covered with dense forest and jungle, than in tracts which are open and cultivated.”
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890), p. 100 · text layer
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. XII: The Southern Trigon. Dehra Dun: Trigonometrical Branch, Survey of India.
Observed-angle tables mark each single measure with l (lamp) or h (heliotrope) for the signal observed.
“Against each single measure is a letter in italics shewing whether the signal observed to was l, a lamp, or h, a heliotrope”
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890), p. 13 · text layer
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. XII: The Southern Trigon. Dehra Dun: Trigonometrical Branch, Survey of India.

Vertical angles and heights

Reciprocal vertical angles reduced by the Vol. XII formula; tables give mean time of observation, observed angle, number of observations, signal and instrument heights, contained arc, refraction in seconds and in decimals of the contained arc, height difference; final heights adjusted to spirit levels where available.

Vol. XII formula assumes reciprocal angles equally refracted; modern vertical angles are generally measured between 1 and 3 p.m., when refraction is usually a minimum.
“In the preceding formulae it is assumed that the reciprocal angles are equally affected by refraction, and in order that this may be as nearly the case as possible, the vertical angles in all the modern operations are generally measured between the hours of 1 and 3 P.M., when the amount of refraction is usually a minimum.”
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890), p. 22
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. XII: The Southern Trigon. Dehra Dun: Trigonometrical Branch, Survey of India.
Coefficient of refraction from reciprocal vertical angles: phi = 1/2 {C - (Da + Db) + beta}; phi/C is the coefficient (terrestrial refraction in decimals of the contained arc). Selected values are used to reduce unreciprocated angles to secondary points.
“and φ/C gives the terrestrial refraction in decimals of contained arc—or in other words the co-efficient of refraction—for each pair of reciprocated observations. From the several values of the co-efficient thus determined, those which are deemed most suitable are selected for employment in the reduction of vertical angles to secondary points, at which reciprocal observations have not been taken.”
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890), p. 23
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. XII: The Southern Trigon. Dehra Dun: Trigonometrical Branch, Survey of India.
Ray grazing a hill near Kem; evening refraction did not lift it clear.
“there was a hill on which the ray to Wápla grazed, but which was so close to Kem, that the increased refraction in the evening produced scarcely any perceptible effect in raising the ray clear of the hill”
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890), p. VIII-B · text layer
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. XII: The Southern Trigon. Dehra Dun: Trigonometrical Branch, Survey of India.
Old heights revised: taken at times other than minimum refraction.
“These heights had been mostly determined 30 to 56 years ago by the trigonometrical method, and they had been found to be less accurate than was desirable, the observations having in many instances been taken at other times than that of minimum terrestrial refraction, the laws of which were scarcely known then, rendering a revision very necessary.”
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890), p. IX-B · text layer
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. XII: The Southern Trigon. Dehra Dun: Trigonometrical Branch, Survey of India.
Table description.
“This is followed by the arc contained between the two stations, and then by the terrestrial refraction and the height of the 2nd station above or below the 1st, as computed from the vertical angles in the usual manner.”
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890), p. 58-B
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. XII: The Southern Trigon. Dehra Dun: Trigonometrical Branch, Survey of India.

South Konkan revision: simultaneous reciprocal angles on the transverse rays because of unequal refraction between ghats and coast.
“Owing to the defective character of the vertical observations on the portion of this Series executed with Dollond's instrument, and the inequality of refraction at the ends of the transverse rays—one flank of the Series resting on the ghats while the other lay on the low ground not far from the sea—it was afterwards decided to improve the heights by reobserving the vertical angles, those on the transverse rays being observed simultaneously.”
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890), p. 5 · text layer

Recommended practice

Coefficient of refraction from reciprocal vertical angles: phi = 1/2 {C - (Da + Db) + beta}; phi/C is the coefficient (terrestrial refraction in decimals of the contained arc). Selected values are used to reduce unreciprocated angles to secondary points.

Lambton (letter of 1814, quoted in Vol. XII): terrestrial refraction varied from 1/4 to 1/20 of the contained arc, which he attributed to moisture; he proposed hygrometer work and a table of low-altitude refractions from observation.
“In the course of my observations during the last ten years, I have at times found the terrestrial refraction to be as much as 1/4 of the contained arc, and occasionally as low as 1/20. This great irregularity I attribute to the different degrees of moisture in the atmosphere at different times.”
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890), p. 37 (Appendix)
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. XII: The Southern Trigon. Dehra Dun: Trigonometrical Branch, Survey of India.

“for on this condition freedom from grazing rays—and their concomitant lateral refractions—greatly depends. ... and of these there can be little doubt that the most important is lateral refraction, which is liable to be greater in the plains than in the hills, and much greater in tracts of country which are covered with dense forest and jungle, than in tracts which are open and cultivated.”
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890), p. 100 · text layer
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. XII: The Southern Trigon. Dehra Dun: Trigonometrical Branch, Survey of India.

GTS Guzerat Longitudinal Series (South-West Quadrilateral, Vol. XIV)

India · 1851-1862

The Vol. XIV pages held locally do not mention refraction. The observed-angle tables mark each single measure with h or l, the heliotrope and lamp signal letters, so angles were observed to both heliotrope and lamp signals. The vertical angle and height pages of Vol. XIV are not in the local set, so no coefficient for this series could be read. General GTS rules (1 to 3 p.m. vertical angles, reciprocal-angle coefficient, lateral refraction greater on plains) are attached from Walker (1870) and Vol. XII (1890).

Horizontal angles corrected for refraction: not stated

No refraction correction appears; each concluded angle is the mean of 12 groups on different zeros, with its relative weight; each measure carries an h or l signal mark.

Observed angles with h and l marks for each measure (Karsod, Indrawan, Feb 1862, Haig, Troughton and Simms 18-inch No. 2).
“h45.40 h48.17 h44.80 h47.20 h42.37 l50.90 l50.20 l44.17 l43.40 l41.90 l46.47 l45.00”
, p. 9-K
Observed-angle tables mark each single measure with l (lamp) or h (heliotrope) for the signal observed.
“Against each single measure is a letter in italics shewing whether the signal observed to was l, a lamp, or h, a heliotrope”
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890), p. 13 · text layer
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. XII: The Southern Trigon. Dehra Dun: Trigonometrical Branch, Survey of India.
Vol. XII names lateral refraction as the most important outside influence on the weight modulus of horizontal angles; it is greater in plains than hills, and much greater over dense forest and jungle. Freedom from grazing rays depends on station heights above ground.
“for on this condition freedom from grazing rays—and their concomitant lateral refractions—greatly depends. ... and of these there can be little doubt that the most important is lateral refraction, which is liable to be greater in the plains than in the hills, and much greater in tracts of country which are covered with dense forest and jungle, than in tracts which are open and cultivated.”
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890), p. 100 · text layer
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. XII: The Southern Trigon. Dehra Dun: Trigonometrical Branch, Survey of India.
Heliotropes and lamps were not used in the survey until after 1832 (introduced by Everest). Before that, signals were masts and flagstaves, and it became the practice to take the field after the first heavy fall of rain, the rainy season being the most favourable for viewing such objects.
“such as heliotropes and lamps, to erect on the stations which he was observing; but such signals were not employed in the operations of the survey until after the year 1832, when they were introduced by Colonel Everest. For very many years the signals were “ masts, flagstaves, and other opaque objects, and then days and days often passed away without a glimpse of the distant object.” As the atmosphere in India is usually most favorable during the rainy season, for viewing such objects, it became the practice to wait for the first heavy fall of rain and then take the field”
Walker (1870), p. XIX
Walker, J. T. (1870). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. I: Standards of measure and base-lines. Dehra Dun: Great Trigonometrical Survey.

Vertical angles and heights

Not available in the local pages.

Vol. XII formula assumes reciprocal angles equally refracted; modern vertical angles are generally measured between 1 and 3 p.m., when refraction is usually a minimum.
“In the preceding formulae it is assumed that the reciprocal angles are equally affected by refraction, and in order that this may be as nearly the case as possible, the vertical angles in all the modern operations are generally measured between the hours of 1 and 3 P.M., when the amount of refraction is usually a minimum.”
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890), p. 22
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. XII: The Southern Trigon. Dehra Dun: Trigonometrical Branch, Survey of India.
Coefficient of refraction from reciprocal vertical angles: phi = 1/2 {C - (Da + Db) + beta}; phi/C is the coefficient (terrestrial refraction in decimals of the contained arc). Selected values are used to reduce unreciprocated angles to secondary points.
“and φ/C gives the terrestrial refraction in decimals of contained arc—or in other words the co-efficient of refraction—for each pair of reciprocated observations. From the several values of the co-efficient thus determined, those which are deemed most suitable are selected for employment in the reduction of vertical angles to secondary points, at which reciprocal observations have not been taken.”
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890), p. 23
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. XII: The Southern Trigon. Dehra Dun: Trigonometrical Branch, Survey of India.

Observing rules

GTS rule from about 1836: vertical angles restricted to the time of minimum refraction, 1 to 3 p.m. apparent time, with back and forward angles at the same interval from noon. Between 1800 and 1836 vertical angles were taken at any hour, often at night.
“During the immediately preceding period of about twenty years the rule had been followed of restricting the observations of vertical angles as closely as possible to the time of minimum refraction, which occurs daily between the hours of 1 and 3 P.M. apparent time ; the back angle was also measured at nearly the same interval from apparent noon as the forward angle. But during the operations of the period between 1800 and 1836 the observations were taken at any hour of the day and often during the night.”
Walker (1870), p. 102
Walker, J. T. (1870). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. I: Standards of measure and base-lines. Dehra Dun: Great Trigonometrical Survey.
Over level country, rays grazing the ground pass through refraction from a low negative to a high positive value during day and night; reciprocal angles were several days apart, so equal refraction at both ends was mistrusted. Spirit levels began in 1856 to control the trigonometrical heights.
“But this method, though susceptible of a high degree of accuracy in a hill region, is beset with many difficulties in a level country; the rays of light, grazing the surface of the ground, pass through various gradations of refraction, from a low negative to a high positive value, during the course of the day and night; the success of the method depends on the reciprocal angles being equally refracted, which probably would not be the case even if the observations were simultaneous ; whereas from the circumstance of there being only one large theodolite with each survey party, there is always an interval of several days between the reciprocating observations, and thus there is all the more reason to mistrust the assumed equality of refraction.”
Walker (1870), p. 102
Walker, J. T. (1870). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. I: Standards of measure and base-lines. Dehra Dun: Great Trigonometrical Survey.
Vol. XII formula assumes reciprocal angles equally refracted; modern vertical angles are generally measured between 1 and 3 p.m., when refraction is usually a minimum.
“In the preceding formulae it is assumed that the reciprocal angles are equally affected by refraction, and in order that this may be as nearly the case as possible, the vertical angles in all the modern operations are generally measured between the hours of 1 and 3 P.M., when the amount of refraction is usually a minimum.”
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890), p. 22
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. XII: The Southern Trigon. Dehra Dun: Trigonometrical Branch, Survey of India.

Recommended practice

Coefficient of refraction from reciprocal vertical angles: phi = 1/2 {C - (Da + Db) + beta}; phi/C is the coefficient (terrestrial refraction in decimals of the contained arc). Selected values are used to reduce unreciprocated angles to secondary points.
“and φ/C gives the terrestrial refraction in decimals of contained arc—or in other words the co-efficient of refraction—for each pair of reciprocated observations. From the several values of the co-efficient thus determined, those which are deemed most suitable are selected for employment in the reduction of vertical angles to secondary points, at which reciprocal observations have not been taken.”
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890), p. 23
Great Trigonometrical Survey of India. (1890). Account of the operations of the Great Trigonometrical Survey of India, Vol. XII: The Southern Trigon. Dehra Dun: Trigonometrical Branch, Survey of India.

US Transcontinental Triangulation, 39th parallel

United States · 1844-1898

Horizontal directions were not corrected for refraction. Persistent lateral refraction is named as an error that shows in the net (triangle) adjustment and not in the station adjustment. Irregular lateral refraction is listed among causes of discrepancies in the local station measures. In the Nevada series the forenoon and afternoon measures were balanced to guard against lateral refraction. In the plains and wooded east the theodolite stood on scaffolds and towers to keep the line of sight above the ground, and in Indiana and Illinois the directions were observed at night on lights; in the western mountains they were observed on heliotropes in the early morning and late afternoon. Heights came from reciprocal (mostly non-simultaneous) zenith distances with the coefficient m (= k/2, refraction as a fraction of the intercepted arc) computed per line and averaged by region: about 0.053 in eastern Colorado, 0.085 on the California coast, 0.075 farther inland, 0.082 around Mount Hamilton, and two empirical formulas in temperature and height for the Rocky Mountain and Great Basin stations. Special hourly series showed refraction greatest at night, least within about 2 hours of noon, and nearly constant from about 10 a.m. to 5 1/2 p.m.; Schott names this as the best window for zenith distances.

Horizontal angles corrected for refraction: no

No refraction correction to horizontal directions is printed. Persistent lateral refraction is treated as an error source that does not show in the station adjustment and appears in the net (triangle) adjustment; irregular lateral refraction is listed among causes of discrepancies in the local measures. The Nevada series balanced forenoon and afternoon measures to guard against it.

Irregular lateral refraction is listed among the causes of the discrepancies in the local (station) measures.
“The discrepancies in the local measures are for the greater part due to defective graduation or want of adjustment of instrument, to irregular lateral refraction, defective illumination of object sighted, and to other causes.”
Schott (1900), p. 348 (footnote) · text layer
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Persistent lateral deviation of the line of sight is listed among errors that show only in the net adjustment, not in the station adjustment.
“want of coincidence of the center of a station and of heliotropes or targets subsequently mounted over it, persistent lateral deviation of the line of sight, constant or uncompensated graduation errors of the instrument, all of which causes exert no influence on the station adjustment.”
Schott (1900), p. 50 · text layer
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Salt Lake base net: the probable error of a direction from the base net adjustment (0.28") is three times that from the station adjustment (0.09"); lateral refraction is given a share of the cause.
“Persistent lateral refraction also has a share in producing the above result.”
Schott (1900), p. 206 · text layer
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Nevada series, from 1880: forenoon and afternoon measures of horizontal directions equalized, partly to provide against lateral refraction.
“The equalization of the number of measures of horizontal directions at a station taken in the forenoon and in the afternoon was first put into execution in 1880 in this section; its purpose was to eliminate any effect of unequal heating of the theodolite as well as to provide against possible lateral refraction along the lines of sight.”
Schott (1900), p. 573
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Local adjustment gave all observations equal weight, although observations on unsteady signals were known to be less accurate.
“It is known that observations upon some signals (which appear distinct and steady) are more accurate than others (upon signals which appear unsteady or indistinct). But the difficulty of properly estimating the relative weights, and the extra labor of making the computation after they have been introduced, make it advisable to assign equal weights to all observations.”
Schott (1900), p. 39 · text layer
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.

Vertical angles and heights

Heights west of First View, Colorado, come from zenith distances (spirit levels in the east). The coefficient m (= k/2) was computed for each line from reciprocal zenith distances, treated as simultaneous although observed in different years, under the assumption of equal refraction at both ends; regional means were used for one-way lines. For large height differences (Sierra Nevada lines, Rocky Mountain and Great Basin stations) a term for the difference of refraction at the upper and lower stations was kept, from an adopted value (-0.005) or from empirical formulas in temperature and height. Long lines across the Sacramento valley were excluded from the height adjustment.

Notation: m is used for half of the usual k.
“(k stands there for 2m in our notation.)”
Schott (1900), p. 329 (footnote)
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Formula for m from reciprocal zenith distances and its weight.
“The coefficient of refraction was computed as usual by the formula— m = 0·5 − ρ sin 1"/2s (ζ, + ζ,, − 180°)”
Schott (1900), p. 309
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Assumption of equal refraction at both ends, non-simultaneous data treated as simultaneous, m taken for the hours near the daily minimum.
“In deducing the coefficient of refraction m, we must, as usual, make the assumption of equality of angle of refraction at the upper and lower stations, treat the observations of zenith distances as "simultaneous reciprocal," though made in different years and different months, and take m as referring to the hours of the day when the refraction is near its minimum.”
Schott (1900), p. 276 · text layer
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Eastern Colorado: m falls with shorter lines, explained by the heated air near the ground; instruments were raised only enough to clear the curvature.
“The tabular values of m show an apparent dependence upon the length of lines s, viz: the shorter s, the smaller m. This fact may be explained by the circumstance of the line of sight being nearer the heated ground the shorter the distance. The comparatively warm stratum of air is quite close to the ground, particularly during insolation.”
Schott (1900), p. 263
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Eastern Colorado: instrument height.
“The instrument was only elevated sufficiently to overcome the earth's curvature and permit the visibility of the distant object.”
Schott (1900), p. 263
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Eastern Colorado: early and some late zenith distances excluded because refraction was well above its daily minimum.
“As a rule (with some exceptions) the early observations—say those made before 9 or 10 a. m.—and some late ones in the afternoon could not be included, the refraction being then much above its ordinary minimum daily amount.”
Schott (1900), p. 267 · text layer
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
California coast: coastal climate increases refraction.
“These results are in accordance with the known influence of a coast climate on the atmospheric refraction, which is to increase it. For the 19 lines close to the coast—say within 20 or 30 kilometres of it—we find the value m = 0·085 4, whereas farther inland—say within 60 or 90 kilometres—it has diminished to 0·075 1.”
Schott (1900), p. 277
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Sacramento and San Joaquin valleys: 1898 strictly simultaneous reciprocal measures over shorter lines were added because of difficulties over the long valley lines.
“This was done in the summer of 1898, by strictly simultaneous reciprocal measures over shorter lines, along which the variations of refraction are less injurious.”
Schott (1900), p. 304 · text layer
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Coast versus interior: hour of minimum and unequal refraction at upper and lower stations.
“the greater was the refraction, and that in the diurnal variation the hour of the minimum refraction occurred in the forenoon for coast stations, but in the early afternoon for stations in the interior. Apparently, also, a great difference in the angle of refraction was shown to subsist at the lower and upper stations in the Martinez East and Mount Diablo experiments”
Schott (1900), p. 305
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Long lines across the valley (up to 229 km) excluded from the height results.
“it does not seem surprising that it became necessary to exclude the resulting differences of height over the several long lines across the valley which range up to 229 kilometres or 142 statute miles.”
Schott (1900), p. 305
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Sierra Nevada lines: difference of refraction term retained with an adopted value; elsewhere the adjustment is trusted to absorb unequal refraction.
“It was evident, however, that local conditions had a powerful effect upon the refraction, and it seemed best, as usual, to trust to the adjustment to correct for the assumption of equal refractions where in reality they were unequal. The same plan has been followed here except for the three lines Pine Hill to Mount Lola, Pine Hill to Round Top, and Jackson Butte to Round Top, extending from the valley to the top of the Sierra Nevada.”
Schott (1900), p. 310
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Rocky Mountain and Great Basin heights: term for unequal refraction (Eimbeck) retained.
“In the development of the expression for the difference of height from reciprocal zenith distances there appears a term* depending upon the difference of the refraction at the two stations. This term is usually suppressed, in application, as insignificant. Assistant W. Eimbeck called attention to it when determining the heights of the Rocky Mountain stations and applied it in his field computation.”
Schott (1900), p. 329
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
m increases as station height decreases, except at abnormal temperatures.
“An inspection of this table shows a steady increase in the coefficient of refraction as the height decreases, except where the temperature is abnormally high or low, in which case the value of m is correspondingly low or high.”
Schott (1900), p. 332 · text layer
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Rocky Mountain series: minimum refraction found late in the afternoon.
“This brought to light the fact that the minimum refraction of the day occurs late in the afternoon, even after the heat of the day has passed.”
Schott (1900), p. 553
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Rocky Mountain series: no simultaneous reciprocal zenith distances.
“No simultaneous reciprocal zenith distances were obtained.”
Schott (1900), p. 553
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.

Observing rules

Best hours for zenith distances, from the special hourly series; noon images faint and unsteady.
“The refraction changes but little, comparatively, between the hours of 10 a. m. and 5½ p. m. and the intervening time is best suited for observing zenith distances for altitudes, so far as refraction is concerned; but in other respects, as for telescopic visions, the hours about noon are unfavorable on account of faint, unsteady, and distorted images being then more prevalent.”
Schott (1900), p. 256
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Jackson Butte-Round Top series: best time for vertical angles.
“The value of m is least variable near its minimum, and the best time for observing vertical angles would appear to be between 11½ a. m. and 4½ p. m. The minimum occurs between 2 and 3 p. m., the maximum apparently at 9 p. m.”
Schott (1900), p. 287
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Central section (plains): theodolite on tripods or scaffolds to keep the line of sight above the ground.
“As a rule the theodolite was mounted on tripods or scaffolds in order to overcome the earth's curvature and keep the line of sight sufficiently elevated above the ground.”
Schott (1900), p. 25 · text layer
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Eastern Shore series: instrument up to 120 ft and targets up to 260 ft above ground.
“at six of those on the peninsula the instrument was mounted 120 feet (36·6 metres) above the ground, and the targets observed on were in some instances as much as 260 feet (79·2 metres) above the ground.”
Schott (1900), p. 352
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Allegheny series: pole targets to about 56 km, heliotropes beyond.
“No high signals were necessary in this section and poles with lozenge-shaped targets could usually be seen up to 56 kilometres (35 statute miles, about), beyond which heliotropes were used.”
Schott (1900), p. 369 · text layer
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Ohio series: signals 100 to 150 ft; heliotropes on lines over 10 miles unless the sun was behind the observer.
“here signals of from 100 to 150 feet in height were necessary, and heliotropes were used on lines over 10 miles in length unless the sun was at the observer's back, in which case a pole with a lozenge target could be observed on.”
Schott (1900), p. 396
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Indiana series: highest towers; all observations at night on lights.
“It was through this section that our highest towers were needed, and even then considerable cutting had to be resorted to. All the observations were made on lights at night.”
Schott (1900), p. 418
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Illinois series: scaffolds averaging 18.1 m; night lights after 1880.
“During the first season, in 1880, observations were made on poles; after that all observations were made on lights at night.”
Schott (1900), p. 435 · text layer
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Missouri-Kansas series: theodolite about 25 ft up; afternoon observing.
“As a rule the observations were pursued during the afternoon hours every favorable day.”
Schott (1900), p. 481 · text layer
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Rocky Mountain series: heliotropes; hours for horizontal directions and three periods for zenith distances, over many days.
“Respecting the time of observations, they were made from sunrise till 8 o'clock, and resumed in the afternoon at half past 4 o'clock and continued till sunset. The seeing was usually better in the morning than in the evening”
Schott (1900), p. 553
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Rocky Mountain series: zenith distances in three daily periods, spread over at least twelve days.
“Double zenith distances for heights of stations were observed at three different periods of the day, viz. between 6½ and 8 o'clock in the morning, between 11½ and 1 o'clock, and again between 4½ and 6 o'clock in the evening.”
Schott (1900), p. 553
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Rocky Mountain series: observations spread over twenty or more days for varied atmospheric conditions.
“To secure observations under a variety of atmospheric conditions, observations were extended over twenty or more days.”
Schott (1900), p. 553
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Coast Range: at Great Caspar the theodolite stood 41.14 m above ground on a 13-story scaffold.
“the 30-centimetre (12-inch) theodolite was mounted on the top of a quadrangular scaffold 13 stories high, and stood 41·14 metres (or 135 feet) above ground”
Schott (1900), p. 598 · text layer
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.

Recommended practice

Schott names 10 a.m. to 5 1/2 p.m. as the best window for zenith distances with respect to refraction (see observing_rules, p. 256).
“the intervening time is best suited for observing zenith distances for altitudes, so far as refraction is concerned”
Schott (1900), p. 256
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
For lines with a great height difference and length, the unequal-refraction term must be kept.
“For a great difference in elevation and a large distance the effect of this term evidently becomes a matter of importance.”
Schott (1900), p. 329
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Introductory statement: refraction laws need change for large height differences.
“The laws of refraction applicable at lower and equal elevations require modification when great inequalities exist in the heights of stations.”
Schott (1900), p. 18 · text layer
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.

Triangle closures and excess

Refraction does not enter the spherical excess (Legendre's theorem, pp. 51-52). Persistent lateral refraction is named as an error source that does not show in the station adjustment and appears in the net adjustment (pp. 50, 206); irregular lateral refraction is listed among causes of discrepancies in the local measures (p. 348).
“persistent lateral deviation of the line of sight, constant or uncompensated graduation errors of the instrument, all of which causes exert no influence on the station adjustment.”
Schott (1900), p. 50 · text layer
Schott, C. A. (1900). The transcontinental triangulation and the American arc of the parallel (Special Publication No. 4). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.

  • No refraction correction applied to horizontal directions.
  • No mention of reverberatory lamps by that name; lamps/lights at night are named (Indiana, Illinois), heliotropes in the west.
  • No refraction term in the spherical excess computation (pp. 51-52 searched).
  • No 'Bilby' tower named; towers and scaffolds described generally.
  • No rejection of horizontal directions for unsteady images stated as a rule; unsteady signals were noted but given equal weight (p. 39).

US Eastern Oblique Arc, Calais (Maine) to New Orleans

United States · 1833-1898

The report does not correct horizontal directions for refraction. Persistent lateral refraction is named as an error that shows in the triangle closures and not in the station adjustment. In Alabama, from 1888, all primary horizontal measures were made at night on lights for the clearer air and better seeing, with two or three lights shown vertically on long lines, and the theodolite was raised on scaffolds up to 46.5 m. The report has no heights part and prints no refraction coefficient; zenith distances appear only once, as double zenith distances of Stone Mountain measured with spirit levels to reduce the Atlanta base to sea level.

Horizontal angles corrected for refraction: no

No refraction correction is applied. Persistent lateral refraction is listed among errors that the figure adjustment, not the station adjustment, reveals.

Lateral refraction is listed among errors that do not show in station adjustment but in triangle combination.
“The errors brought to light by geometrical conditions, and which have been called triangle-combination errors, are of a different character from those developed by a comparison of the measures made at a station, where defects of centering at observing and observed stations, effects of large local deflections of the vertical at a station, persistent lateral refraction along a line, etc., do not show”
Schott (1902), p. 26
Schott, C. A. (1902). The eastern oblique arc of the United States and osculating spheroid (Special Publication No. 7). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.

Vertical angles and heights

Not stated. The report gives approximate station heights (from levels or earlier sources) for reductions of base lines and of directions for height of the observed station. The only zenith distances named are double zenith distances of Stone Mountain, used with spirit levels to reduce the Atlanta base to sea level (p. 74); no refraction coefficient is printed.

Atlanta base: spirit levels and double zenith distances of Stone Mountain were used to reduce the base to sea level; no refraction coefficient is given.
“In order to obtain the data required to reduce each part of the base to sea level, two lines of spirit levels were carried over it, double zenith distances of Stone Mountain were measured for difference of height at Southwest Base, Middle Base, and Northeast Base, and reciprocal observations were made at Stone Mountain.”
Schott (1902), p. 74
Schott, C. A. (1902). The eastern oblique arc of the United States and osculating spheroid (Special Publication No. 7). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.

Observing rules

Alabama, from 1888: all primary horizontal measures at night on lights, for transparency and seeing; multiple vertical lights on long lines.
“Subsequent to this date all horizontal measures of the primary triangulation between these stations and the Gulf coast were made at night by Assistants F. W. Perkins and W. B. Fairfield. Advantage was thus taken of the greater transparency of the atmosphere and of the better seeing during the night as compared with the day. On long or difficult lines two or three lights arranged vertically were shown at the same station.”
Schott (1902), p. 194
Schott, C. A. (1902). The eastern oblique arc of the United States and osculating spheroid (Special Publication No. 7). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Fourth section west of Atlanta base net: night observing on lights over the stations; at Pollard the theodolite was raised 46.53 m.
“46·53 meters (or 152½ feet) above the ground, in order to raise it above the high woods obstructing the lines of sight.”
Schott (1902), p. 200
Schott, C. A. (1902). The eastern oblique arc of the United States and osculating spheroid (Special Publication No. 7). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Fourth section: primary directions at night on lights.
“The observations of the primary directions were made at night upon lights mounted over the respective stations.”
Schott (1902), p. 199 · text layer
Schott, C. A. (1902). The eastern oblique arc of the United States and osculating spheroid (Special Publication No. 7). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Fifth section to Mobile Bay: scaffolds 12.4 to 46.5 m; all observing at night on signal lights.
“The country being well timbered and cutting impracticable, it was necessary to elevate the theodolite on scaffolds ranging from 12m·4 to 46m·5 in height, with several 37m high. All observing was done at night on signal lights.”
Schott (1902), p. 209
Schott, C. A. (1902). The eastern oblique arc of the United States and osculating spheroid (Special Publication No. 7). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.
Alabama sections give nearly equal direction errors whatever the instrument height or day/night observing.
“For the several sections these last values are very nearly equal notwithstanding the number of observers, of instruments, and methods, and other circumstances, such as elevation of instruments, day or night observations, etc. The values of the probable error of a direction for the second, third, fourth, and fifth sections average ± 0"·34”
Schott (1902), p. 209
Schott, C. A. (1902). The eastern oblique arc of the United States and osculating spheroid (Special Publication No. 7). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.

Triangle closures and excess

Spherical excess by the simple formula ab sin C / (2 ρm ρn sin 1"); no refraction term. Lateral refraction is placed among triangle-combination errors (closures).
“since none of the lines of the oblique arc are very long the spherical excess was computed by the simple formula”
Schott (1902), p. 26
Schott, C. A. (1902). The eastern oblique arc of the United States and osculating spheroid (Special Publication No. 7). U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey; Government Printing Office.

Maclear's verification and extension of La Caille's arc of meridian, Cape of Good Hope

Southern Africa · 1840-1850

The horizontal angles were not corrected for refraction. Maclear reports that unsteady signals over heated sand and bush lowered the precision of pointing, and he took this into the weights of the angles. He gives one comparison: 5 results in first-rate definition gave a probable error of 0.05" (weight 360), while 16 results during bad definition and lateral refraction gave 0.3" (weight 11). For heights, zenith distances were measured with the Dollond repeating circle and the Beaufort theodolite, and the co-efficient of refraction m was computed from 10 sets of reciprocal zenith distances. The mean m = .0712 (fraction of the arc at the earth's centre) was adopted for the altitudes above the sea.

Horizontal angles corrected for refraction: no

No correction for lateral refraction is applied. Unsteady (undulating) signals were treated as a source of error in the weights of the angles, and the number of partial results per angle depended on the atmospheric conditions.

On the flat ground of the base-line triangulation, heated sand and bush made the signals undulate, except in gloomy and moist weather or early morning and late evening. This element of error was put into the weights.
“the line of sight passing in close proximity to heated sand and bush, causing the signals to undulate so much, except during gloomy and moist weather, or early in the morning and late in the evening, as to materially diminish the precision of the pointing : it is therefore necessary to introduce this element of error.”
Maclear (1866), p. 338
Maclear, T. (1866). Verification and extension of La Caille's arc of meridian at the Cape of Good Hope (Vol. 1). London: Lords Commissioners of the Admiralty.
Size of the effect on the Beaufort theodolite: 5 results in first-rate definition gave probable error 0.05" (weight 360); 16 results in bad definition and lateral refraction gave 0.3" (weight 11). Footnotes: (a) Simon's Berg, Nov. 19, 1845; (b) Kapoc Berg, Feb. 12, 1846.
“The value of the latter may be gathered from the fact of five results obtained during first-rate definition, giving a probable error of 0"·05 and weight 360 (a) ; whereas during bad definition and lateral refraction, 16 results gave a probable error of 0"·3, and weight 11 (b).”
Maclear (1866), p. 400
Maclear, T. (1866). Verification and extension of La Caille's arc of meridian at the Cape of Good Hope (Vol. 1). London: Lords Commissioners of the Admiralty.
The number of partial results for each angle depended on the importance of the angle and the atmospheric circumstances.
“the required number of such results depended upon the importance of the angle, and the atmospheric circumstances during the time when measuring.”
Maclear (1866), p. 400
Maclear, T. (1866). Verification and extension of La Caille's arc of meridian at the Cape of Good Hope (Vol. 1). London: Lords Commissioners of the Admiralty.
At Boschluis (Bushman Flat, January 1847) the angle between Keibiskow and Kamies-Sector Berg was delayed, as the top of Keibiskow could be seen only during the maximum refraction, shortly after daybreak. The margin note reads 'Effect of mirage on the Keibiskow light.'
“The angle between the Keibiskow and Kamies-Sector Berg station detained me there until the 11th, for the top of Keibiskow could only be seen during the maximum refraction. Shortly after daybreak, the culminating top of the mountain gradually ascended above the horizon”
Maclear (1866), p. 415
Maclear, T. (1866). Verification and extension of La Caille's arc of meridian at the Cape of Good Hope (Vol. 1). London: Lords Commissioners of the Admiralty.
Station description for Boschluis repeats that Keibiskow is visible only in the hours of maximum refraction.
“whence the top of Keibiskow is visible above the horizon, during the hours of maximum refraction.”
Maclear (1866), p. 472 · text layer
Maclear, T. (1866). Verification and extension of La Caille's arc of meridian at the Cape of Good Hope (Vol. 1). London: Lords Commissioners of the Admiralty.

Vertical angles and heights

Zenith distances were measured with the Dollond repeating circle (D) and the Beaufort theodolite (B), often at eccentric stations, with hour of observation recorded. The co-efficient of refraction m was found from 10 sets of reciprocal zenith distances; the mean .0712 was adopted to compute the differences of altitude. Weights were proportional to the number of observations and inverse to the distance. Spirit-levelled heights of some stations served as data.

The Dollond repeating circle was used for the zenith distances of the triangulation.
“It was used on the triangulation for measuring zenith distances.”
Maclear (1866), p. 400
Maclear, T. (1866). Verification and extension of La Caille's arc of meridian at the Cape of Good Hope (Vol. 1). London: Lords Commissioners of the Admiralty.
Formulae for m and for the difference of height.
“then h'-h = s cot. (z + m c - ½ c) (1 + (h'+h)/2ν). and m = ½ (1 - (z+z'-180°)/c).”
Maclear (1866), p. 598
Maclear, T. (1866). Verification and extension of La Caille's arc of meridian at the Cape of Good Hope (Vol. 1). London: Lords Commissioners of the Admiralty.
m was calculated from 10 sets of reciprocal zenith distances; the mean is .0712.
“The calculation of m from 10 sets of reciprocal zenith distances is contained in the following table : ... The mean value of m from the above is ·0712.”
Maclear (1866), p. 598
Maclear, T. (1866). Verification and extension of La Caille's arc of meridian at the Cape of Good Hope (Vol. 1). London: Lords Commissioners of the Admiralty.
Weights of the height results.
“The weight of each separate result is assumed to be directly proportional to the number of observations, and inversely as the distance of the stations.”
Maclear (1866), p. 599 · text layer
Maclear, T. (1866). Verification and extension of La Caille's arc of meridian at the Cape of Good Hope (Vol. 1). London: Lords Commissioners of the Admiralty.
For La Caille's base at Klyp Fonteyn, levelled heights and the refraction entered the computed altitudes; zenith distances were observed at both ends of the base and the mean taken.
“These quantities are taken into account in the computation of the altitudes, together with the refraction : the zenith distances having been observed at both ends of the base, the mean of the results was taken.”
Maclear (1866), p. 49 · text layer
Maclear, T. (1866). Verification and extension of La Caille's arc of meridian at the Cape of Good Hope (Vol. 1). London: Lords Commissioners of the Admiralty.

Observing rules

Heliostat signals were used; aperture was set by distance.
“A 2-inch aperture is sufficient in the Cape climate for 30 miles, 3 inches for 40 or 50 miles, 4 inches for 70 or 80 miles, &c.”
Maclear (1866), p. 401 · text layer
Maclear, T. (1866). Verification and extension of La Caille's arc of meridian at the Cape of Good Hope (Vol. 1). London: Lords Commissioners of the Admiralty.
Heliostat light 80 miles away could be observed with the Beaufort theodolite.
“with respect to heliostat signals, the light through a 4-inch diaphragm aperture, distant 80 miles, appeared as a small star in the telescope and was successfully observed ! (c).”
Maclear (1866), p. 400
Maclear, T. (1866). Verification and extension of La Caille's arc of meridian at the Cape of Good Hope (Vol. 1). London: Lords Commissioners of the Admiralty.
If the elevations of the two signals differed much, the Y bearings were reversed for half of the partial measures.
“and if the difference between the elevation of the signals was considerable, the Y bearings were reversed 180° in Azimuth for one half of the partial measures.”
Maclear (1866), p. 400
Maclear, T. (1866). Verification and extension of La Caille's arc of meridian at the Cape of Good Hope (Vol. 1). London: Lords Commissioners of the Admiralty.
The zenith distance table records the hour of each observation and the height of the object above the station surface; H marks a heliostat target.
“In the eighth column is given the height of the object observed above the surface of the station ; and when the object was a heliostat, the letter H is annexed to the name of the station in column five.”
Maclear (1866), p. 591 · text layer
Maclear, T. (1866). Verification and extension of La Caille's arc of meridian at the Cape of Good Hope (Vol. 1). London: Lords Commissioners of the Admiralty.

Recommended practice

Steady signals are noted when the sky was clouded (Uyle Kraal station, base-line triangulation).
“While occupying this station the sky was clouded and the signals steady.”
Maclear (1866), p. 337 · text layer
Maclear, T. (1866). Verification and extension of La Caille's arc of meridian at the Cape of Good Hope (Vol. 1). London: Lords Commissioners of the Admiralty.

Not found in the pages read

  • No correction of horizontal angles for lateral refraction.
  • No link between refraction and the spherical excess or triangle closures is stated.
  • No variation of m with time of day or season is discussed; the 10 values of m are not dated in the table on p. 598.
  • Volume 2 holds the zenith sector star observations; its only refraction entries are the astronomical refraction column notes (e.g. 'The Refraction is always to be applied additively'), not terrestrial refraction.
  • No explicit rule or recommendation on how to deal with refraction beyond the weighting of angles for undulating signals.
  • A second scan of Vol. 1 was not read separately (same work).
