Geodetic Survey of the Transvaal and Orange River Colony (Morris; preface and introduction by Gill)

Southern Africa · 1902-1908 (scheme 1902, report 1908; field season dates not extracted)

The 1908 report gives no correction of horizontal angles for lateral refraction. The observer was told to observe half the arcs in the early morning and half in the late afternoon, when the air was expected to be clearest and steadiest; morning mist and haze often prevented this. All vertical angles were observed in the afternoon as near to sunset as possible. Differences of height were computed from mutual angles of elevation with h' - h = s tan ½(α - α'), and coefficients of refraction from the mutual vertical angles are tabulated per line, with chain means from .057 to .068 (Table M). Heights of the base lines above mean sea level depended on the vertical angles, with probable errors up to 10 feet.

Horizontal angles corrected for refraction: no

No lateral refraction correction or discussion in the 1908 report. Observing hours (half the arcs early morning, half late afternoon) were set for clear, steady air, and the number of bisections depended on steadiness and definition.

Rule for horizontal arcs: half early morning, half late afternoon, for the clearest and steadiest air; morning mist and haze often prevented it.
“The instructions to the observer were to observe half the arcs in the early morning and the remainder in the late afternoon, as being the hours of the day when the air might be expected to be clearest and steadiest. But it was soon found that to observe strictly in this way would be the cause of considerable delay when the morning observations were frequently quite prevented by mist and haze.”
Morris (1908), p. 19
Morris, W. G. (1908). Reports on the geodetic survey of the Transvaal and Orange River Colony (Geodetic Survey of South Africa, Vol. 5; preface and introduction by D. Gill). London: Harrison and Sons.
The number of bisections of a heliograph light or beacon depended on steadiness and definition.
“In the observations of the horizontal angles, a number of bisections of the heliograph light, or beacon, were made, depending on their steadiness and definition in the field of view”
Morris (1908), p. 19
Morris, W. G. (1908). Reports on the geodetic survey of the Transvaal and Orange River Colony (Geodetic Survey of South Africa, Vol. 5; preface and introduction by D. Gill). London: Harrison and Sons.

Vertical angles and heights

All vertical angles were observed in the afternoon as near to sunset as possible. Differences of height came from mutual angles of elevation, weighted inversely as the line length, and adjusted in blocks; the Cape Colony and Natal heights were held fixed. Coefficients of refraction from the mutual vertical angles are listed per line and per chain in Table M.

Vertical angles observed in the afternoon near sunset.
“The vertical angles were all observed in the afternoon as near to sunset as possible, and in repetition equivalent to the first pair of arcs as for horizontal angles, the readings of the vertical arc level being always recorded.”
Morris (1908), p. 19
Morris, W. G. (1908). Reports on the geodetic survey of the Transvaal and Orange River Colony (Geodetic Survey of South Africa, Vol. 5; preface and introduction by D. Gill). London: Harrison and Sons.
Height formula from mutual elevation angles.
“The differences of height were computed by the formula h' - h = s . tan ½ (α - α') where s = the length of the line in feet α, α' = the angles of elevation of each point from the other.”
Morris (1908), p. 151
Morris, W. G. (1908). Reports on the geodetic survey of the Transvaal and Orange River Colony (Geodetic Survey of South Africa, Vol. 5; preface and introduction by D. Gill). London: Harrison and Sons.
Weights inverse to line length; adjusted in blocks; Cape Colony and Natal heights held fixed.
“As in previous surveys in South Africa, weights were assigned to the observations in inverse proportion to the length of the lines. The heights were adjusted in blocks or sets as shown in the Diagram for Solution of Vertical Heights, Diagram No. 8. The heights of all stations in the Survey of Cape Colony and Natal have been taken as fixed by the original solution (G.S. Report, Vol. I, pp. 191-216).”
Morris (1908), p. 151
Morris, W. G. (1908). Reports on the geodetic survey of the Transvaal and Orange River Colony (Geodetic Survey of South Africa, Vol. 5; preface and introduction by D. Gill). London: Harrison and Sons.
Table M lists coefficients of refraction and chain means; bracketed values omitted.
“1. The coefficients of refraction and the mean coefficient for each chain Quantities in brackets are omitted in the mean (Table M).”
Morris (1908), p. 151
Morris, W. G. (1908). Reports on the geodetic survey of the Transvaal and Orange River Colony (Geodetic Survey of South Africa, Vol. 5; preface and introduction by D. Gill). London: Harrison and Sons.
Heights of base lines above mean sea level depended on the vertical angles, except Belfast and Kroonstad.
“as only two base lines (Belfast and Kroonstad) were directly connected with the mean sea level, the heights above mean sea level of the others were entirely dependent on the results of the vertical angles”
Morris (1908), p. 27
Morris, W. G. (1908). Reports on the geodetic survey of the Transvaal and Orange River Colony (Geodetic Survey of South Africa, Vol. 5; preface and introduction by D. Gill). London: Harrison and Sons.
Probable errors of base heights from vertical angles: Wepener 10 ft (830 miles of chain), Ottoshoop 8 ft (520), Houts R. 7½ ft (440). Levelling minus vertical-angle heights: Belfast Base +8.5, Observatory Hill +6.1, Kroonstad Base -7.3, Muckleneuk +8.8 feet.
“Probable error of heights of base lines above mean sea level through the vertical angles : Wepener Base, through 830 miles of chain ... 10 feet.”
Morris (1908), p. 27
Morris, W. G. (1908). Reports on the geodetic survey of the Transvaal and Orange River Colony (Geodetic Survey of South Africa, Vol. 5; preface and introduction by D. Gill). London: Harrison and Sons.

Observing rules

Heliograph light shown morning and evening from sunrise, and from a set time in the afternoon till sunset.
“The light should be shown to the observing station both morning and evening from sunrise onwards till the call "C W N" is received, and from a pre-arranged time in the afternoon till sunset.”
Morris (1908), p. 41
Morris, W. G. (1908). Reports on the geodetic survey of the Transvaal and Orange River Colony (Geodetic Survey of South Africa, Vol. 5; preface and introduction by D. Gill). London: Harrison and Sons.
Beacons to be cleared of bush and grass so that they project on the sky where possible.
“The signaller should use every endeavour to make the beacon and its discs clearly visible to the observer by removing obstacles such as bush, long grass, or stones both before and behind the beacon, in the latter case that the beacon may be projected on the sky where possible.”
Morris (1908), p. 41
Morris, W. G. (1908). Reports on the geodetic survey of the Transvaal and Orange River Colony (Geodetic Survey of South Africa, Vol. 5; preface and introduction by D. Gill). London: Harrison and Sons.
Theodolite (Repsold 10-inch) always in a hut, protected and screened from sun and wind.
“It was always protected and screened from the sun and wind by an enclosing hut”
Morris (1908), p. 19
Morris, W. G. (1908). Reports on the geodetic survey of the Transvaal and Orange River Colony (Geodetic Survey of South Africa, Vol. 5; preface and introduction by D. Gill). London: Harrison and Sons.

Triangle closures and excess

Spherical excess computed by Legendre's theorem from the triangle area; refraction is not mentioned in this connection.
“Spheroidal triangles are calculated as spherical triangles by Legendre's theorem, the spherical excess being obtained from the formula e" = 2 x Area of triangle (in square feet) x E,”
Morris (1908), p. 183
Morris, W. G. (1908). Reports on the geodetic survey of the Transvaal and Orange River Colony (Geodetic Survey of South Africa, Vol. 5; preface and introduction by D. Gill). London: Harrison and Sons.

Not found in the pages read

  • No correction for, or discussion of, lateral refraction in horizontal angles in the 1908 report (OCR text searched for refraction, lateral, tremulous, boiling, unsteady).
  • Definition of the coefficient in Table M (fraction of arc or other) is not printed; no formula for it is given (full OCR text and XML searched for refraction and coefficient).
  • No discussion of variation of the coefficient with time of day, season or ground.
  • No statement of whether the Table M coefficients were applied to one-way lines.
  • Gill (1906) BA report: no mention of refraction, heliograph observing hours or vertical angles (text layer searched).
  • Gill's report on the Cape Colony and Natal survey (1896), where Gill discusses lateral refraction (as cited by Furber 1898), is not among the assigned files.

Trigonometrical Survey of Van Diemen's Land (Tasmania)

Australia · survey in progress at the 1855 report

Cotton's report does not treat terrestrial refraction. Horizontal angles were measured by repetition with a twelve-inch instrument, and no refraction correction is mentioned. Angles of elevation and depression were observed at every principal station to find relative altitudes, but no refraction coefficient or method for the heights is given. The only mention of refraction is in Sprent's astronomical report: the latitude from star elongations does not depend on refraction.

Horizontal angles corrected for refraction: not stated

Angles measured by repetition with the twelve-inch instrument; refraction is not discussed.

Angles observed by repetition; error rarely more than 4 or 5 seconds.
“the error rarely amounting to more than four or five seconds, and generally not exceeding two seconds. The observations have been all made by Mr. Sprent, and with the same twelve-inch instrument. By means of the repeating table each angle is observed under a series of repetitions, and the mean obtained is exceedingly near the truth.”
Cotton (1855), p. 89
Cotton, H. C. (1855). On the trigonometrical survey of Van Diemen's Land. Papers and Proceedings of the Royal Society of Van Diemen's Land, 3, 82-107.

Vertical angles and heights

Angles of elevation and depression were observed at every principal station for relative altitudes. No coefficient, reduction method or station heights are printed; one field-book extract (p. 95) shows an observed vertical angle with barometer and thermometer readings.

Elevation and depression angles at every principal station.
“At every principal station Angles of Elevation and Depression for determining relative altitudes have been observed,”
Cotton (1855), p. 89
Cotton, H. C. (1855). On the trigonometrical survey of Van Diemen's Land. Papers and Proceedings of the Royal Society of Van Diemen's Land, 3, 82-107.
Sprent's latitude report (astronomical, not terrestrial): refraction does not enter the latitude from elongations.
“It is to be observed that in the observations from which the above results were obtained, refraction forms no function in the elements for the calculation.”
Cotton (1855), p. 101
Cotton, H. C. (1855). On the trigonometrical survey of Van Diemen's Land. Papers and Proceedings of the Royal Society of Van Diemen's Land, 3, 82-107.

Observing rules

Azimuths of circumpolar star elongations were observed from another station or from a fixed lamp.
“observing their azimuthal angle from another station, or from a fixed lamp whose position with reference to some other station is known.”
Cotton (1855), p. 89
Cotton, H. C. (1855). On the trigonometrical survey of Van Diemen's Land. Papers and Proceedings of the Royal Society of Van Diemen's Land, 3, 82-107.
The field-book extract for Dromedary Station records a barometer reading (27.016) and attached and detached thermometer readings (46, 46) with the elevation and depression observations to Rummey's Hill.
“Elevations and Depressions. ... Baro- meter. ... Thermometer. ... Att'd. ... Det'd. ... 27·016 ... 46 ... 46”
Cotton (1855), p. 95
Cotton, H. C. (1855). On the trigonometrical survey of Van Diemen's Land. Papers and Proceedings of the Royal Society of Van Diemen's Land, 3, 82-107.

Not found in the pages read

  • No refraction coefficient for heights.
  • No correction or discussion of lateral refraction in horizontal angles.
  • No observing hours, heliotropes or signal rules aimed at refraction.
  • No link of refraction with spherical excess or triangle closures.
  • Heights of stations are not tabulated in the paper.
  • Other mentions of the paper in the volume (pdf pp. 202, 226: proceedings note and contents) hold no refraction content.

Trigonometrical Survey of New South Wales

Australia · 1867-1898 (resumed 1890; paper read January 1898)

Furber gives no lateral refraction correction for New South Wales, but he reports Gill's South African finding that lateral refraction exceeded pointing errors and that Gill combined morning and afternoon observations. Heights came from zenith distances observed at each station, not simultaneous at both ends, but always at the same time of day (the usual time 2 to 4 p.m.). The coefficient of refraction K was computed from reciprocal observations on nearly a hundred south-coast lines; it grew with line length (.096 under 10 miles to .135 over 20 miles), and Furber gives K = .135 for lines over 30 miles and a distance formula for shorter lines. He names refraction as the main disturbing factor in trigonometrical levelling and plans an adjustment that minimises the sum of squares of the coefficients.

Horizontal angles corrected for refraction: no

No correction stated for New South Wales. Furber reports Gill's experience with lateral refraction in South Africa and recommends attention to it; photographs from each station are wished for to study vertical and lateral refraction.

Reports Gill: lateral refraction errors exceeded pointing/reading errors; wind change shifted directions; systematic difference before and after noon.
“he states that during the course of the survey it was shown that the accidental errors of pointing or reading were less to be regarded than those caused by lateral refraction. Not only was it found that a change of wind was sometimes followed by marked change in the direction of a distant station, but that a systematic difference, small but appreciable, existed between directions measured after and before noon,”
checked on the page images of pp. 181 and 182
Furber (1898), pp. 181-182
Furber, T. F. (1898). The trigonometrical survey of New South Wales, with mention of similar surveys in the other Australian colonies. Report of the Australasian Association for the Advancement of Science, 7, Section A, 176-237.
Reports Gill's remedy: vary observing conditions, smaller 10-inch theodolite, morning and afternoon combined; probable error of an angle fell from 0.49" to 0.33".
“the general conclusion was come to that steps were to be taken to reduce the effects of refraction by varying the conditions under which the observations were made rather than by seeking precision from extraordinary accuracy of the instruments used. In this view a 10-inch theodolite was substituted by Dr. Gill for the 18-inch instrument previously employed, and throughout the remainder of the work morning and afternoon observations were combined,”
checked on the page image of p. 182
Furber (1898), p. 182
Furber, T. F. (1898). The trigonometrical survey of New South Wales, with mention of similar surveys in the other Australian colonies. Report of the Australasian Association for the Advancement of Science, 7, Section A, 176-237.

Vertical angles and heights

Zenith distances of surrounding points were measured at each station with a 10-inch theodolite, in pairs of rounds with the telescope reversed, with a level along the line read at each sighting. Reciprocal observations were not simultaneous but were made at the same time of day. Differences of height used a formula in the reciprocal zenith distances; heights agreed with railway spirit-levelling except at Skeletar (9 feet).

Not simultaneous; same time of day instead.
“The simultaneous observation at each end of the line involved, which is necessary to an ideal determination of relative heights (for by that means only is it made certain that the co-efficients of refraction at the two ends of the line are the same) has, of course, been impracticable. The work, however, is performed always at the same time of the day, so as to secure, as far as possible, the same atmospheric conditions in the reciprocal observations.”
Furber (1898), p. 204 · text layer
Furber, T. F. (1898). The trigonometrical survey of New South Wales, with mention of similar surveys in the other Australian colonies. Report of the Australasian Association for the Advancement of Science, 7, Section A, 176-237.
Refraction named as the main difficulty; Indian example of zenith distances varying through nine minutes.
“There is little doubt that the principal source of difficulty in trigonometrical levelling lies in the considerable and irregular changes in the atmospheric refraction. This cannot be better illustrated than by the experience obtained in India, where apparent zenith distances of points only 10 or 12 miles away varied under certain circumstances through a range of as much as nine minutes.”
Furber (1898), p. 205 · text layer
Furber, T. F. (1898). The trigonometrical survey of New South Wales, with mention of similar surveys in the other Australian colonies. Report of the Australasian Association for the Advancement of Science, 7, Section A, 176-237.
Coefficient varies with line length; grouped means.
“Under 10 miles. 10 to 15 miles. 15 to 20 miles. Over 20 miles. K 096 '113 126 135”
Furber (1898), p. 206 · text layer
Furber, T. F. (1898). The trigonometrical survey of New South Wales, with mention of similar surveys in the other Australian colonies. Report of the Australasian Association for the Advancement of Science, 7, Section A, 176-237.
Coefficient refers to the usual observing time, 2 to 4 p.m., at one extreme of the daily range.
“It should be observed too, that the co-efficient determined above results, not the mean refraction, but the refraction at the usual time of observation, 2 to 4 p.m., when though less liable to fluctuation, it is really at one extreme of its daily range.”
Furber (1898), p. 208 · text layer
Furber, T. F. (1898). The trigonometrical survey of New South Wales, with mention of similar surveys in the other Australian colonies. Report of the Australasian Association for the Advancement of Science, 7, Section A, 176-237.
Struve's pressure factor not applied (area nearly at one level).
“Struves' pressure factor ... by which all co-efficients may be referred to sea level, has not been applied, the area covered being nearly enough at the one level to enable it to be disregarded without affecting the conclusions.”
Furber (1898), p. 208 · text layer
Furber, T. F. (1898). The trigonometrical survey of New South Wales, with mention of similar surveys in the other Australian colonies. Report of the Australasian Association for the Advancement of Science, 7, Section A, 176-237.
Coefficient is found only on the longer reciprocal lines but is needed on shorter one-way lines.
“the co-efficient is determinable only by the reciprocal observations which are usually made only on the longer lines of the survey, and that it is, as a rule, needed for application on the shorter lines, which are those fixing stations from which no observations have been made.”
Furber (1898), p. 208 · text layer
Furber, T. F. (1898). The trigonometrical survey of New South Wales, with mention of similar surveys in the other Australian colonies. Report of the Australasian Association for the Advancement of Science, 7, Section A, 176-237.

Observing rules

Vertical angles in pairs of rounds with level along the line.
“The actual field work consists in the measurement, with a theodolite having a 10-inch circle, of the elevations of all the stations in the surround, noting, at the time of sighting to each, the reading of a level the axis of which is in the direction of the line being sighted, any variations of the level being subsequently applied to the circle reading. The instrument is then reversed and a similar round taken, each such pair of rounds constituting a set from which the differences of height may be derived.”
Furber (1898), p. 204 · text layer
Furber, T. F. (1898). The trigonometrical survey of New South Wales, with mention of similar surveys in the other Australian colonies. Report of the Australasian Association for the Advancement of Science, 7, Section A, 176-237.
Horizontal angles: twenty double sets on different parts of the circle, telescope reversed after each round.
“the method has been to reverse the telescope after each round and observe in reverse order of azimuth back to referring mark, twenty such double sets being altogether observed on different parts of the circle.”
Furber (1898), p. 182 · text layer
Furber, T. F. (1898). The trigonometrical survey of New South Wales, with mention of similar surveys in the other Australian colonies. Report of the Australasian Association for the Advancement of Science, 7, Section A, 176-237.
Signals: pine pile on the cairn carrying metal vanes.
“erected a pile, usually of 4 in. x 4 in. pine, carrying metal vanes of 3 feet or more radius.”
checked on the page image of p. 181
Furber (1898), p. 181
Furber, T. F. (1898). The trigonometrical survey of New South Wales, with mention of similar surveys in the other Australian colonies. Report of the Australasian Association for the Advancement of Science, 7, Section A, 176-237.

Recommended practice

More sets of zenith distances recommended, following Bessel's weighting by number of days at each end.
“the great range among the observations forms, itself, the reason for extending them to cover a longer period of time. Bessel's opinion on this subject may be gathered from his estimate of the relative weights to be assigned to such observations, the principal factor in the weight being n, n,, where n, and n, are the number of days on which observations are made at opposite ends of the same line.”
Furber (1898), p. 205 · text layer
Furber, T. F. (1898). The trigonometrical survey of New South Wales, with mention of similar surveys in the other Australian colonies. Report of the Australasian Association for the Advancement of Science, 7, Section A, 176-237.
Average coefficient practice needs modification; distance (height of ray) should enter.
“It would seem then to be established that the practice of using an average value derived from the formula ... will need modification.”
Furber (1898), p. 208 · text layer
Furber, T. F. (1898). The trigonometrical survey of New South Wales, with mention of similar surveys in the other Australian colonies. Report of the Australasian Association for the Advancement of Science, 7, Section A, 176-237.
Photographic topographic data wanted for study of vertical and lateral refraction.
“leads to the hope that it may be usefully applied to obtaining such topographical information as is needed for a proper discussion of terrestrial refraction, both vertical and lateral.”
Furber (1898), p. 208 · text layer
Furber, T. F. (1898). The trigonometrical survey of New South Wales, with mention of similar surveys in the other Australian colonies. Report of the Australasian Association for the Advancement of Science, 7, Section A, 176-237.

Triangle closures and excess

Height closures: planned adjustment minimising the sum of squares of the refraction coefficients.
“The approximate adjustment of errors of close in the altitudes by a system of means, which on taking up the work it was found had been followed, will be shortly replaced by reduction on the principle of making the sums of the squares of the co-efficients of refraction to be a minimum, recognising the refraction as the main disturbing factor.”
Furber (1898), p. 206 · text layer
Furber, T. F. (1898). The trigonometrical survey of New South Wales, with mention of similar surveys in the other Australian colonies. Report of the Australasian Association for the Advancement of Science, 7, Section A, 176-237.
